5. RE-WEIGHT MOTOR: NINGUNO · TRANSMISIÓN: VENDIDA. Right now it is the fifth clause of a five-clause inscription, buried behind provenance. It is the sentence that makes the object a memorial rather than a souvenir. Set it alone, largest on the face, and let the dates be small.

6. CUT THE F92 NOTE, THE RULE-34 DEFENCE AND THE TRADEMARK PARAGRAPH OUT OF THE CONCEPT AND PUT THEM IN A FOOTNOTE WHERE THEY BELONG. They are internal exculpation, they are the loudest thing in the write-up, and their presence is what tells a reader this concept expects to be attacked and has already arranged its defence. A concept arguing its own admissibility has stopped arguing for itself.

If only one of these is done, do (1). The reverse is the whole difference between a medal a restaurant issues about itself and an object that admits half the vehicle has never been seen.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

### MANDIL 515 — audit mean 1.88/4

**FEASIBILITY — 2.5/4 — MAKEABLE OBJECT, UNAVAILABLE PROOF. The garment is the cheapest thing on the bench in machine terms — I verified every figure it leans on and they are all live in the source (`t1_shell.py`: `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550` and `BAY_W = 0.5155`; `t1_mats.py`: `RED = (0.5520, 0.0294, 0.0176) # = sRGB(196,49,36)` and `CREAM ... sRGB(206,208,200)`; `M["brass"] = tarnished("brass", (0.6600, 0.4750, 0.1750), 0.255, 0.34)`; `t1_detail.py`: `CNT_ZT, CNT_ZB = 1.2540, 1.1470 # 107 mm thick`, `CNT_Y_IN, CNT_Y_OUT = 0.8450, 1.1660 # 321 mm plan depth`, `CNT_NOSE_F = 0.1860`). Not one figure is invented, and the restraint about the tie height is real: `CNT_ZT` is un-dropped and no AG counter height has been watched print. So the concept needs ZERO renders, zero line pass, zero glTF export, and does not touch the clipped front camera — it routes around every machine blocker the program has. That is a genuine feasibility asset and nothing else on the shortlist matches it. But it fails on three counts that are about the world, not the machine, and the first is close to disqualifying as written.**

* ATTACKS: 1. TOP — THE FIRST STEP ASKS FOR THE ONE THING THIS PROJECT HAS NEVER ONCE OBTAINED, AND DOES NOT DECLARE IT. "photograph a cook wearing it standing at the counter beside the actual hatch" requires physical access to the bus, a cook, a restaurant's consent and a photographer. `CLAUDE.md` states flatly: "He has never stood in the vehicle: do not ask what the real one looks like, ask what a PHOTOGRAPH shows." `HANDOFF_CARRIERS.md` §0.1: "THEY CANNOT BE RE-SHOT." `PHOTOS_WANTED_rev49.md` items 1-5: "He has said 1-5 are not possible now. DO NOT RE-ASK THEM." And the decisive precedent — `PHOTOS_WANTED_rev52.md` item 7 asks for ONE HUBCAP, SQUARE ON AND CLOSE. It has sat unfulfilled for 24 revisions. If this program cannot get one static photograph of a wheel cover, it cannot get a staged photograph of a working cook at a service counter. The concept lists four STATED RISKs and none of them is "I cannot take the photograph that IS the concept." That is a new PHOTOS_WANTED item wearing a first-step costume.

2. TOP — THE COTTON CANNOT HOLD THE MEASUREMENT, AND THE WHOLE IDEA IS THE MEASUREMENT. The claim is identity: same width, same height, same corner radius as the hatch. Cotton drill shrinks 2-5% raw, 1-3% sanforized, and a kitchen apron is washed hot daily. 3% of 515.5 mm is 15.5 mm. A seamed-and-faced inset panel varies ±3-8 mm operator to operator before any wash. The machine fought to 516/515/516 — a 1 mm spread — and the substrate destroys that spread on the first laundry cycle. The concept quotes 0.1 mm (515.5, 403.0) on a carrier that cannot hold 5 mm, and never mentions shrinkage, pre-shrink, or a tolerance. That is rule 12 failing inside the object itself.

3. THE COST AND TIMELINE ARE OFF BY 1-2 ORDERS. "a single sample ... is a day's work" and "the price of a metre of red cotton" are wrong units. A bib apron with a seamed inset panel, bound facing and 55 mm radiused corners is not a stock pattern: pattern draft $150-400, sample sew $80-250, 1-3 fittings, 1-3 weeks. Holding the panel across a size run means GRADING a pattern where one component must not grade — more pattern hours, and every shop will get it wrong once. Production: most US/MX cut-and-sew shops quote 50-100 pcs MINIMUM PER SIZE; "fifty is an ordinary small order" is true for one size and becomes 150-200 units across a run. Honest all-in: $500-1,200 development, $1,500-5,000 for 50-100 units landed, 4-10 weeks. Cheap for merch, but it is weeks and four figures for an owner whose live words are "we need to finally present something."

4. NOTHING REV 76 CAN SHIP THIS WEEK IS THE OBJECT. The presentable artifact from the machine is a tech pack and a flat spec drawing — which lands in the register THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE already occupies. The garment itself arrives after third parties, and the on-body image needs either the impossible photograph (attack 1) or a figure illustration nobody in this program has committed to producing. So against "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry", this concept ships a PDF and an invoice.

5. ONE SMALL MISSING CAPABILITY, HONESTLY CHEAP. Nothing in this repo emits a true-1:1 physical cutting template. It is ~20 lines writing an SVG/PDF at exact scale with a printed 100 mm calibration bar. 1-2 hours, no Blender, no render. Not a blocker — but it does not exist and nobody has built it, and without it every downstream party re-keys the numbers by hand, which is where the 515.5 will get rounded to 515 or 520.

6. WHAT SURVIVES INTACT, AND I WANT IT ON THE RECORD: zero ink means zero separations, zero registration, zero press proofing and zero trademark surface. Set against PAPEL DE GRASA (back-to-front register on a translucent substrate — a genuinely hard press job) or LA LOTERIA (24 plates), this is the lowest-risk manufacture on the bench. No factory minimum of 5000. No photographer needed for the OBJECT, only for the PROOF. And the model dependency is four constants, which means the concept cannot be blocked by a render queue, a coin-flip verifier row, or the emblem.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. REPLACE THE FIRST STEP WITH ONE THAT NEEDS NO BUS, NO COOK AND NO RESTAURANT — the free version exists and the concept skipped past it. This week, on a table: print the bay elevation at true 1:1 from `BAY_W = 0.5155`, `Z_HEAD - Z_SILL = 0.4030`, `BAY_R = 0.0550` with a 100 mm calibration bar in the margin; cut the same rectangle from red cloth; pin it to ANY apron on ANY person; photograph the person standing beside the printed elevation so both rectangles are in one frame at the same scale. Total cost about $10 and one afternoon, and it answers the only question that matters — does a 515 × 403 rectangle read as a hatch on a human chest, or as a bib pocket. Keep the cook-at-the-counter photograph as the ASPIRATION, and file it explicitly as PHOTOS_WANTED item 8 with the note that items 1-5 and 7 are all still unfulfilled, so this one is not to be budgeted on.

2. PUT THE TOLERANCE ON THE OBJECT AND MAKE THE SUBSTRATE HOLD IT. Spec sanforized or garment-washed drill; cut the pattern shrink-compensated so the FINISHED washed panel reads 515.5 × 403.0; state a build tolerance (±3 mm is achievable, ±0.5 mm is not) and put it ON THE WOVEN LABEL beside the figure. The label already declares the tie height as NOT MEASURED — extend the same honesty one line: "515.5 × 403.0 mm ± 3 mm as sewn; the machine reads 516 / 515 / 516." That turns the strongest attack into the label's best line and costs nothing.

3. RE-COST IT IN THE CONCEPT, NOT IN THE OWNER'S HEAD. Two numbers, stated: development (pattern + grade + sample) $500-1,200 and 2-4 weeks; production 50-100 units $1,500-5,000 landed and 4-8 weeks further. Add the sentence the concept is missing: "no cut-and-sew shop will hold the panel constant across a graded run unless it is called out as a NON-GRADING component on the tech pack" — one line on the spec sheet that saves one whole wrong sample.

4. BUILD THE 1:1 TEMPLATE EMITTER AS THE REVISION'S ACTUAL SHIP. `apron_template.py`: read `BAY_W`, `Z_SILL`, `Z_HEAD`, `BAY_R` from `t1_shell` directly — never retype them — and write a tiled PDF at true size with a calibration bar, seam allowance offset, and the RGB/CMYK for `RED` and `CREAM` pulled from `t1_mats`. Guard it the way this project guards everything: a row asserting the emitted path's bounding box equals `BAY_W × (Z_HEAD - Z_SILL)` to 0.1 mm, and watch it FAIL by feeding it a wrong radius. That is buildable in an afternoon with no Blender and no render, it makes the geometry chain unbreakable from mesh to fabric, and it is a real machine artifact for a revision under rule 55.

5. ONE SENTENCE TO THE OWNER BEFORE ANY ORDER, as multiple choice with the pinned-rectangle photograph attached: does the rectangle read as the hatch, or as a bib pocket? He is the only one who can answer it and the answer costs $10 to obtain. Do not order fifty of anything until it comes back.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1.5/4 — NOT FRESH. The core move is already banked twice inside this pool and once on the shortlist, and the object itself is the single most saturated item in restaurant merchandise. The one genuinely original thing here is a photograph, not a garment — and the concept's own FIRST STEP admits it. It also fails the second charge: the flat-measured-line aesthetic was never the line, it was the CONFESSION, and this concept relocates the confession to a woven label without changing it. Cut the label out and you are holding a cream apron with a red bib panel, an object that exists in a hundred restaurants today.**

* ATTACKS: 1. INTERNAL PRIOR ART — THE POOL'S OWN AUDIT ALREADY CONVICTS IT, IN WRITING. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md` line 179: "Two aprons (garment-3 MANDIL 812, free-3-3 MANDIL 515) are the same move on the same rectangle." The same line lists TWELVE concepts using 515.5 x 403.0 r55 at 1:1 — photographic-2, environmental-1, free-4-2, f18-sticker-2, free-6-2, free-8-2, garment-3, free-3-3, packaging-1, people-2 ("The frame IS the hole"), polychrome-2 ("sheet cut to 515.5 x 403.0 r 55"), frontier-inside-neck-1. And TRES HUECOS is already on the NEXT EIGHT: "the three apertures as the mark, vehicle deleted." MANDIL 515 is TRES HUECOS minus two holes, sewn. The brief says a concept duplicating a core move is screened out; this duplicates the pool's most-used rectangle in the pool's most-used register.

2. AND environmental-1 BEATS IT AT ITS OWN IDEA. That concept CUTS the hole — a working 1:1 hatch, fibre-laser cut from the model's own outline, "then the same rounded rectangle becomes every opening in the room." MANDIL 515 takes a VOID and renders it as a FILLED BLOCK of red cloth. It converts an aperture into a solid. That is not a translation of the finding, it is its inversion, and the object next to it in the same pool does it correctly.

3. EXTERNAL PRIOR ART — EVERY SPECIFIED ELEMENT IS THE CATEGORY DEFAULT. Cream cotton drill + cross-back straps + contrast panel + brass hardware + woven inside-neck label is not a design, it is the Hedley & Bennett (LA, 2012) and Tilit (NYC, 2012) house spec, verbatim; both companies were built on precisely this garment and both run restaurant collaborations as their main business. The restaurant-branded apron is the most-done non-tee object on the hospitality ladder — the concept says so itself in risk 1 and then proposes it anyway. Nothing in the material spec distinguishes this from stock.

4. THE MEANING-CARRIER IS THE MOST DERIVATIVE PART OF THE OBJECT. A blank woven label carrying a number as provenance is Margiela's numbered label; a spec sheet sewn inside a workwear garment is Japanese repro (Real McCoy's, Warehouse) and Engineered Garments; "confession printed on the object" is Muji-plus-Everlane. The bench's own finding at line 69 already names Margiela as garment-2's lineage and "Muji-plus-Everlane for the confession-on-the-object house move." So the ONLY place this concept's idea lives is the one element with three named precedents.

5. THE CULL CRITERION THAT KILLED ITS TWIN APPLIES UNCHANGED. Bench line 128 culled MANDIL 812 with: "the meaning lives in a woven label, a colophon or a 6 pt caption that can be cut out, and the moment it is, the object is a coat with odd pockets... An object whose idea is detachable is not a designed object." MANDIL 515 has the identical property. It escaped the cull on score, not on merit.

6. THE VISUAL RESULT COLLIDES WITH THE MOST FAMOUS VW GRAPHIC IN EXISTENCE. A cream field with a red block is the two-tone panel-van livery. On VW-bus merchandise that rectangle is already spoken for: the audience most likely to look twice will read "bus paint," not "serving hatch." It also collides internally with the pool's two-tone overshirt and LADO E, which are working that same break deliberately.

7. THE ORIGINALITY IS IN THE CAPTION AND THE CONCEPT CONCEDES IT THREE TIMES. Risk 1: invisible until a person is told. Risk 3: does not read as Tacombi at all. FIRST STEP: the deliverable is a photograph of two rectangles 300 mm apart. The fresh artefact here is that photograph — a good image, but a one-shot gag that works once, in one frame, only where the physical hatch stands. The garment carries none of it away from the counter.

8. A GEOMETRY PROBLEM THAT SHOULD EMBARRASS THIS PROJECT IN PARTICULAR. 515.5 mm is not a bib. A cross-back chef bib is on the order of 250-300 mm wide; an adult chest is on the order of 950-1050 mm around, so 515.5 mm is over half the circumference — armhole to armhole — and 403 mm tall runs collarbone to below the navel. CEILING ON MY OWN CLAIM: I have measured no apron and no cook, and I publish no figure; this is a tape-measure check, not a measurement. But the concept's risk 2 states the problem as "on a small wearer the panel will read oversized," and the real objection is stronger and is not a size-run issue: at 1:1 the rectangle may be wider than the bib on EVERY wearer, in which case the garment is not a cross-back bib apron at all. And on a curved torso a flat 515.5 mm panel photographs foreshortened — the 1:1 claim survives on the pattern table and dies in the concept's own first-step photograph. A programme that retired a 20 mm asymmetry at 0.67 sigma cannot ship a garment whose central dimension has never been checked against a body.

9. THE "OLD AESTHETIC UNDER A NEW LABEL" CHARGE — PARTLY ACQUITTED, MOSTLY GUILTY. Acquitted on ink: zero printed inks, no drawn line, genuinely not a print carrier. Guilty on substance: the programme's signature was never the line, it was dimension-plus-provenance-grade-plus-NOT-MEASURED-disclosure. This object reproduces that signature exactly and merely weaves it. THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE in a neck label.

10. THE DANGER IS GONE AND IS PRICED AS A FEATURE. "Zero ink is also zero legal surface." No wordmark, no emblem, no trademark exposure, no press, nothing that could offend anyone, "an ordinary small order." Its own four stated risks are four ways of saying nobody will notice. This is the safest object on the bench.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. CUT THE HOLE. The source is a void; stop facing it as a solid. Make the 515.5 x 403.0 r55 rectangle an actual aperture in the garment, bound in red, worn over a red under-layer so the red appears THROUGH it — or put the hole where a hand already goes: a chest-height welt whose MOUTH is the r55 rounded rectangle, so a hand entering the garment traces the path a plate takes through the hatch. Then the idea is in the construction, survives the label being cut out, and reads at three metres. Stated cost: a real hole in a cook's garment has a food-safety and warmth penalty; if it must close, close it with a flap that OPENS — a hatch is a thing that opens.

2. IF THE PANEL STAYS SOLID, MAKE THE RECTANGLE THE GARMENT, NOT A PATCH. 515.5 x 403.0 is not a bib but it is very nearly a standard bistro/waist apron body (~20 x 16 in). Cut the half-apron AS the aperture: one panel, no applied block, nothing to scale, no size-run fight, and the dimension becomes structural instead of decorative. Cheapest fix on the list and it dissolves risk 2 entirely.

3. SHIP THE FINDING, NOT THE SHAPE — THIS IS THE ONE ROUTE NOBODY HAS TAKEN. The machine's result is not "a rectangle," it is 516 / 515 / 516: THREE, EQUAL, at 1 mm, after a taper was retired twice and a pillar asymmetry forbidden at 0.67 sigma. Make it a SET OF THREE garments, one per bay, indistinguishable to the eye and differing by 1 mm, worn by the three people on the pass. No apron company and no repro brand has made a size run that varies by a millimetre for a reason. That de-duplicates from TRES HUECOS and environmental-1, because those own the SHAPE and this would own the EQUALITY.

4. KILL THE PROVENANCE-GRADE LABEL. It is the pool's own named derivation (Margiela + Muji/Everlane) and the programme's tic in miniature. If a label survives, it carries nine characters — 516 515 516 — and no explanation. The moment it says "NOT MEASURED — maker's choice" it is a woven Uncertainty Plate.

5. BREAK THE VW TWO-TONE READ. Drop the red fill. Let the rectangle exist as construction rather than colour: a raw-edge seam, a topstitched box, a change of grain or weave in one cloth, or the aperture's EDGE condition (the cut, the seal, the 5.5 mm shut line). A rectangle visible only when light rakes across it is far more Tacombi-specific than a red block that every bus person reads as livery.

6. TWO FREE CHECKS BEFORE IT IS SHOWN TO HIM. (a) Tape the bib width of the apron Tacombi already owns and the chest of a real cook, and publish both beside 515.5 — if the panel cannot lie flat, that belongs in the concept, not in the sample review. (b) Put the existing Tacombi apron next to the Hedley & Bennett and Tilit catalogues; if the object as specified is already for sale, this is a colourway and should be withdrawn or re-registered.

7. IF THE PHOTOGRAPH IS THE DELIVERABLE — AND THE FIRST STEP SAYS IT IS — REGISTER IT AS ONE. "Two identical rectangles, 300 mm apart, one steel, one cloth, one person between them." Commission it, shoot it, let the apron be the prop it actually is. That is a more original object than the fiftieth restaurant apron, it is the first photograph of a person this programme has produced, and it costs a metre of red cotton either way.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

**OWNER-AND-BRAND — 2/4 — MANDIL 515 is the safest concept in the pool on trademark and the most misaligned on owner intent. It cannot damage the brand legally — zero ink, no roundel, no script, no folk art, nothing SPEC 10.10 governs (rule 34: 10.10's object is the vehicle's painted elements, and this garment reproduces none of them). But safety is not fit. Four things sink it against this specific owner, and none is aesthetic.

FIRST, IT IS IN NEITHER LINE HE RULED. F331, `RULED-rev77`: "Split it deliberately — kids get one line, adults another," stated to him as *free/handed-to-you things are for children, paid/kept things are for adults*, and the register closes with **"Every future shortlist must say which line each item is in."** A cook's apron is neither. It is not handed to a child and it is not bought by an adult — it is a STAFF UNIFORM, a fifth category the ruling does not open. The concept never names its line because it has none. The first question he asks is "who is this for," and the honest answer is "the person already being paid to be there."

SECOND, IT REMOVES THE WORDMARK FROM THE BRAND'S MOST-PHOTOGRAPHED GARMENT. The bench itself records, three separate times, that the cook in `ref_side.jpg` wears **"a white cap, a white shirt and a Tacombi apron"** — `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md:1238` and :2266 both say "an apron carrying the script." That apron is walking signage: it is in the frame of every customer photograph taken across that counter, at the exact moment of the transaction this concept is about. MANDIL 515 proposes replacing a script-bearing garment with an unmarked one, and its own risk (3) concedes "to anyone not told it does not read as Tacombi at all." Net brand impressions: negative. That is dilution in the plainest operational sense — not lawsuit dilution, just fewer people seeing the name, forever, in exchange for a geometry joke that must be spoken aloud. A restaurateur who has run a taqueria for twenty years will see this in one second and it is the correct objection.

THIRD, THE PROJECT'S OWN CRITIC ALREADY CULLED THIS OBJECT, AND THE TWIN. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md:128` puts `[dream:garment-3] MANDIL 812` on "THE ART-SCHOOL SHELF, TO BE CULLED IN ONE PASS," with the diagnosis: *"the meaning lives in a woven label, a colophon or a 6 pt caption that can be cut out, and the moment it is, the object is a coat with odd pockets… An object whose idea is detachable is not a designed object."* MANDIL 515's meaning lives in **one woven inside-neck label**. Apply :131's survivor test — *if the caption is removed, is this still worth owning?* — and what remains is a cream apron with a red rectangle. Worse, :179 records that **"Two aprons (garment-3 MANDIL 812, free-3-3 MANDIL 515) are the same move on the same rectangle,"** and that 515.5 × 403.0 r55 at 1:1 is the rule in **thirteen** concepts. He asked for "fresh ones, good ones, and lots of em." Handing him the thirteenth use of one rectangle, as the second of two aprons, reads as one idea wearing thirteen hats.

FOURTH, THE FIRST STEP SHIPS NOTHING FROM US AND HANDS HIM HOMEWORK. His words are "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry" and "we need to finally present something." The first shippable step is: *the restaurant* finds an apron it already owns, *someone there* cuts red cotton, *a cook* wears it, *someone* photographs it. Every actor is his. Our contribution is a rectangle he could have been told over the phone — the concept says so itself: "Beyond that rectangle the model contributes NOTHING." No line pass, no render, no geometry, no file. And it lands in the revision where F330 fired — **"Yes — start it now"** on the die-cut sticker, the oldest live row and his original deliverable, with a locked style, a locked scene, a named audience and a written spec already sitting in `AUDIT_rev43.md` §5. Presenting an apron in the same breath as that ruling is choosing the thing he did not ask for over the thing he just unblocked.

AND ONE OPERATIONAL DEFECT THE CONCEPT NEVER MENTIONS, WHICH IS THE ONE HE WILL RAISE FIRST. **Cream drill with a body-red faced panel is a bad kitchen garment.** The photographed apron is WHITE because white is bleachable; cream is not, and in a taqueria it goes grey-and-salsa-flecked inside a week. A saturated red facing seamed to a cream ground will crock and bleed onto that cream in industrial hot wash and repeated bleaching — the panel is the exact place a cook wipes their hands. Then brass hardware on a garment washed daily near a plancha: tarnish, corrosion, and loose metal fittings at food height, which some kitchens ban outright. The concept treats the cream/red departure from the photograph as a pure design decision ("not a measurement") and never asks whether the object survives a shift. He runs kitchens. He will ask in ten seconds and there is no answer in the write-up.

WHAT SURVIVES THE ATTACK, HONESTLY. The trademark position is genuinely the best in the pool and the concept is right that zero ink is near-zero legal surface — though **"no trademark clearance"** is not the same as **"zero legal surface"**: a garment sold to the public still needs fibre content, care and country-of-origin labelling, which is exactly the label the whole idea hangs on. It is also the only concept that puts the cook back in, and its account of why — the vendor's shirt, the child's hair, the 37 % of variance that is the man — is the truest sentence in the bench. And risk (2) is a real held line: fixing the panel across the size run and refusing to scale it to the body is a decision that costs something and that every maker will fight. That is not a confession, it is a constraint, and it is the one place where the measurement changes the object rather than being printed on it.

WOULD HE BE PROUD TO HAND SOMEONE THIS? He would be comfortable handing it, not proud. It is handsome, defensible, cheap and safe. But he is the man who rejected his own emblem nine times because it did not look like his bus, and SPEC 10.10's stated bar is *"the owner recognises his own vehicle."* This object is unrecognisable as his vehicle by construction, carries none of his artwork, has no line in the audience split he just ruled, and takes his name off the garment his customers photograph. It will not embarrass him. It will not be the thing he shows people either.**

* ATTACKS: 1. AUDIENCE: falls outside both lines F331 ruled at rev 77 (children = free/handed-to-you, adults = paid/kept). A staff uniform is neither; the ruling requires every shortlist item to name its line and this one cannot.
2. BRAND SUBTRACTION: the record says three times (CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md:1238, :2266, :2030) that the photographed cook wears a Tacombi apron CARRYING THE SCRIPT. This replaces a wordmark-bearing garment — in frame at every counter transaction and in every customer photo — with an unmarked one. Its own risk (3) concedes it does not read as Tacombi. Fewer brand impressions, permanently, for a joke that must be spoken aloud.
3. ALREADY CULLED, TWICE OVER: the bench's own critic (:128) shelved the twin MANDIL 812 for precisely this failure mode — "the meaning lives in a woven label… an object whose idea is detachable is not a designed object" — and :179 records the two aprons as "the same move on the same rectangle." 515.5 x 403.0 r55 at 1:1 is now the rule in THIRTEEN concepts. Against "lots of em," this is the thirteenth hat on one idea.
4. SHIPS NOTHING FROM US: the first step is executed entirely by the restaurant (their apron, their cloth, their cook, their photograph). "Beyond that rectangle the model contributes NOTHING" — no line pass, no render, no geometry, no file. This is the exact pattern behind "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry."
5. WRONG REVISION: F330 fired at rev 77 — "Yes — start it now" on the die-cut sticker, his original deliverable, style locked, scene locked, audience named, spec already written in AUDIT_rev43.md §5. Presenting an apron beside that reads as declining the thing he just unblocked.
6. UNADDRESSED OPERATIONAL DEFECT: cream drill is not bleachable and the photographed apron is WHITE for that reason; a saturated red facing seamed to cream will crock and bleed in hot industrial wash, at the precise panel a cook wipes their hands on; brass hardware tarnishes and corrodes on a daily-washed kitchen garment and loose metal at food height is banned in some kitchens. The write-up treats cream+red as a pure design decision and never asks whether the object survives a shift. He runs kitchens.
7. OVERSTATED LEGAL CLAIM: "zero ink is also zero legal surface" is wrong in the small. No trademark clearance, agreed — but a garment sold to the public carries mandatory fibre-content, care and origin labelling, on the very label the whole idea depends on. Minor, but it is an unearned "zero" in a project whose first rule is not to publish a figure you have not watched print.
8. RECOGNITION BAR: SPEC 10.10's stated purpose is "the bar is that the owner recognises his own vehicle." Not a violation (rule 34 — 10.10's object is the model's painted elements, and this reproduces none) but a fit failure against the standard he set. Nine emblem rejections are evidence he wants the recognisable thing right, not an abstraction of it.
9. WHAT SURVIVES: best trademark position in the pool; the only concept that puts the cook back in, and its reasons (the vendor's shirt, the child's hair, the 37 % variance) are the truest lines in the bench; and risk (2) — the panel fixed across the size run, never graded to the body — is a real held constraint that changes the object rather than being printed on it.
* WOULD FIX IT: FIVE CONCRETE CHANGES, IN ORDER OF WHAT THEY BUY.

1. PUT THE SCRIPT BACK ON, AND MAKE THE HOLE FRAME IT. The single change that flips this from brand-subtraction to brand-addition: the red panel stops being a blank rectangle and becomes an APERTURE — the Señor Tacombi script sits inside it, in cream, framed by the 515.5 x 403.0 r55 opening. Now the geometry is the frame and the name is the payload; the cook is still wearing the hole, but the hole has the brand in it the way the real hole has the cook in it. It reads as Tacombi from three metres, it survives staff turnover, it survives the label being cut out — and it advances nothing on F191, because the flank script is not the roundel and needs no emblem work. This also passes the bench's own survivor test at :131.

2. NAME THE LINE, AND CHANGE THE OBJECT TO MATCH. F331 requires it. The honest answer is neither, so pick and rebuild: (a) STAFF — then it is a uniform, priced and specified as one, and the deliverable is a spec sheet plus a wash-tested sample, not a merch concept; or (b) ADULT PAID LINE — then it is a retail apron, and it needs a hangtag, a price, a reason a stranger buys it, and it must carry the mark (see 1). Do not ship it as both.

3. SOLVE THE KITCHEN BEFORE THE CONCEPT. Change the ground to a bleachable white or a dark that hides salsa, or keep cream and state the wash regime honestly. Specify the red as a piece-dyed, wash-tested fabric with a stated crocking grade, not a shader albedo — and say plainly that cream (206,208,200) is an albedo, not a dye recipe, and the round-trip is untested (the twin concept at :2266 states exactly this and this one does not). Replace brass hardware with the same brass COLOUR in a non-corroding fitting, or drop hardware entirely and use ties. One wash test on one sample settles all of it and it is a week.

4. MAKE THE FIRST STEP OURS, NOT HIS. The current step is homework for the restaurant. Replace it with something we deliver in this revision: render the side elevation through the line pass, put a 1:1 flat pattern of the bib and the panel on one sheet beside the hatch it came from, both rectangles at the same scale, dimensions on both. That is a file, it is a presentable thing, it uses the model's line pass (which now exists at HEAD), and he can hand it to any maker on any continent without cutting cloth himself. The photograph of the cook then becomes the SECOND step, and it is his to enjoy rather than his to arrange.

5. FOLD THE TWO APRONS INTO ONE AND STOP AT ONE APERTURE OBJECT. The bench's own remedy at :180 is "Cap the aperture family at two: one 1:1 fabricated object and one garment." Merge MANDIL 812 and MANDIL 515 into a single garment entry, kill the other, and let the sticker (F330, fired) take the slot this was competing for. If after all of that the apron still cannot say which line it is in or why a stranger would buy it, it is a good staff uniform and should be presented as exactly that — one page, no manifesto — rather than as a concept.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**DANGER — 1.5/4 — THE DANGER HAS BEEN SANDED, AND THE CONCEPT'S OWN STATED RISK 1 IS THE CONFESSION: "from three metres this is a cream apron with a red panel." The idea — a service worker wearing the aperture they are handed food through, on a project that has three times subtracted a living man as measurement noise — is the most genuinely uncomfortable material anywhere on this bench. The object built from it is a cream cotton-drill cross-back with a red colour-block bib, brass hardware and a dimensional woven label: the single most category-conformant garment in restaurant merchandise, assembled entirely from the visual grammar (drill, cross-back, brass, woven label, no print) that exists to make things look considered and feel safe. The killing detail is that THERE IS NO HOLE. The panel is FACED, not cut. A hatch is an absence; this is a presence. "The cook wears the hole they hand the food through" is refuted by the specification one line under it, and the whole concept's claim to danger rests on a sentence its own artefact does not perform. Every uncomfortable thing in the write-up — the erasure of the man, the equation of worker and orifice, the departure from the white apron the real staff wear — lives in the pitch document and in a label sewn inside the neck where nobody stands. The bench already grades this class correctly: "the idea lives in a label you can cut off."**

* ATTACKS: WHAT IS ACTUALLY DANGEROUS HERE, NAMED FIRST SO THE LOSS IS MEASURABLE.

(A) THE WORKER AS APERTURE. The real proposition is that a human being is a hole in a machine — the transposition of a service opening onto a service worker's torso. That is either tender or brutal, and it is dangerous in both directions. A brand putting "you are the hatch" on the chest of the person earning $17/hr behind the counter is a statement about labour, and it is the most political object in this whole programme.
(B) THE ERASURE, WHICH IS REAL AND WHICH I VERIFIED. All three citations check out live: SURVEY_rev49_photoreal.md ln 965 — "one of my four candidate findings died precisely because a pale band turned out to be the vendor's shirt"; LEDGER_rev67.md ln 63 — "three of its 106 points sit on a child's hair"; SPEC.md ln 1167 / SURVEY_rev49 ln 874 — "about 37 % of bay 1's photographed variance is the man standing in it." Seventy-six revisions of a project whose method was, repeatedly and literally, to identify the human in the frame and subtract him as contamination. That is mournful, it is machine-provenanced, and it is the best raw danger in the pool.
(C) THE COSTUME PROBLEM. A designed cut-and-sew garment referencing the working clothes of people who currently wear plain white, sold or issued in a market where "workwear" is bought by people who do not work.

NOW WHAT THE CONCEPT DID TO ALL THREE.

1. IT DID NOT CUT THE HOLE. "The chest panel is a separately cut and seamed rectangle ... faced in body red. ZERO printed inks." Faced. Seamed. Closed. The concept's central verb is "wears the hole" and its specification delivers a red rectangle. A colour-blocked bib is the safest decision available in garment design; it is what every heritage apron brand has done since 2012. The idea's entire risk was in the void and the void was specified away. Note the concept never once uses the word "opening", "cut", "void" or "aperture" of the garment itself — it only uses them of the vehicle. It knows.

2. IT TREATED INVISIBILITY AS A COMMS PROBLEM, NOT AS A DIAGNOSIS. STATED RISK 1 says the idea "is INVISIBLE until a person is told, once, across a counter ... if the staff do not tell the story it is decoration." Correct, and it is the verdict, not a caveat. The author noticed the object does not carry the idea and responded by proposing a staff script. A design that requires a verbal footnote to become dangerous is a design whose danger has been removed and replaced with a claim that it exists elsewhere.

3. THE LABEL CARRIES THE RECTANGLE INSTEAD OF THE MAN. "One woven label inside the neck carrying the rectangle's dimensions and its provenance grade." The concept's own most powerful paragraph — the vendor's shirt, the child's hair, the 37 % — appears NOWHERE ON THE OBJECT. Not on the label, not on the facing, not anywhere. It is rationale. Strip the pitch and this garment says "515.5 × 403.0 mm, MEASURED." A dimension is the most comfortable thing a person can wear; it is the aesthetic of precision, which is exactly the aesthetic this programme retreats into when it does not want to say anything.

4. THE BRASS IS THE SANDING AGENT AND IT IS DEFENDED WITH A PROVENANCE. "Brass hardware at the strap crossings, taking its colour from the counter's own measured nosing." Brass on a cross-back drill apron is the single most-done detail in restaurant merch of the last fifteen years — the concept says so itself about aprons generally and then specifies it anyway. Deriving the alloy colour from `t1_mats` does not make it less of a heritage-workwear signal; it makes it a heritage-workwear signal with a footnote. Every material decision in this brief (cream drill, cross-back, brass, woven neck label, no print) is the standard kit of the reassuring artisanal object. The concept dressed a statement about being reduced to an orifice in the uniform of tasteful craft.

5. RISK 2 IS THE DANGER WEARING ITS OWN WARNING LABEL. "On a small wearer the panel will read oversized and slightly wrong." Say what it actually is: 515.5 mm wide is wider than the bib of any apron ever cut and wider than most human chests; 403.0 mm tall runs from collarbone to below the navel. On a small wearer the rectangle does not "read oversized" — the hole is bigger than the person, and it must run past the side seams. THAT IS THE CONCEPT. It is the sentence the whole thing was built to produce, and it has been demoted to a manufacturing caveat about makers fighting the spec. And unexamined: on a female-bodied wearer this is a hard-edged red rectangle drawn around the breasts at 1:1 with a 55 mm radius. Whether that is a violation or the point, an unexamined framing device pointed at a worker's chest is not a risk you get to leave in the "makers will fight it" paragraph.

6. RISK 4 REVERSES THE INTERESTING DECISION. "The white apron in the photograph is what Playa actually wore. This deliberately departs from the photographed garment." So the record contains the actual working garment of the actual workers — white, plain — and the concept replaces it with the vehicle's red. That is the brand painting over the worker with the truck. Which is a genuinely savage idea IF STATED; here it is filed as a colour-provenance disclaimer.

7. UNIFORM OR RETAIL IS NEVER DECIDED, AND IT IS THE ENTIRE ETHICS OF THE OBJECT. Read as uniform: staff are issued a garment that says they are a hatch, made from a programme that subtracted them, and they were not asked. Read as retail: a customer buys the cook's clothing with the cook's hole on it and stands on the other side of the counter in it. Both are dangerous. The concept commits to neither and so is neither — the FIRST STEP even proposes photographing a cook wearing a pinned mock-up without one word about asking him, in a critique that opens by mourning how this project has treated the men in its frames.

8. THE TIE-HEIGHT REFUSAL IS HONEST AND IT IS ALSO WHERE THE BODY WAS DROPPED. The MODEL section's refusal to invent a figure is genuinely the best methodological writing in the concept. But look at what got kept and what got dropped: the RECTANGLE (verified, safe, already used in thirteen other concepts on this bench) is kept; the ONE dimension that relates the wearer's body to the counter — where the vehicle meets a standing person — is handed to MAKER'S CHOICE. Rule 12 permits a bracket. Abandoning the body relation and keeping the graphic is the sanding pattern in miniature.

9. IT IS NOT EVEN A SINGULAR RISK. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md` ln 179: "Two aprons (garment-3 MANDIL 812, free-3-3 MANDIL 515) are the same move on the same rectangle," inside a set of THIRTEEN concepts that are 515.5 × 403.0 r55 re-substrated. And ln 189 lists free-3-3 among the concepts "FLAGGED TRUE [contains food or people] WITH NEITHER IN THE OBJECT." A rectangle deployed thirteen times is a house style. Nobody took a risk that twelve other concepts also took.

WHAT SURVIVES: one sentence — "the cook wears the hole" — and three verified facts about a man who was measured around. That is real, and it is why this is not scored at zero. But every one of them is in the prose and none is in the garment.
* WOULD FIX IT: CUT THE HOLE. This is the whole fix and everything else follows from it. The chest panel is not faced, it is OPEN: a real 515.5 × 403.0 mm aperture with a 55 mm radius, bound in body red, through which you see the wearer's own shirt, their body, their sweat and whatever the shift has put on it. An apron with a hole in it is a defective apron — a protective garment that has had its protection removed at exactly the place protection matters — and that is the object. It makes the title literal, it makes the idea visible at thirty metres with nobody telling any story, and it turns the FIRST STEP photograph into one image that cannot be argued with: the cook at the counter, the hatch behind, the counter visible THROUGH the cook. Practical fallback only if a full void is unwearable: bind the opening and set a cream inner panel 25 mm behind it so it reads as depth and reveal — never as a colour block. If the hole cannot be cut, kill the concept; there is nothing else in it.

THEN, in order:

1. THE LABEL CARRIES THE MAN, NOT THE RECTANGLE. Replace the dimensions with the erasure, provenanced verbatim, inside the neck where only the wearer reads it: "BAY 1. ABOUT 37 % OF THE PHOTOGRAPHED VARIANCE IN THIS HOLE WAS A MAN STANDING IN IT — SPEC.md §10. HE IS NOT IN THE MODEL. A PALE BAND IN THE RECORD WAS THE VENDOR'S SHIRT. A MEASUREMENT WINDOW LANDED ON A CHILD'S HAIR. ALL THREE WERE SUBTRACTED." Put the 515.5 × 403.0 on the outside of the bound edge if it must exist at all. The wearer should be the only person who reads that the record erased people like him.

2. MAKE IT WHITE AND PUT THE RED ONLY IN THE BOUND EDGE OF THE HOLE. The real staff wear white; the departure to red is the one move that makes this a brand garment rather than a working one. White drill, red binding on the aperture only — now the vehicle shows through the worker instead of being painted over him, RISK 4 dissolves into content, and the object stops looking like the merch category it currently belongs to.

3. DELETE THE BRASS, or specify it in the counter's actual condition — worn through on the leading edge, unlacquered, going green — never new heritage brass. If the hardware is the reassuring part, it is working against the object.

4. PROMOTE RISK 2 TO THE PITCH. Fixed rectangle across the whole size run: state in millimetres what it does at a 34" chest and a 48" chest, sample both, photograph both together. If the panel runs past the side seam on the smallest size, THAT is the hero image — the hole is bigger than the person — and it goes on the front of the deck, not in the caveats.

5. DECIDE UNIFORM OR RETAIL IN WRITING AND PUT THE ANSWER ON THE OBJECT. If uniform: a written consent step — the staff are shown the label text and may refuse the garment, and that step is in the spec sheet, not assumed. If retail: say out loud that a customer is buying the hole they are served through and price/word it as the confrontation it is. Either way, ask the cook in the FIRST STEP photograph, by name, and record that you did.

6. TIE HEIGHT: print the BRACKET, do not abandon it. "COUNTER TOP ABOVE GROUND: NOT A SINGLE FIGURE. It varies along the vehicle at 17.75 mm/m. Band drawn to scale." Keeps the body in the object under rule 12 instead of handing the only body-relating dimension to the maker.

7. RESOLVE AGAINST MANDIL 812 AND THE OTHER TWELVE RECTANGLES BEFORE PRESENTING. One apron ships or neither does; and if the aperture rectangle is appearing on thirteen objects, it is a house rule, and this concept must justify itself as the one place the rectangle is a HOLE rather than a shape.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

### EL FIERRO — audit mean 1.75/4

**FEASIBILITY — 2/4 — BUILDABLE OBJECT, UN-CLOSEABLE LOOP, AND ITS ACCURACY PREMISE DIES AT ITS OWN SCALE. The iron itself is one of the few things in this pool a person with $500 can actually buy — the vendors exist, the geometry is millable, the first step is genuinely $50. But nothing about it can be evaluated on this machine, in this revision, or by this team: the decision needs a laser shop, a shipping label, a working kitchen, a service day and the owner physically holding it — six weeks before any evidence, and the AI contributes nothing after the DXF. Worse, the reason to do it *in this project* — "only the mesh gives you the true silhouette and the r55 aperture corners" — is destroyed by the process it is printed with. At 45.2:1 reduction the corner radius is 1.22 mm and char bloom on paper is ±0.3–0.7 mm, so the model-only features are the exact features the burn cannot hold. And two of the four dimensions on the spec are wrong against the mesh.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE FACE DIMENSIONS ARE TRANSCRIBED, NOT MEASURED — AND THEY GO ONTO METAL. 90 × 44 is 2.045:1, which is 4065 / 1983.3 — body length over ROOF-AT-REAR-AXLE. That is not the silhouette's height. Measured this session on a T1_SUB=1 build (watched print): side-view bbox excluding counter/tail board/lid = x −1.9048..2.1600, z 0.0000..2.0113, i.e. 4064.8 × 2011.3 mm, ratio 2.021 → a 90 mm face is 44.5 mm tall, not 44.0. With the counter and propped board in — the taqueria, which is what the brand actually is — it is 4580.0 × 3160.5 mm, ratio 1.449 → 62.1 mm tall. Both stated faces (90×44 and 120×59) inherit the same wrong pair. This is a 1.1% vertical squash on a concept whose entire pitch is "the first accurate one", cut permanently into brass, in a project whose first rule is that a figure in a paragraph goes stale silently.

2. THE MODEL'S UNIQUE CONTRIBUTION DOES NOT SURVIVE THE PROCESS. Reduction is 4065 → 90 mm = 45.2:1, so 1 mm on the face = 45.2 mm of vehicle. Char bloom from a 0.8 mm raised line on deli paper runs 1.4–2.2 mm depending on dwell and moisture — call it ±0.3–0.7 mm lateral, i.e. ±14–32 mm of vehicle. Now price the three claimed model-only features against that: aperture corner r55 → 1.22 mm, GONE. Rear arch lip to tyre gap 39.7 mm → 0.88 mm, GONE. Aperture 515.5 × 403.0 → 11.4 × 8.9 mm counters that close to roughly 9.4 × 6.9 under bloom, surviving as blobs without their corners. Only the gross silhouette, the wheel stations and the rake (17.75 mm/m → 71 mm over the body → 1.57 mm on the face) survive — and every one of those is something a competent illustrator gets right from a photograph. The concept says "the model is how you find that out before you cut brass"; it is not, because the model cannot predict char bloom. Only test burns can, and the concept schedules them AFTER the reduction is committed.

3. "HEATED ON THE PLANCHA THE KITCHEN ALREADY OWNS" IS THE LOAD-BEARING COST CLAIM AND IT IS FALSE FOR HALF THE SUBSTRATES. A conducting block cannot exceed the plancha's own surface temperature: ~200–230 °C, reached asymptotically. The 90 × 44 × 8 mm brass face is ~269 g (0.0317 L × 8.5 g/cm³) needing ~29 kJ to reach 300 °C; the 120 × 59 face is ~566 g. Wood branding wants 350–450 °C — impossible on a plancha, so the serving board is out. Masa at ~60% moisture on a 200 °C iron does not char in a service-length dwell; the tortilla is marginal at best and will dry where it marks. Paper is borderline at 200–220 °C. Leather is the only clean one. So "consumables are zero, substrates are already in the building, no added equipment" collapses: the $150–600 electric press belongs in the FIRST budget, not the volume upgrade.

4. THE COST AND LEAD TIME ARE OPTIMISTIC BY ROUGHLY 2×. $40–80 for a 3 mm laser test face is right; "next day at any laser shop" is right only if he has a local one, otherwise 3–7 days plus shipping. $180–350 for a CNC'd 8 mm brass face, silver braze and a turned ash handle is under the market — custom irons of this size typically land $250–600, and two weeks is the quote, not the delivery; four to six is normal. The shank engraving needs a fiber marker on a 6 mm round — a second vendor the budget does not name. Milling 2.5–3 mm of relief across a 90 × 44 background with a ≤2 mm cutter (needed for the r1.2 corners) is slow brass time and it is what pushes the price. Honest total to a working production tool: $600–1,200 and 6–10 weeks.

5. IT CANNOT CLOSE ITS LOOP INSIDE A REVISION, AND THIS OWNER SAID "WE NEED TO FINALLY PRESENT SOMETHING". Every other shortlisted item ships a file the same session — rev 77 already shipped two SVG/PNG artefacts through line_pass.py and sheet.py. EL FIERRO ships nothing to look at for six weeks and then ships a photograph the AI cannot take, of a test the AI cannot run, in a kitchen the AI cannot reach. "There is no file to send" is written as the virtue; on this machine it is the schedule risk. The proposed first step — ten sheets side by side, photographed — is the right experiment and it is entirely outside the team's hands.

6. THE INFRASTRUCTURE GAP IS SMALLER THAN THE BRIEF SAYS AND THE CONCEPT MISSES THE CREDIT AND THE REMAINING GAP BOTH. The line pass DOES exist as of rev 77 (`line_pass.py`, Grease Pencil Line Art → polylines → SVG, with `--svg`), and the side ortho camera does fit (5.90 in `studio.py`; front and rear sit at 3.55 and clip, correctly avoided here). But Line Art emits OPEN STROKES in camera-normalised coordinates, not the single CLOSED boundary with sorted positive/negative regions that a cutter or a CNC toolpath needs, and nobody has built that step. Checked this session: `ezdxf`, `shapely`, `svgpathtools`, `potrace` and `svgwrite` are all absent from the environment; there is no DXF writer in the tree. So "the model gives you the face" is one unbuilt conversion away, not zero — small work, one or two sessions, but it is work and it is unnamed.

7. TWO BLOCKERS IT DOES NOT COST AT ALL, BOTH ALREADY ON THIS PROJECT'S OWN BENCH. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md` flags this exact concept (`dream:free-1-1`) as one of the three worst trade-dress exposures in the pool — "three-dimensional metal objects cut to the profile — that is trade dress, not fair-use depiction" — and the concept answers a different charge, arguing the ROUNDEL is composed out. The roundel is not the exposure; the SILHOUETTE is the asserted mark, and this is a metal tool cut to that profile used commercially. The same file names it derivative of the branded burger bun and gives the livestock connotation an "honourable mention" among the concepts that would embarrass the brand. None of that is priced here, and clearing it costs one email that must happen BEFORE the $180–350, not after.

8. THE PASS-TIME COST IS UNMEASURED. A taqueria pass is the tightest constraint in the building. A 3–5 s press per cover at 200 covers is 10–17 minutes of pass time, plus a 269 g hot iron parked in the station next to hands and, per the concept's own risk (a), children. Nobody has costed the service-rate hit, and the operator — not the designer — decides it.

WHAT IT GETS RIGHT, SO THE SCORE IS NOT LOWER: it uses the one camera that works and needs neither of the clipped ones; it touches neither F191 nor F193 nor the emblem; the geometry it asks for is genuinely millable; it carries its own ceiling on the shank ("ROOF UNMEASURED"); and it is the only item in this pool that arrived with real prices, which fixes the rev-77 critic's own TOP complaint that nothing in ~50 concepts had a cost.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. RE-DERIVE THE FACE OFF THE MESH BEFORE ANYTHING IS CUT. Use the figures measured this session: closed-van silhouette 4064.8 × 2011.3 mm (ratio 2.021) → 90 × 44.5 mm; taqueria-with-counter-and-propped-board 4580.0 × 3160.5 mm (ratio 1.449) → 90 × 62.1 mm. Decide WHICH vehicle the mark is and print the number in the same script that writes the cutter file, so it cannot go stale. Note the propped board is a POSE the project has flagged wrong by ~10° for seven revisions — if it enters the outline, it enters the brass; prefer the closed van for that reason alone and say so.

2. MEASURE THE BLOOM FIRST, AND MAKE IT THE FIRST STEP INSTEAD OF THE TASTE TEST. One $60–80 laser order, THREE steel test faces at 90 / 130 / 180 mm, same artwork. Burn a grid — 3 dwells × 3 temperatures × 3 substrates — scan the sheets flat at 1200 dpi, and measure mean lateral bloom in mm against the cut geometry. That yields the project's minimum feature size for EVERY burn artefact, converts "he either loves it or he doesn't" into a number, and is exactly the kind of watched measurement this repo's standard demands. Do not cut brass until that number exists.

3. THEN PICK A SCALE THAT THE ACCURACY CLAIM SURVIVES, OR DROP THE CLAIM. If the bloom lands at ±0.5 mm, a 90 mm face cannot carry the aperture corners or the arch gap and the pitch must be trimmed to "a correct silhouette, not a caricature" — still true, still worth saying. If the model's precision is the point, go to 160–200 mm on the wooden board only, where 1 mm = 20–25 mm of vehicle and the r55 corners, the 39.7 mm arch gap and the rake all clear the bloom. Two tools, two claims, priced separately.

4. FIX THE HEAT STORY IN THE BUDGET, NOT IN THE PITCH. A plancha caps at its own surface temperature. Either put the bench-top electric element in the first spend (making the honest starting budget ~$250 rather than ~$50) or cut the substrate list to paper and leather and delete the board and the tortilla from v1.

5. SHIP A SCREEN DELIVERABLE THIS REVISION SO THE CONCEPT IS NOT INVISIBLE FOR SIX WEEKS. `line_pass.py --view side --svg` already exists; the missing piece is a closed contour, not a line pass. Build silhouette-mask → `trace_outline.py` (already in the tree, with a selftest) → simplify → offset to a burn line → SVG, and render a 1200 dpi proof with the stroke DILATED by the measured bloom radius. That hands the owner a picture of the burn before the burn exists, and it is one or two sessions of AI work, not weeks. Send the shop SVG or add one dependency; there is no DXF writer here and `ezdxf`/`shapely`/`potrace` are not installed.

6. CLEAR THE TRADE DRESS BEFORE THE $180–350, AND ANSWER THE RIGHT CHARGE. The exposure is a metal object cut to VW's T1 profile used commercially, not the roundel. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md` already flags this concept by id as one of the three worst in the pool. One email, one gate, before tooling.

7. COST THE PASS. Time a mock press ten times, multiply by peak covers, and put the seconds in the concept. If it exceeds a few seconds a cover, restrict the tortilla burn to a photographed launch and keep the paper burn for service — which is where the emotional transaction lives anyway.

The hot iron, the inconsistency and the burn stay exactly as they are. Nothing above defangs it; it makes the risky part measurable and the dimensions true.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1.5/4 — REFUSED ON ORIGINALITY. The artefact category is a catalogue product, the cultural ancestor is the wrong one (and the right one is a documented Mexican tradition the concept never found), the image is the most-reproduced vehicle outline in consumer graphics and is VW's asserted trade dress, and the design move underneath — accurate ortho elevation + the three apertures as the only counters + an engraved dimension that confesses its own ceiling — is TRES HUECOS welded to THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE. A substrate change is not a concept change. What is fresh here is the reduction-as-engineering-problem and the $50 first step; what is claimed as fresh — "an identity with zero ink", "the first accurate bus silhouette" — is neither.**

* ATTACKS: FIRST, WHAT SURVIVES, SO THE REST IS FAIR. Every source figure checks out against the tree this session: studio.py line 1678 reads `ortho=5.90` for the side camera and 3.55 for the two that clip; t1_shell.py reads `BAY_W = 0.5155  # equal, measured`, `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550`, `ARCH_R = 0.3735`; t1_core.py reads `TIRE_R = 0.3325` and `RAKE_DZDX = 0.017750`. The 90/55/25 mm faces are labelled CHOICES. It does not touch the forbidden pillar asymmetry. The first step is cheap and genuinely decisive. None of that is originality.

1. THE BOLDEST ELEMENT IS THE MOST PRECEDENTED ONE. "The risky one — the tortilla itself" is an off-the-shelf product. Branding Irons Unlimited sells a whole Branding Irons for Food line and states the low-temperature settings for "burger buns, bread and tortillas" (https://brandingirons.com/collections/branding-irons-for-food). Etsy sells custom electric food branding irons to any logo, next day (https://www.etsy.com/listing/1887044681/personalized-food-branding-iron-multiple). And it has been done in this exact cuisine and market: Evil Cooks, an LA pop-up, brands its demon mark onto tortillas — Texas Monthly covers branded/ceremonial tortillas and quotes the founders' rationale verbatim, "When you see it, you remember us" (https://www.texasmonthly.com/food/tortillas-ceremoniales/). That is the concept's ritual claim, already made, by a cooler operator, five years earlier. A concept whose stated risk is its most conventional element has mis-priced itself.

2. IT PICKED THE WRONG MEXICAN ANCESTOR, AND THE RIGHT ONE IS SITTING UNDER ITS PRIMARY SUBSTRATE. The fierro is a livestock ownership mark — the concept itself flags that as a liability on food, correctly. Meanwhile the tradition of burning a closed-line institutional mark into PAPER is Mexican, documented, and catalogued: marcas de fuego, New Spain from the 17th century, burned onto book fore-edges with a red-hot iron, one registered mark per library, now a Catálogo Colectivo across seventeen institutions (http://www.marcasdefuego.buap.mx:8180/xmLibris/projects/firebrand/, https://fahho.mx/marcas-de-fuego/, https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marca_de_fuego). The concept's own primary substrate is paper, its own framing is "one closed line, registered, burned" — and it cited cattle. Two consequences: the framing is not an invention, it is a graphic category with existing scholarship; and the version of it the concept chose is the one with the connotation problem it then has to apologise for. Not finding this in the research is the originality failure, not the trademark risk.

3. IT IS THE FLAT-MEASURED-LINE AESTHETIC UNDER A NEW LABEL, AND THE BENCH ALREADY HAS BOTH HALVES. The face is: true ortho side elevation, reduced, with the three serving apertures as the only interior counters — that is TRES HUECOS ("the three apertures as the mark"), already on the next-eight. The shank is: a measured dimension engraved beside a declaration of what is unmeasured — that is THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE ("every dimension line confesses how it was obtained"), fused with AS MEASURED and TAMAÑO REAL. Change the substrate from paper to brass and the drawing is the same drawing. Worse, "4065 × 1750 · ROOF UNMEASURED" is now the fourth artefact in this program to engrave its own epistemics on its own body. The first time that is a finding. The fourth time it is a house tic, and a reviewer outside the project will read it as such: a studio that cannot make an object without signing it with its own methodology.

4. THE ORIGINALITY CLAIM IS BELOW ITS OWN MEDIUM'S NOISE FLOOR, AND THE CONCEPT SAYS SO IN THE NEXT PARAGRAPH. It asserts "every VW bus silhouette in the world is a caricature… at 90 mm the difference is the whole concept." At 4065 → 90 mm the scale is 45:1, so 45 mm of real vehicle is 1 mm on brass. The accurate-vs-caricature delta lives in nose radius and roof crown — a few millimetres on the face, against char bleed on deli paper and masa that nobody in this program has ever measured. And the concept concedes the medium destroys detail in its own risk section: the counters land at 11.4 × 8.9 mm with r 1.2 and are "at the edge of what char will hold". You cannot claim the burn is too coarse to hold a 1.2 mm radius and fine enough to publish a 2 mm silhouette correction. No burn line-spread function has been measured; there is no floor under the difference; by this project's own standard that is not a measurement, it is a hope. Separately: measurable is not perceptible and perceptible is not meaningful — no customer holds a caricature next to the paper.

5. THE IMAGE IS THE LEAST ORIGINAL OBJECT AVAILABLE, AND ACCURACY AGGRAVATES THE PROBLEM RATHER THAN SOLVING IT. The T1 side silhouette is the most reproduced vehicle outline in forty years of consumer graphics. Composing the roundel out buys nothing, because the shape is itself the asserted mark: VW has litigated on "the Microbus design mark" and trade dress in "the distinctive shape and overall appearance of the VW Microbus automobile", and commentary is explicit that a recognisable vehicle shape can infringe without the logo (https://www.gerbenlaw.com/blog/what-is-trade-dress-and-how-can-it-protect-your-brand/, https://intellectualpropertyplanet.wordpress.com/2022/02/14/volkswagen-successfully-defended-its-classic-van-trademark-in-the-eu/). The concept's differentiator — maximum fidelity to the real T1 — is precisely the axis on which confusion is judged. The accurate silhouette is legally worse than the caricature, and this is the one artefact in the program intended to be replicated permanently, in brass, onto every item that crosses the counter.

6. THE RITUAL IS THE GENERIC ARTISANAL PITCH, AND IT DOES NOT FIT THE ACTUAL BUSINESS. "Made in front of the person it is made for, by the hand that cooked, at the moment of hand-off" has been the standard craft-food line since roughly 2010; it is what the Etsy iron is sold on. And the client is not a ranch: Tacombi runs 15+ taquerias across five cities with a stated target of 75, and already manufactures a packaged tortilla brand, Vista Hermosa (https://www.tacombi.com/our-story, https://www.adweek.com/brand-marketing/tacombi-is-servicing-its-community/). A hot iron at every pass in a scaling multi-unit QSR is not an identity, it is a training, insurance and franchise-consistency problem — and branding the tortilla puts a second mark on a product that already carries one, at volumes no hand iron reaches.

7. "THERE IS NO FILE" IS BOTH A FAMILIAR TROPE AND FACTUALLY FALSE HERE. The physical-only, no-logo-file identity is a well-worn conceptual register. It is also not what is being proposed: the brass is CNC-cut from a vector, so the file is not absent — it is the only deliverable, and the reduction (the designer's week the cost section names) is the entire work. "Ink count: zero. Print run: none. There is no file to send" is rhetoric that the production spec contradicts three lines above it.

8. MINOR BUT REAL: THE NAME. Fierro/El Fierro is a common Spanish-language restaurant and brand name; it is weak on distinctiveness and weak on search, and this program has already burned a revision on a mark it could not clear.
* WOULD FIX IT: SIX CONCRETE MOVES. The first two are the ones that decide whether this is a new concept or a relabelled one.

1. CHANGE THE ANCESTOR, AND WITH IT THE SUBSTRATE HIERARCHY: FIERRO → MARCA DE FUEGO. Stop marking the food and start marking the paper the way New Spain marked its libraries — burned on the FORE-EDGE of the stack, so the mark is visible on the closed block of deli paper at the pass and appears in fragments on each sheet as the stack is drawn down. That single change (a) removes the livestock connotation the concept itself identified as its worst liability, (b) removes the food-safety and children-near-hot-iron story from the customer-facing moment, (c) inherits a genuinely Mexican graphic tradition that no food brand is currently using, with a seventeen-institution catalogue to cite for provenance, and (d) makes "registered mark, one owner, one iron" literally true rather than metaphorical. Register the mark into that lineage explicitly — the tradition is that each mark is unique to its holder, which is the concept's own claim, already institutionalised.

2. DROP THE BUS SILHOUETTE. It is simultaneously the most-copied image available and VW's asserted trade dress, and its accuracy claim cannot survive the medium. Burn instead the one closed contour that only this mesh can produce and that is not a bus: a SECTION CUT at a named station — the body's true closed outline at, say, x = −1.100, where the serving band, the counter and the arch all appear in one contour. It is a closed line, it has natural counters, it is unmistakably not a caricature, it is not anyone's trade dress, and it is unforgeable without the model. Let the station coordinate be the mark's serial: a different station is a different iron, so locations, years or product lines each get a real, non-arbitrary variant. That is a system, not a logo, and nothing on the 78-title bench is it.

3. MEASURE THE MEDIUM BEFORE CLAIMING ACCURACY — SAME $50, SAME LASER CUT. Do not cut the reduction first. Cut a test plate: a line-width ladder at 0.3 / 0.5 / 0.8 / 1.2 / 2.0 mm, a corner-radius ladder at r 0.4 / 0.8 / 1.2 / 2.0 mm, and a pair of nested closed counters. Burn it on deli paper, board and masa at three iron temperatures × three dwell times, scan at 1200 dpi, and publish the burn's line-spread and the minimum radius the char actually holds, with its spread across the batch. THEN state what accuracy survives. Until that plate exists, "the first accurate bus silhouette" is a claim with no floor under it, and the program's own rules forbid publishing it.

4. RESOLVE THE TORTILLA EXPLICITLY, DO NOT LEAVE IT AS THE BRAVE BIT. Either cut it — Vista Hermosa is an existing packaged tortilla brand with its own mark and volumes a hand iron cannot serve — or keep it and place it, in writing, against Evil Cooks' branded tortillas and the commodity food-iron trade, and name what is different. "Ours is dimensionally accurate" is not a difference at masa scale; "ours is a section, not a picture of the van" might be.

5. TAKE THE EPISTEMICS OFF THE OBJECT. One artefact in this program may carry ROOF UNMEASURED; this is the fourth to want to. Engrave the shank with the station coordinate, a serial and a date, and let THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE — which exists for exactly this — carry the ceilings. The tool should be a tool, not a footnote with a handle.

6. CLEAR THE NAME AND THE SHAPE BEFORE BRASS, NOT AFTER. A knockout search on "Fierro" in class 43 and a fifteen-minute counsel read on the silhouette both cost less than the $180–350 production face and far less than discovering it on the object that, by design, ends up in every customer's hands.

If moves 1, 2 and 3 are taken, this stops being TRES HUECOS on a stick and becomes something the bench does not have: a serialised, section-cut fire mark in a documented Mexican paper-marking tradition, with its own measured legibility floor. That is worth re-scoring. As submitted, it is not.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**OWNER-AND-BRAND — 1.5/4 — REJECT AS SPECIFIED, SALVAGE THE OBJECT. Scale used: 0 = he rejects it and it damages the brand; 4 = he would be proud to hand it to someone. EL FIERRO scores 1.5. The tool is genuinely handsome and the shank engraving is the most owner-native line in the whole rev-76 bench. But as written it (a) puts hot brass on food, which is a lead and food-code problem the concept does not mention at all, (b) frames a Mexican taqueria's identity as a livestock OWNERSHIP mark, (c) makes a third party's protected vehicle shape the restaurant's source-identifying mark in trade, which is a worse trademark posture than any poster or sticker in the programme, (d) hands the owner nothing this revision after he said "we need to finally present something", and (e) sits in the handed-to-you tier that rev 77 (F331) just assigned to CHILDREN, while requiring a red-hot iron at the pass. Two of those five are fixable in an afternoon; three need a decision he has not been asked for.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE TORTILLA KILLS IT, AND THE CONCEPT NEVER SEES THE REAL REASON. Its stated risk (c) is char variance; the unstated one is materials. CNC shops cut brass in free-machining alloy by default, which is leaded (C360 is roughly 3 % lead). The concept specifies "8 mm brass" and then presses it into masa. That is a hot leaded food-contact surface, unlisted, uncleanable between covers, stored on a plancha. A health inspector does not need to reach the "scorched paper" question the concept anticipates; he asks what the tool is made of and whether it is sanitized. This is the one attack the owner cannot argue with, because it is not taste. It is also entirely removable — see fix 1.

2. A FIERRO IS AN OWNERSHIP MARK ON AN ANIMAL, AND THE CONCEPT ASKS A MEXICAN-AMERICAN CUSTOMER TO EAT IT. The concept lists this as risk (b) and then proceeds, which is the wrong order for the one risk that is about the brand's relationship to the culture it trades on. This is a hospitality group in the United States selling Mexican food; the sentence "we burn our ownership brand onto your food before we hand it to you" is one screenshot away from being the only thing anyone knows about the campaign. It is not a fringe reading — it is the literal, correct reading, and the concept supplies it itself. The register is wrong, not the object.

3. THE VW SILHOUETTE IS A WORSE TRADEMARK EXPOSURE HERE THAN ANYWHERE ELSE IN THE PROGRAMME, AND THE CONCEPT DISCHARGES THE WRONG RISK. "Composes the VW roundel out — F191 is untouched, and the roundel is VW's trademark anyway" banks credit for a free move: a side elevation never contained the nose roundel, so nothing was composed out. Meanwhile the concept's whole selling proposition is that this is the FIRST ACCURATE T1 SILHOUETTE, i.e. maximally recognisable as Volkswagen's body shape — and a fierro is defined in the concept's own words as "a registered ranch mark". That is third-party trade dress used as a source identifier in commerce, on every package, by a multi-unit operator. A poster is decorative use; an identity mark is not. Nine emblem rejections show the owner cares about this vehicle's marks being right; he will not enjoy discovering the mark he adopted belongs to Wolfsburg.

4. IT CONTRADICTS THE RULING MADE AT REV 77, WHICH THE CONCEPT DOES NOT CITE. F331: "Split it deliberately — kids get one line, adults another", with the FREE / HANDED-TO-YOU things assigned to CHILDREN and the paid/kept things to adults. EL FIERRO's payload is precisely the handed-to-you tier: the paper the food arrives on. So by his own fresh ruling this is a children's artefact, and it is delivered by a red-hot iron in a room with children in it, marking food with a livestock brand. F18's brief for the children's line is "spark joy … for children" and "something families keep". A scorched sheet of deli paper is not kept; it is thrown away with the grease on it. Every child-facing property of this concept is negative.

5. IT SHIPS HIM NOTHING, IN THE REVISION WHOSE WHOLE POINT IS SHIPPING. "There is no file to send" is offered as the poetry and is the operational defect. He said "we need to finally present something" and "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry." What this concept hands him is a request: spend $50 at a laser shop, then a designer's week, then two weeks of lead time at a trophy maker. He cannot see it in the conversation, cannot forward it, cannot put it on a cup, an awning, an app icon, a tote or a grocery pack. Telling a restaurateur "the identity is not a file" reads, from where he sits, as a designer being clever at his expense — it says NO to the deliverable he asked for and calls the refusal the idea.

6. HIS TEMPERAMENT IS THE ONE VARIABLE THIS CONCEPT BETS AGAINST. "A fraction of every batch will be blobs and there is no QC. The inconsistency is the charm and it is also the defect, and he may hate it." This is the man who rejected an emblem NINE times over stroke directions at sub-millimetre scale, whose standing bar is "any single measurement off is unacceptable, per-measurement and not on average", and whose finish ruling is "I want this 3d model to look like new." The concept's FIRST STEP frames the decision as a coin flip — "he either loves that no two are the same or he does not". It is not a coin flip. Lay ten sheets in front of him and he will pick up the worst one. Presenting a variance you cannot control to an owner defined by refusing variance is a predictable rejection, and it burns an asking on him.

7. THE MARK'S OWN CAPTION DISAGREES WITH THE MARK. The shank reads 4065 × 1750. But `STATE.md` measures 4.317 m WITH the projecting parts and 4.065 m without — "what projects: counter, counter_nosing, counter_top, tail_board, tail_board_stay …". The side ortho of this vehicle AS IT SERVES contains the cantilevered counter (SPEC §1: "running past the tail"), the opened roof lid and the propped tail board. So either the burned outline is the serving bus and the engraved number is 252 mm wrong against its own shape, or the outline is a bare Kombi with no counter — in which case it is not this vehicle and the accuracy claim that justifies the whole concept evaporates. In a project whose rule 12 is "report the measurement WITH ITS CEILING", shipping a caption that does not describe the object it is engraved on is the exact failure the programme exists to avoid.

8. A MIRRORING ERROR AT THE LASER SHOP SILENTLY FABRICATES AN ELEVATION AND NOTHING HERE CAN CATCH IT. A raised face burns mirror-reversed. The three serving apertures are on the SHOW side (+Y, `STATE.md`: "open serving apertures on +Y: 3"; SPEC grades it M). The other side is SPEC's "Side, off side (−Y) … twin outward-hinged cargo doors + three glazed windows", graded **E (never photographed)** — different features, different counters, no reference frame in existence. Cut the face unmirrored and every impression the brand ever makes is of a side of this vehicle that no photograph shows and no gate scores. The concept's cut-file workflow has no mirror control and no check.

9. THE ARTISANAL KIT IS THE MOST EXHAUSTED ONE THERE IS. The concept anticipates "steakhouses brand buns" and misses the bigger problem: burned wooden board + leather apron patch + kraft paper + brass tool is the complete, saturated 2014 Brooklyn craft-hospitality set. The claim "every mark this brand could make already exists as vinyl; a fierro is the opposite" is itself the most familiar move in the category. To a design-literate viewer this does not read as fresh; it reads as heritage cosplay — which is the one accusation a real, Mexican-food, actually-has-a-1963-bus brand should never be exposed to, because it is the only brand in the room that does not need to fake it.

10. IT DOES NOT SCALE, AND AN IDENTITY THAT DOES NOT SCALE IS A RITUAL, NOT AN IDENTITY. It requires a trained hand, a hot tool and half a second of dwell at the pass, at every location, on every ticket, forever. Ticket times, burn injuries, staff turnover, and — worst for the brand — inconsistent application across units, so the mark reads sloppy rather than handmade. The concept's own answer to this is the bench press at $150–600, which quietly deletes the entire premise: a thermostatically controlled press at the back is not "made by the hand that cooked, at the moment of the hand-off".

WHAT IS GENUINELY GOOD, AND SHOULD SURVIVE ANY REWRITE: the engraved shank — "4065 × 1750 · ROOF UNMEASURED" — is the single most owner-native idea on the bench. A tool that carries its own ceiling is this project's method turned into an object, and it is the one thing here he would be proud to put in someone's hand. The hand-off moment is also correctly identified as the brand's real emotional transaction, and no poster contains it. Both survive the fixes below.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. DELETE FOOD CONTACT ENTIRELY. Paper, board, leather. No tortilla, no bun, nothing eaten. That removes the lead question, the sanitation question and most of the inspector's interest in one line, and it costs the concept nothing it can defend. If food contact ever returns, it is 316 stainless or a lead-free alloy (C69300 / C87850) with a written cleaning step and a spare tool — spec it before quoting a shop, because the shop will default to C360.

2. CHANGE THE FRAME FROM OWNERSHIP TO CLOSURE, AND CHANGE THE NAME WITH IT. A fierro marks an animal as property; a SELLO closes a package. Same brass, same heat, same half-second of smoke, opposite sentence: the mark of the house sealing what it made, like a wax seal or a panadería stamp. Test it the cheap way — say the name out loud to Mexican-American kitchen staff before it is cut. If the name has to be defended in a caption, it is the wrong name.

3. CURE THE TRADEMARK POSTURE BEFORE BRASS, AND BUILD THE FALLBACK IN THE SAME WEEK. Put "our identity mark is an accurate VW T1 body outline" to counsel as a source-identifying use, not a decorative one. In parallel cut the mark that carries zero VW shape: THE THREE APERTURES ALONE — 515.5 × 403.0 mm, r 55, three, EQUAL, straight off `t1_shell.py` — three burned rectangles and a rule. It is the programme's own TRES HUECOS move, it is unmistakably this vehicle to anyone who has stood at the counter, it survives at 25 mm where the silhouette closes up, and it is cheaper to cut. Test both faces in the same $50 laser order and let the burns decide.

4. PUT A MIRROR CONTROL IN THE CUT FILE. The face must be mirrored so the impression reads the SHOW flank (+Y, three open apertures). Cut an asymmetric fiducial into the face, and write the acceptance test as a sentence anyone can run: burn one, hold it beside `out/rNN_side.png`, confirm the three apertures and the counter sit on the same end as the frame. The off side (−Y) is graded E, never photographed, and has cargo doors and glazed windows instead — a mirroring error would ship a fabricated elevation that nothing in this repo can score.

5. FIX THE CAPTION OR FIX THE OUTLINE, AND SAY WHICH. Either burn the bus AS IT SERVES — counter, propped board, roof lid — and engrave the figure that describes it (4317 mm with the projections, 4065 without; `STATE.md` prints both), or burn the bare body and say so. Two numbers on a shank, one of them labelled, is more in his register than one number that is silently wrong.

6. SHIP HIM SOMETHING THIS REVISION, BEFORE ANY BRASS EXISTS. The side ortho already fits (`studio.py`, side cam at +Y). Render the reduction at 90 / 55 / 25 mm, hand him a one-page PDF of the three, and mark where the aperture counters close — that is the legibility finding, it costs one render, and it is a thing he can hold this week rather than in three.

7. TURN THE CHAR COIN-FLIP INTO A MEASUREMENT BEFORE PUTTING IT TO HIM. Do not ask "do you love that no two are the same". Burn twenty on his own paper at one temperature and one dwell, photograph them in a row, and publish the failure rate with its ceiling — n = 20, one paper stock, one hand. Then the question becomes "one in N is a blob; is that acceptable", which is a question this owner is equipped to answer. As posed, it is a taste ambush and it will lose.

8. RECONCILE IT WITH F331 EXPLICITLY, IN THE CONCEPT'S OWN TEXT. As written it is in the handed-to-you tier, which rev 77 assigned to CHILDREN. Move it to the adult line — the tool itself, plus a burned board, sold or gifted, kept and displayed, which is where the shank engraving earns most — and let the children's line be something a seven-year-old actually keeps. And answer the hot-iron-near-children question in writing rather than listing it: bench press with a dwell stop and a thermostat, behind the pass, or one flagship's ritual with a named trained operator and nothing rolled out.

9. IF ONLY ONE THING IS BUILT, BUILD THE SHANK. A small brass object engraved "4065 × 1750 · ROOF UNMEASURED" is the giftable, photographable, unarguable half of this concept, it carries no VW shape, it touches no food, it needs no ruling, and it is the only thing on this bench that turns the project's method into something you can put in a person's hand.

dangerIntact = true, deliberately: these fixes keep the heat, the smoke at the pass, the mark made in front of the customer, and the fact that no two impressions match. What they remove is only the uninsurable part — lead on food, an ownership brand on a culture's dish, and another company's body shape as this restaurant's registered mark.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**DANGER — 2/4 — The dangerous thing here is a FORM, and the concept has filled it with the safest possible CONTENT.
