A fierro is an ownership cipher burned onto a living thing by the person who owns it. This one is a picture of a van. That single substitution — cipher replaced by charming silhouette — is the wheel-arch-plant-becomes-tin-of-herbs move performed at the level of the mark itself, and everything downstream of it goes soft. What survives is real but incidental: an identity with no file, no QC and no repeatability is a genuine institutional threat to a multi-site hospitality group, and ROOF UNMEASURED engraved on a production tool is an admission of failure permanently cut into the object. What has been sanded is the whole reason the object is a fierro rather than a stamp. Score 2, danger NOT intact.**

* ATTACKS: FOUR SANDINGS, IN ORDER OF SEVERITY.

1. THE MARK OWNS NOTHING AND BELONGS TO NOBODY. The concept's own second paragraph gets it exactly right — "a registered ranch mark" — and then never registers it, never names an owner, and never says what is being claimed. A fierro is legally and semantically an assertion: this animal is MINE, and here is the registry entry that proves it. Strip the ownership assertion and you have a decorative hot stamp with a rural costume on. The concept knows the connotation is live ("a fierro marks ownership on an animal, and applying it to food will read badly") and files it under RISK rather than under CONTENT. That is the failure I am here to catch: the danger is named in the register and evacuated from the artefact. The genuinely uncomfortable version of this piece says out loud that the food is owned by the business until the instant it crosses the counter, and burns a mark that means that. This version says "look, our bus."

2. THE FACE IS A FRIENDLY BUS. Every fierro that has ever existed is a cipher — a monogram, a brand-letter, a closed abstract line legible at a glance across a field. The concept substitutes an accurate T1 side elevation with three window holes. At 90 mm on paper that is a food-truck logo; it is the single most reassuring image the project holds. The concept even flags the failure mode itself — "reads as a generic hippie van at 90 mm" — and treats it as a reduction-quality problem when it is a CONTENT problem. No amount of accuracy rescues it: an accurate hippie van is still a hippie van. And the piece's own physics point the other way. It admits the 11.4 x 8.9 mm apertures are "at the edge of what char will hold." Char is going to close those counters. The mark that actually survives a burn IS a cipher — and the concept has the evidence in hand and declines to follow it.

3. THE RISKY SUBSTRATE IS OPTIONAL, AND THE FIRST STEP PROVES IT. Four substrates: deli paper, wooden board, leather apron patch, "and — the risky one — the tortilla itself," listed fourth, in a parenthesis, after a leather patch that carries no risk whatsoever and is pure merch. Then the first step: ten sheets of paper, one board, THREE tortillas. Ten to three. The photograph he is asked to judge is "lay the ten sheets side by side" — paper. The concept has already decided which substrate it believes in, and it is not the one it calls risky. Branding a tortilla is the entire provocation; branding deli paper is what a steakhouse does to a bun, which the concept itself names as prior art.

4. THE TRADE-DRESS PARAGRAPH IS FALSE REASSURANCE, WHICH IS WORSE THAN NO PARAGRAPH. "Composes the VW roundel out entirely — F191 is untouched, and the roundel is VW's trademark anyway." This gets the exposure backwards, and this project has already found it: CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md's own TOP finding says "THE SHAPE IS THE MARK," and names the medal, the sundial and THIS BRANDING IRON as "the worst exposure because they are three-dimensional metal objects cut to the profile — that is trade dress, not fair-use depiction." Omitting the roundel clears nothing; a solid brass object CUT TO THE T1 SILHOUETTE, used in commerce, on a product handed to a paying customer, is the exposure. The concept has taken a live legal danger and written a diligence-shaped sentence that makes it feel handled. A risk register that removes a real risk by a wrong argument is a sanding, not a disclosure.

TWO SMALLER ONES.

5. "THERE IS NO FILE TO SEND" IS NOT TRUE AND THE CONCEPT KNOWS IT. Three paragraphs later it renders a legibility ladder at 90/55/25 mm and sends a profile to a laser shop. A DXF is a file. In a project whose method rule reads "a claim in prose is not a measurement," this is a line written for the feeling. Either drop it or earn it.

6. THE CRAFT REGISTER IS DOING PACIFICATION WORK. "Silver-brazed to a 6 mm steel shank and an ash handle," "a trophy or branding-iron maker," "turned handle." This is heritage-catalogue language, and it converts a hot iron into an heirloom before anyone has been burned by it. Meanwhile risk (a) — fire, children, a health inspector — is stated and then not decided. Stating a risk is not carrying it.

WHAT IS GENUINELY STILL DANGEROUS, AND SHOULD NOT BE TOUCHED: the refusal of reproducibility. "No two impressions are the same," "a fraction of every batch will be blobs and there is no QC," "there is no file to send." For a group with more than one location that is an identity that cannot be delegated, scaled, or enforced — a real and unpleasant position that most brands would refuse outright. And ROOF UNMEASURED cut into the shank of a tool that goes on the pass every service is a confession of failure made permanent on a working object. Those two things have teeth. They are not, however, what the piece is about.
* WOULD FIX IT: FIVE PUT-BACKS. Each is a change to the artefact, not to the risk register.

1. NAME THE OWNER, ON THE TOOL. A fierro is registered. Decide whose mark this is and put the registration on it: register a mark in an actual Mexican fierro/marcas registry in the founder's name or in the name of the cook who will hold the iron, and engrave the registry number on the shank beside 4065 x 1750 · ROOF UNMEASURED. Two figures on that shank, one measured and open, one legal and closed. If nobody in the business will put their name to a mark burned onto food, that is the answer and the concept should be killed on the spot — but that refusal is the real test and the concept currently avoids taking it.

2. LET CHAR DECIDE THE MARK, NOT THE SILHOUETTE. Keep the reduction ladder, but do not stop it at legibility. Run 90 / 55 / 25 mm AND go past the point where the three apertures close, and ship the mark that masa can hold. It will not read as a VW bus and it must not be rescued into reading as one. Publish the ladder with the closed frames included, so the owner sees the accuracy he paid 76 revisions for being destroyed by the medium — that is the uncomfortable conversation this object exists to force, and it is currently hidden behind "the model is how you find that out before you cut brass." The cipher is the artefact; the bus is the thing you had to give up to get it.

3. THE TORTILLA IS THE ONLY SUBSTRATE. Delete the leather apron patch entirely — it is merch with no stake. Demote paper and board to failure surfaces. Rewrite the first step: sixty tortillas across three masa hydrations and two plancha temperatures, in one service, and the photograph he is shown is THE FAILURES — the blobs, the tears, the ones that came off the iron in pieces — laid out before the successes are shown at all. If he picks the successes the concept is dead and you have found that out for $50. Ten sheets of deli paper cannot ask him anything he does not already know the answer to.

4. RETRACT THE TRADE-DRESS SENTENCE AND REPLACE IT WITH THE TRUE ONE. Delete "the roundel is VW's trademark anyway" — it clears nothing. Write instead: this is the programme's largest legal exposure, because a three-dimensional metal object cut to the T1 profile and used in commerce is trade dress, not depiction (the project's own rev-77 bench says so at TOP severity), and it is priced at counsel's hourly rate or at a licence or at a deliberate, declared distortion of the profile. Pick one and say which. A concept that is dangerous should look dangerous in its own risk section, not safe.

5. DECIDE THE FIRE, IN THE ARTEFACT. Put it in the description, not the register: the iron lives on the pass, in the customer's sight line, hot, all service, and children watch it. That is the piece — a taqueria that keeps an open flame and a piece of glowing brass in front of the people it feeds. If the honest answer is that it goes back-of-house for insurance reasons, then the object is a stamp, the hand-off theatre in the concept's best paragraph is fiction, and the concept has no reason to exist. Choose in public.

AND ONE OPTIONAL MOVE THAT WOULD MAKE THE NO-FILE CLAIM TRUE: do not send a DXF. Print the model's orthographic profile at 1:1, cut the brass face by hand against it, and let the tool carry the maker's error. Then no two IRONS are the same either, the claim stops being a flourish, and the object's imprecision becomes a measured departure from the mesh you can actually publish.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

### ¿TRAE SU ENVASE? — audit mean 1.75/4

**FEASIBILITY — 2/4 — FEASIBLE AS A TEST, NOT AS AN ARTEFACT. The model dependency is real and I watched it work; the shipping object is four regulated businesses stapled together and its own brief never states the joint lead time or the total cash. The first step is the best on the bench. The bottle is the worst.**

* ATTACKS: WHAT I RAN, NOT WHAT I READ. Everything below marked [RAN] came off this machine this session.

1. [RAN] THE MODEL DEPENDENCY IS REAL, AND IT IS CHEAPER THAN THE CONCEPT'S OWN BRIEF ADMITS. The brief handed to me says "NO line-extraction pass exists yet." That is rev-76 text and it is stale: `line_pass.py` shipped at rev 77 (LEDGER_rev77.md), with `sheet.py` and two finished artefacts in `design_out/`. I ran `python3 line_pass.py --view side` — 18 s wall, 2711 strokes / 19467 points, "0 point(s) outside the frame". I drew it at the concept's stated 90 mm and LOOKED at it: /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-combi-render/36d24290-245a-543a-b2d3-9d646e20e4c5/scratchpad/proof90_600dpi.png. The bus is legible, the three apertures read, the cab shut line reads, the fringe reads. This concept's model deliverable costs eighteen seconds. That is the strongest verified dependency of anything I have audited here.

2. [RAN] BUT ONE OF THE FOUR THINGS IT SAYS THE MODEL SUPPLIES IS NOT IN THE PASS. The concept: "correct proportions, THE TWO-TONE BREAK, three equal apertures, the shut lines as screen registration marks." Three of four are in my proof. The two-tone break is NOT. Grease Pencil Line Art draws contours and creases; the cream/red boundary is a MATERIAL boundary on a smooth surface and generates no stroke. On a VW flank the belt-line break is the composition line — it is what a signwriter lays out against. It is buildable (`t1_mats.z_belt(x)` is live and is already a function, not a scalar) but it is a build, not a free by-product, and the concept hands the painter a ground that is missing the one line the painter most needs.

3. [RAN] 90 mm IS THE WRONG BRIEF FOR THE ROTULISTA, AND THE ASPECT IS WRONG FOR A BOTTLE. Measured off the pass: the drawn bbox is 4.580 × 3.132 m, aspect 1.463 — because the tail board is propped at 38° and adds 515 mm of width and the mural lid eats the top third of the panel (visible in my proof). A 150 ml squat bottle band wants roughly 2:1. `T1_REAR_OPEN=0` is one env var and gives you that. Separately: at 90 mm the raw pass carries 3740 mm of line in 90 × 61.5 mm — 0.7 mm of line per mm², ~11 % area coverage at a 0.15 mm stroke. No enamel brush expresses at that density. The painter must work at 300–400 mm and be reduced. The concept specifies the size of the OUTPUT as the size of the INPUT, and that is a spec error on "the asset the entire concept hangs on."

4. THE ARTEFACT IS FOUR BUSINESSES, NOT ONE, AND THE CONCEPT COSTS THEM ONE AT A TIME AND NEVER TOGETHER. (a) a scheduled-process acidified food — 21 CFR 114, process-authority letter, FCE/SID registration: 3–6 months, and the concept says so; (b) a decorated-glass run — capital and MOQ; (c) a commissioned artwork with a licence; (d) a returnable-container operation — regulated AND staffed. Each has a different long pole and they do not run in parallel, because you cannot separate colours off a panel that has not been painted, or order glass before you know the sauce is co-packable. Honest joint estimate: 4–7 months and $30–55k of cash out before the first real bottle exists. That total appears nowhere in the concept, which prices only per-unit and then reports 79–86 % gross. Per-unit margin on unbuilt inventory is not a feasibility argument. Against an owner who said "we need to finally present something," this is the longest-dated item on the bench by an order of magnitude.

5. FIVE INKS ON GLASS IS THE MOST EXPENSIVE DECISION IN THE CONCEPT AND ITS JUSTIFICATION IS AESTHETIC. "the vehicle carries five inks and this is the one object that can carry all of them" is a reason to want it, not a reason it can be made. Cylindrical direct-screen decoration is routine at 1–3 colours; 4–6 means five registered passes with intermediate cure on a curved body, a specialty vendor pool, higher MOQ and higher scrap. And it buys nothing legible: the print panel is ~50–60 mm tall on a ~58 mm-diameter body. Five inks cannot be resolved there. Two would halve decoration cost, quadruple the vendor list, and look the same at arm's length.

6. THE PREPRESS STEP HAS NO OWNER. Hand-painted enamel panel → photograph → five clean spot separations that hold the brushwork is skilled prepress. Nobody on "one plus an AI" does that, it is not on this machine, and it is not in the cost table. It is also the step where the commissioned hand gets destroyed — flatten a rotulista's enamel to five spots and you have bought a $150 panel to make a vector that looks like it was never painted.

7. [RAN] THE EVIDENCE FOR THE RETURNABLE ECONOMY IS REAL BUT DOES NOT SAY WHAT THE CONCEPT SAYS. I opened IMG_2073.jpeg. The stacked red crates of returnable glass Coca-Cola bottles are there, lower left — the concept says four; I count five or six faces in the near stack with a second stack behind. But that frame is the GREEN vehicle at the Mexican location (SPEC.md:10165 "IMG_2073.jpeg GREEN"; the menu board prices in pesos with "1 USD = $12"), and the deposit system in it is Coca-Cola FEMSA's, not Tacombi's. It proves the CUSTOMER is culturally fluent in refill. It is not evidence Tacombi can run one, and it is evidence from the jurisdiction where refill is legal, not the one where the concept says it may be prohibited.

8. THE REFILL'S REAL COST IS LABOUR, AND IT IS UNPRICED. "roughly $1.10 of product ... no packaging cost at all" omits the only genuinely scarce thing at a taco counter at 1 pm: 60–90 seconds of a counter person, plus a sanitising step if the fallback (dirty-in / clean-out) is what the inspector allows. `SURVEY_rev49_photoreal.md` documents the counter already carrying five foil caddies, a condiment rank and the tail-run bottles; a 24-bottle crate is ~300 × 450 mm of the most contested surface in the building. The mechanic is cheap in COGS and expensive in operations, and the concept only counts the first.

9. [RAN] A PRINTED FIGURE IS WRONG, ON AN OBJECT WHOSE WHOLE PROPOSITION IS MEASUREMENT AUTHORITY. The concept prints "roof at the rear axle 1983.5 (n = 230 mesh samples)". STATE.md reads 1.9833 at n=230 — 1983.3. Everything else I spot-checked holds: counter 107 mm thick / 321 mm deep and CNT_NOSE_F 0.1860 are verbatim `t1_detail.py:1596-1613`; FRINGE_R 0.0090 / FRINGE_PITCH 0.0260 verbatim at :3474 (the "65 per aperture" is a docstring at :3128 — a source comment, which rule 10 says is not a measurement, and the concept correctly labels it INFERRED); ⌀439.6 and "SIXTEEN INCH, NOT FIFTEEN" is genuine and carries its method in SPEC.md:193 (wheelbase/flange 5.46 ± 0.08, 4σ off 15"); colours check against `t1_mats`/SPEC. So this is one transcription slip in an otherwise well-grounded sheet — which is exactly the class the project's own rules exist to catch, and it would be printed on ten thousand bottles.

10. WHAT IT CORRECTLY DOES NOT NEED, AND THIS MATTERS. No photographer of the bus. No access to the vehicle. No front or rear camera fix — the side ortho fits at ortho 5.90 (studio.py:1677) and the front/rear clip at 3.55, and this concept never touches them. And the stated emblem risk is SMALLER than stated: in side elevation the nose roundel is edge-on and absent entirely from my proof; only the two hubcap badges appear, at ~1.7 mm, unjudgeable. F191 and F193 are untouched. The dependency graph is genuinely short.
* WOULD FIX IT: SPLIT IT INTO TWO PROJECTS AND SHIP THE CHEAP ONE THIS QUARTER. Concretely:

1. MAKE THE FIRST STEP THE DELIVERABLE, NOT THE PILOT. It is already the best first step on the bench — zero manufacturing, real customers, a real number by Friday. Stop calling it a precursor to the glass and let it be the rev-78 ship.

2. FIX THE BAND'S SPEC BEFORE PRINTING IT. Run `python3 line_pass.py --view side` with `T1_REAR_OPEN=0` so the propped board stops adding 515 mm of width; that takes the drawing from 1.46:1 to roughly 2:1, which is a bottle band. 18 seconds. Then reduce a curated stroke subset, not the raw 3740 mm of line.

3. BUILD THE BELT LINE INTO THE PASS AS A NAMED LAYER before any painter sees the ground. `t1_mats.z_belt(x)` already exists and is already a line, not a scalar. Half a day, and it is the flank's composition line.

4. BRIEF THE ROTULISTA AT 300–400 mm AND REDUCE. Same fee, same week, a panel whose brushwork survives separation. Photograph it flat, evenly lit, on a phone — that part is genuinely not hard.

5. TWO INKS FOR v1, NOT FIVE. Keep five as the ambition for a later run. This one decision moves the artefact from specialty-vendor-with-high-MOQ to routine, and nothing is lost at 50 mm of print height.

6. CALL THE HEALTH DEPARTMENT IN WEEK ONE — before the $150 rotulista fee, not after. It is a phone call and it is the only input that can zero the whole concept. Get the answer in writing.

7. PUT THE TOTAL CASH NUMBER IN THE CONCEPT. Not per-unit margin: the number that leaves the account before the first bottle sells. My estimate is $30–55k and 4–7 months for the artefact as written. If that number is right the owner should see it; if it is wrong, get quotes and replace it. Either way the concept currently reports 79–86 % gross without ever stating what is at risk.

8. PRICE THE REFILL IN SECONDS OF COUNTER LABOUR, and decide where the crate physically stands given what SURVEY says is already on that counter.

9. FIX 1983.5 → 1983.3, and add the same rule the sheets already use: every figure on the artefact is read out of STATE.md at draw time, never typed. `sheet.py` already does this for 40 figures on `sheet3_not_issued.svg`. Reuse it and the class of defect disappears.

10. WRITE THE LICENCE BEFORE THE BRUSH MOVES. The concept says this and is right; make it a dated item, not a sentence.

WHAT I WOULD NOT CHANGE: the commissioned-hand move, the refill mechanic, the credit line next to the ingredients, and naming the health-code risk first. The nerve is right. It is the scale and the sequence that are wrong.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1.5/4 — ORIGINALITY: WEAK. This is the best BUSINESS object on the bench and close to the least fresh. Every one of its five layers is a mature, named, findable thing, and the layer it claims as its own — proportional accuracy from the model — is invisible on the artefact. It is not the old flat-measured-line aesthetic (that charge mostly fails, and I say so below), but it escapes that trap by handing the drawing to a stranger, and what the stranger will make is a hand-painted VW bus on a bottle, which is the single most reproduced object in Western consumer goods.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE CLIENT ALREADY DOES THIS, AND THE CONCEPT DOES NOT KNOW IT. Tacombi runs Vista Hermosa, a packaged-Mexican-food CPG brand (tortillas, chips, salsa) sold at grocery, founded out of the same business, plus Tacombi Bodega retail. A house salsa in glass is not a merch concept for this client; it is a line extension of an operating packaged-goods company. The concept never names Vista Hermosa, never says how a counter-only refill SKU is categorically different from the shelf product, and therefore cannot claim novelty inside its own brand, let alone the category. This is the deepest hit and it is fatal as written.

2. THE ARTEFACT IS A FAITHFUL REPRODUCTION OF AN EXISTING OBJECT CLASS, AND THE CONCEPT PUTS THE PRIOR ART IN ITS OWN EVIDENCE PHOTO. Five colours screened direct to flint glass, no paper label, lug/crown closure, wooden crate of 24 — that is Applied Ceramic Labeling, the standard Mexican refresco bottle since the 1930s. Coca-Cola retornable, Jarritos, Sidral Mundet, Topo Chico, Squirt. The concept cites the crates in IMG_2073 as proof the economy exists; those same crates are the prior art for the object. Reproducing a vernacular faithfully is a legitimate design act, but it is not fresh, and the concept presents it as an invention ("the only merch item that..."). The one genuinely under-occupied pocket — ACL refresco idiom applied to SALSA rather than refresco — is narrow, is never named as the novelty, and is not what the concept is arguing for.

3. THE REFILL MECHANIC IS ZERO-NOVELTY AND THE CONCEPT ADMITS IT. Deposit-return and bring-your-own-container are mature on every axis: Mexican retornable, Loop by TerraCycle (2019, Häagen-Dazs/Tropicana), the refillery movement (Package Free, Precycle), growlers, glass-deposit dairy (Straus, Ronnybrook, Manhattan Milk), olive-oil refill bars (We Olive, Oliviers & Co). "Nothing else on the bench has a reorder engine at all" is true and is a commercial argument, not an originality one. Being the only revenue-generating idea in a pool is not the same as being a new idea.

4. THE IMAGE IS THE MOST EXPLOITED ICON AVAILABLE, AND THE COUNTER-ARGUMENT DOES NOT ANSWER THE CHARGE. A VW T1 split-screen on a consumer good: Lego 10220 and 10279, Playmobil, Hot Wheels, Kovap tin toys, Hallmark, a decade of surf and coffee packaging, and an uncountable Etsy tier. Separately, every food truck on earth puts its truck on its merch — Kogi, Wafels & Dinges, Big Gay Ice Cream, and most pointedly Van Leeuwen, a NYC brand born from a truck that puts the truck on everything. The concept's defence is that ours will have the nose the right roundness and the roof the right height. That is a measurement claim answering an originality objection. At 90 mm on curved glass, nobody at the counter can tell, no competitor can be distinguished from us by it, and no jury would call it new. A correct drawing of an over-drawn thing is still an over-drawn thing.

5. "COMMISSION THE PRACTITIONER" IS THIS BRAND'S EXISTING HOUSE METHOD, NOT AN ADDITION TO IT. The Alÿs citation is accurate but it is a citation. Downstream of it: Sign Painters (Levine/Macon, 2012), Better Letters, Barry McGee/Margaret Kilgallen, and in Mexico a fully commercialised rotulismo revival — Victoria, Modelo, Corona, Cuervo, Bimbo, Nike MX and Adidas MX have all commissioned rotulistas, including onto limited-edition bottles. Victoria has run rotulista-illustrated bottle art. And the target vehicle's own flank in every reference frame we hold IS rotulista work. Commissioning a sign painter to paint a Tacombi bus is describing what Tacombi already paid someone to do. The "converts an appropriation charge into a credit line" ethics argument is sound and I do not dispute it; it just is not new, and it is not new HERE least of all.

6. THE COMMISSION IS SELF-DEFEATING AS SPECIFIED. You pay a rotulista for a loaded brush — drop shadow, blend, the wet edge — then photograph the panel and posterise it to five flat separations. Five flats reproduce the DRAWING and throw away the PAINTING, which is the only thing you commissioned that a vector artist could not have supplied for the same money. The credit line next to the ingredients then decorates an object that no longer carries the hand it credits.

7. AN INTERNAL FIGURE DEFECT, IN A PROJECT WHOSE FIRST RULE FORBIDS EXACTLY THIS. "The vehicle carries five inks and this is the one object that can carry all of them" is contradicted by the concept's own colour list four sentences later, which names SEVEN: RED (196,49,36), CREAM (206,208,200), strip yellow (172,144,17), mural RED (159,45,22), ORANGE (163,66,21), GOLD (172,119,24), INK (72,46,6). "Five" is a screen-count and cost decision dressed as a property of the vehicle. (Credit where due: the INK caveat is handled correctly — lid_gen.py does read "INK = (72, 46, 6) # ... measured median (91,59,7) is blur-limited", so the concept's warning is accurate. The "⌀439.6 rim flange — SIXTEEN INCH, NOT FIFTEEN" is a live inference on top of a real ruler, not a watched figure; label it.)

8. ON THE SECOND CHARGE — FLAT-MEASURED-LINE UNDER A NEW LABEL — I MOSTLY ACQUIT IT, THEN CONVICT ITS FALLBACK. Handing the outline to a human and printing what they paint is the only proposal on this bench that genuinely leaves the house aesthetic. But two things drag it back. (a) The FIGURES block lists fifteen dimensions as "design material" for an object on which not one of them will appear; they are there out of habit and do no work. (b) The FIRST STEP — print the true-ortho elevation at 90 mm and wrap it as a paper band — IS the flat measured line, and it is what ships if the $150 one-week commission slips, which commissions do. The pilot is a test of the house style wearing the concept's name.

9. THE MODEL'S CONTRIBUTION IS OVERSTATED AS "DECISIVE." A painter working over an underlay at 90 mm cannot express the difference between a true orthographic projection and a competently perspective-corrected trace of ref_nolita_doorshut.jpg (door shut, the project's own preferred nose-geometry frame). Risk 4 already concedes the painted flank departs from the vehicle's artwork. If the artwork departs anyway, the sub-millimetre correctness of the ground under it is convenience, not necessity. Report it with its ceiling.

10. THE TRADEMARK EXPOSURE IS FILED AS AN AESTHETIC RISK AND IT IS A LEGAL ONE. The concept worries the roundel "cannot be judged at 90 mm." The real question is whether Volkswagen AG will permit the V-over-W and the T1 silhouette on a product offered for sale. VW licenses and defends both. Nominative use on the actual vehicle is one thing; a retail SKU is another. This is a harder blocker than the health-code item — a refusal kills the SKU after screens are cut, whereas the refill law only kills the $6 tier — and it sits fourth on a four-item list.

11. THE ASSEMBLY TEST. I cannot point to one existing object that is all five layers at once — this vehicle, ACL glass, refill tier, rotulista credit, NYC taqueria — so this is not zero. But the honest test is: given the brief "make merch for a taqueria that operates out of a VW bus," is this the first idea or the fortieth? It is roughly the third. It is where a competent brand studio lands in hour one. That is the definition of a sound idea that is not a fresh one, and the owner asked for fresh ones.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. TAKE THE BUS OFF THE BOTTLE. Highest-leverage single change. Print instead the one form on this vehicle that belongs to no one else and appears in no VW merch anywhere: the THREE SERVING APERTURES — 515.5 × 403.0 mm, r 55, equal, three — as a bare ACL device, three rounded rectangles in one or two inks, vehicle absent. It reads as a mark rather than a picture, it is unique to this one converted bus, it is the strongest solid measurement we hold, and it dodges VW's trademark entirely. TRES HUECOS sits on the bench with no object attached; this is its object. Keep the bus for the crate's end panel, where it is furniture rather than the mark.

2. RECONCILE THE INK COUNT OR DROP THE CLAIM. Choose the five, print the five swatches on flint glass, watch them print, and caption the constructed INK as constructed. Until then "five inks because the vehicle carries five" is a slogan and the concept's own list refutes it at seven.

3. IF YOU COMMISSION A PAINTER, REPRODUCE THE PAINT. Either one-colour ACL of the painted line over a single flat ground with a real halftone (brushwork survives), or — far better on originality — have the rotulista paint DIRECTLY onto a short run: 200 hand-painted bottles at $28 is an object with no prior art, that cannot be knocked off, and whose credit line is load-bearing. Ten thousand screened ones sit on the shelf beside Jarritos and lose.

4. ANSWER VISTA HERMOSA ON THE FIRST PAGE, NOT AT ALL. One paragraph: either this SKU lives inside the existing CPG line (in which case relabel the concept honestly as a packaging brief and its novelty claim shrinks to the refill tier), or state precisely what a counter-only, refill-tiered, hand-painted bottle does that a grocery SKU cannot. Unanswered, an informed reader kills this in ten seconds.

5. PROMOTE THE ROUNDEL TO RISK #1 AND MAKE IT A GO/NO-GO. Trademark clearance on the V-over-W and the T1 silhouette for a product for sale, before any screen is cut. Fix 1 makes this moot, which is a further argument for fix 1.

6. RUN THE TEST THAT TELLS YOU WHETHER THE MODEL IS NECESSARY. Pay two rotulistas: one over the true-ortho elevation, one over a perspective-corrected trace of ref_nolita_doorshut.jpg. Photograph both at 90 mm. If nobody can separate them — and I expect nobody can — say so with its ceiling and stop calling the model "decisive." If they CAN, you have earned the claim and it becomes the concept's real headline.

7. FIX THE PILOT SO IT MEASURES THE THING IN DISPUTE. Attach rate tells you hot sauce sells, which is already known. Add the only number that separates this from a paper-labelled sauce and costs nothing to collect: of the twelve bottles sold, how many came back inside four days. Return rate is the concept's entire thesis and the pilot as written does not touch it. Also count how many customers, unprompted, mention the bus versus the refill — if it is the refill, fix 1 is free.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

**OWNER-AND-BRAND — 2/4 — SURVIVES, WITH SURGERY — and one of its four stated risks is incoherent with its own artefact.

On my lens alone (owner and brand fit) this is the most handable object on the bench and the only one with a reorder engine, and its FIRST STEP is the best answer in the whole programme to "why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry": a paper band on an off-the-shelf bottle, a dozen on the counter, four days, a real attach number off real customers. He would engage with that on sight. A crate of 24 on the counter is a merchandising image a non-designer gets instantly, and "¿trae su envase?" is culturally right rather than borrowed.

But it asks a restaurateur to become a hot-sauce manufacturer, it hands his single most-rejected graphic element to a $150 stranger at a size the concept itself says cannot be judged, it credits a cook for a product a co-packer makes, and — the part that decides my score — the concept's contact with the model is one PNG, in a programme whose entire justification is that the model is the ground.

I checked the evidence it leans on and it is softer than stated: I opened IMG_2073.jpeg and looked. The crates are real (that half stands), but I count FIVE red Coca-Cola crate bands, not four, and they are cut by the frame edge so five is a floor, not a count; and the bus in that frame is the GREEN vehicle, which the record classes "NOT the target, geometry only — paint and artwork do not transfer" (rule 11, the rule that killed F99/F100/F140). The returnable economy in the picture is Coca-Cola's supply chain at a Señor Tacombi location, not a Señor Tacombi programme. The brand-continuity argument is real but weaker than "already standing in our own reference frame" claims.

Its danger is intact after every fix I propose — the money, the regulator, the third-party hand and the declared §10.10 departure all stay. Nothing below sands it down; the fixes remove unforced errors, not risk.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE ROUNDEL RISK IS INCOHERENT WITH THE ARTEFACT, IN BOTH DIRECTIONS. Stated risk 4 ends: "the VW roundel is composed out of a 90 mm elevation because at that size it cannot be judged and he has rejected it nine times." A true orthographic SIDE elevation of a T1 CONTAINS NO ROUNDEL — the roundel is on the nose panel. Either (a) the risk is imaginary and the concept has published an unmeasured worry, or (b) the artefact secretly needs a nose-bearing view, which THE MODEL CANNOT DELIVER TODAY: the front and rear ortho cameras clip at 2.4406 m against a 3.132 m bbox top. Un-caught, this is a concept whose keystone asset may not be renderable. And the mitigation is backwards on its own terms: "at that size it cannot be judged" is a defence against the owner's eye, not against a trademark examiner, and it inverts the project's first rule — do not publish what you have not looked at.

2. TRADEMARK: A POSTER IS NOT A PACKAGED GOOD, AND THE CONCEPT TREATS THEM ALIKE. Everything else on the bench is an artwork; this is goods in commerce at $14, in a channel, with a supplier chain. If any nose-bearing view ever enters the artwork, that is a VW roundel screened onto a sold product — the single most reliably enforced merchandise mark in the automotive world. A restaurateur receiving a cease-and-desist over hot sauce is the precise inverse of "spark joy". The concept's own risk register does not mention licensing, and it is the only concept on the bench where licensing is not optional.

3. §10.10 IS NOT THE CONCEPT'S TO DEPART FROM, AND THE HISTORY MAKES THIS THE WORST POSSIBLE PLACE TO TRY. SPEC §10.10's own words: "the bar is that the owner recognises HIS OWN vehicle"; the failure mode it exists to stop is "something plausible and generically 1960s"; and the very row in its table for the "Señor Tacombi" script reads REJECTED BY DONALD. He has since rejected the nose emblem NINE times. This concept's central move is to hand a 90 mm printout to a painter who has never seen the vehicle and put the result on a retail object — an approximation, by a stranger, at unjudgeable scale. The defence "the bar's object is the MODEL; a bottle is a different object" is the CONCEPT'S assertion, made on his behalf, about a rule he asked for by name. Rule 34's exact shape: a requirement inherits its object, and you check that with him, not around him. Note also that the strongest thing about the Alÿs argument — a hand working over a measurement — is precisely what he has rejected nine times when the hand was ours.

4. THE CREDIT LINE BECOMES A LIE AT THE CO-PACKER. Naming the cook on the back panel is the most brand-right detail in the pitch — and stated risk 2 concedes that a shelf-stable salsa is "a scheduled-process, pH-validated, co-packer job, not a kitchen job." So the object credits a person who does not make what is inside it, in a brand whose entire equity is that a person makes your food. He will see that in one reading. The two halves of the concept contradict each other on the same 6 pt column.

5. THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES ~1 % OF THE OBJECT AND 0 % OF ITS RISK. "MODEL: One thing, and it is decisive: A TRUE ORTHOGRAPHIC SIDE ELEVATION." Honest, and damning in this programme. After that PNG, every remaining input is someone else's — the painter's hand, the co-packer's process, the inspector's ruling, the screen printer's set-up. He redirected the project to a programme BUILT ON THE MODEL. This is a programme built beside it. Of the figures listed, the ones that reach the object are the outline's proportions; the counter slab, the brass nosing coefficient, the 65 bobble balls, the rear-arch gaps do nothing on a bottle. A concept that would be ~90 % as good with a good photograph is not the one that justifies 76 revisions.

6. "FIVE INKS, THE VEHICLE'S OWN COUNT" IS A RHETORICAL FIGURE, NOT A COUNT. Its own FIGURES paragraph lists SIX colour references (RED, CREAM, strip yellow, mural RED, ORANGE, GOLD) plus INK (72,46,6) — which it correctly flags as CONSTRUCTED, not measured, against a measured, blur-limited median of (91,59,7). So the object's defining production decision rests on a number the concept has not watched print. And it never says WHICH vehicle's five: the palette given is the RED bus, while the atmosphere and the crate evidence come from the GREEN one, and both wear the same brand.

7. THE DIFFERENTIATING HALF IS THE FRAGILE HALF. Everything that makes this more than "restaurant hot sauce with a bus on it" — the envase, the crate, the $6 refill, the question in the title — sits downstream of one health inspector. The concept says so, and then designs the four-day counter test to measure "how many people ask about the refill", a metric with no value if the answer is no. If the refill dies, what remains is a $14 super-premium bottle (top of a category that clears at $8–12) with a licensing question on it and a credit line that has become untrue.

8. THE CHILDREN'S BRIEF GOES ANOTHER REVISION UNSERVED. He named the audience — F18, "spark joy ... for children", open since rev 44. This object is capsaicin: children cannot buy it, use it, or keep it. Not a kill on its own (nothing says every concept must serve F18), but if this is the one object that ships, the pattern he complained about repeats in a new register.

WHAT I WILL NOT ATTACK, BECAUSE IT IS RIGHT: the four-day paper-band test is the best owner-fit move on the bench; the crate-of-24 fixture is genuinely the cheapest good merchandising in the portfolio; and "commission the practitioner, do not imitate them" is the correct answer to the appropriation charge, provided it is paid and papered.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. SETTLE THE ROUNDEL BY MEASUREMENT, NOT BY SIZE. Render the side ortho, crop the nose region, and state in the risk register that a T1 side elevation contains no roundel — then DELETE the "cannot be judged at 90 mm" sentence, which is a defence against his eye and not against a mark. If any nose-bearing view is ever wanted, two prerequisites go in writing first: fix the front/rear ortho clip (2.4406 m against a 3.132 m bbox) and either licence the roundel or replace it with the Señor Tacombi mark. No object goes to a screen printer with that open.

2. PUT §10.10 TO HIM, AS THE PROJECT'S OWN METHOD REQUIRES. One crop, one mark, multiple choice, with the reference attached: the painted panel photographed beside ref_side.jpg at matched magnification, and the question "on a MERCH object, is a hand-painted departure from the actual flank licensed, or is §10.10 absolute here too?" Do not declare the departure on his behalf. This costs one asking and it de-risks the single largest brand hazard in the pitch.

3. PAPER THE ROTULISTA BEFORE THE BRUSH, AND PAY PROPERLY. Written assignment or exclusive licence for the printed uses, plus the credit, signed before work starts. And raise the fee: $150 for the asset the entire concept hangs on is a tell that undercuts the Alÿs argument it is making. Say what was paid on the sheet — the credit only converts an appropriation charge if the transaction is real.

4. FIX THE BACK PANEL SO IT STAYS TRUE AFTER THE CO-PACKER. "Recipe by [cook], for Señor Tacombi" — never phrasing that implies she bottles it — plus the "manufactured for" line the label law requires anyway. Keep the rotulista credit; it is the best detail on the object.

5. GET THE INSPECTOR ANSWER BEFORE THE COUNTER TEST, NOT AFTER. One email to the local health department this week, in parallel with the four days. Then write BOTH card copies now: the refill version and the exchange version (dirty bottle in, clean filled bottle out), so the story survives either ruling and the test measures demand rather than a mechanism that may be illegal. Add the sanitation labour cost of the exchange fallback to the COST block — it is currently absent and it is the fallback's whole problem.

6. RE-CAPTION THE EVIDENCE HONESTLY OR DROP IT. I looked: at least FIVE Coca-Cola crate bands, cut by the frame edge, so five is a floor; and IMG_2073 is the GREEN vehicle, which this record classes geometry-only with paint and artwork explicitly non-transferring. Write it as: "the same brand, at a location, keeps a supplier's returnable-bottle stack beside a Señor Tacombi combi (IMG_2073, ≥5 crate bands, frame-cut; GREEN vehicle — geometry class)." That sentence is still a good argument and it is one nobody can kill at the desk.

7. COUNT THE INKS OR STOP CALLING IT A COUNT. Either measure the palette on a named, painted window and publish the list with its ceiling, or say plainly: "five screens, a production choice; the vehicle's palette has at least six references and one of them, (72,46,6), is CONSTRUCTED." The current phrasing is the exact defect this project retracts figures for.

8. MAKE THE MODEL EARN ITS PLACE, WHICH IS CHEAP AND MAKES THE CONCEPT UNARGUABLE. Give the painter an AO/line underdrawing off the mesh, not just an outline; put the SHUT LINES and the three aperture corners on the screens as literal registration marks, so mis-registration between the five colours is measured against the vehicle's own geometry; and print one measured dimension on the back panel beside the ingredients. Then the object visibly could not have been made from a photograph, and the model is load-bearing rather than decorative.

9. NAME THE VEHICLE ON THE OBJECT. Red or green — pick one and say why, in one line. The brand has both and the bottle cannot have both.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**DANGER — 1.5/4 — COMPETENT, PROFITABLE, AND DEFANGED. This is the most commercially real thing on the bench and the least at stake. It contains four genuinely dangerous things and it neutralises three of them in the same sentence that raises them; the fourth — the one with a real human on the end of it — it has not noticed at all. What is left is a pitch deck with a compliance section. Risk disclosure is not stakes. dangerIntact: NO.**

* ATTACKS: WHAT IS ACTUALLY DANGEROUS HERE (four live wires, in order of voltage)

1. THE ENVASE RETORNABLE IS A POVERTY TECHNOLOGY, NOT A CHARMING RITUAL. The returnable glass bottle persists in Mexico because it is the cheapest way for a poor household to buy sugar water, and the specific system this concept cites — Coca-Cola's returnable crates — is inseparable from Chiapas water extraction, the highest per-capita Coke consumption on earth, and a diabetes epidemic in the communities that keep the deposit economy alive. Taking that and selling it back to Nolita at $14 a bottle is the sharpest and ugliest move anywhere on this bench. That is a real, hot, publishable-against idea.

2. THE PRICE. $14 for the thing that is free on the table at every taqueria in Mexico, from a company whose entire format is street food priced for Manhattan. That is Tacombi's own standing argument with itself, made into an object.

3. THE PAY RATIO, WHICH IS IN THE CONCEPT'S OWN NUMBERS. $150 to one rotulista for the panel the concept itself calls "the asset the entire concept hangs on", against a landed $1.90–3.00 into $14 retail at 79–86 % gross on a 10k run. That is roughly $110k of gross standing on a $150 hand. The concept prints both figures and never puts them next to each other.

4. THE ONE IT HAS NOT NOTICED, AND IT IS THE BEST THING IN IT. Printing THE COOK'S NAME on a mass-produced retail object sold in New York. Kitchen labour in this trade is substantially undocumented. A name on ten thousand permanent, searchable objects ties a specific individual to a specific employer for ever — an immigration exposure, a retaliation exposure after a wage claim, a personal-safety exposure, and a consent question that is never asked. It is also genuinely radical: almost no restaurant merchandise names the person who cooked. The concept stumbles onto the strongest content it has and handles it as a typographic detail.

HOW IT HAS BEEN SANDED — ONE MOVE, REPEATED

Every dangerous item is raised and disarmed inside the same sentence.
* "it is not a sustainability gesture bolted on — it is already in our own evidence." The crate is not deployed as an argument, it is deployed as absolution.
* "converts an appropriation charge into a credit line next to the ingredients." The concept states its own laundering out loud and files it as a solution.
* "That is Francis Alÿs's move." It is not. THE LIAR, THE COPY OF THE LIAR was about authorship collapsing and the original being lost in the copying — it was a problem made visible, not indemnity purchased. Cited here it is an ethics clause. And it is the house absolution across this whole bench: the identical Alÿs sentence appears in at least five other concepts in CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md. A defence used six times is not a position, it is boilerplate.
* STATED RISK is four compliance items — check with an inspector, write the licence, stated not assumed. Not one of them is a thing the concept is willing to be uncomfortable about. Legal risk is not danger; it is homework.
* The only sentence in the piece with any heat in it is "This is the item in the portfolio that actually pays." The concept's real feeling is for the margin.

THE POLITICAL ANCHOR IS BORROWED, AND UNPAINTED. "Four stacked crates of returnable Coca-Cola bottles stand beside the bus in IMG_2073" is the single fact carrying every political claim in the concept. Grepped across 76 revisions of this record: it appears NOWHERE except in this concept's own text. The only related lines in the tree are t1_shell.py's "Crates, a gas bottle and service kit would be invention" and SURVEY_rev49's incidental "green crate" in a different frame. And IMG_2073.jpeg is the GREEN vehicle — not the red target bus — which the reference set flags in four separate briefs. So the evidence licensing the appeal to "our own reference frame" is (a) never painted or looked at, in a project whose rule 8 exists precisely for that, and (b) on the wrong vehicle. The politics are not just sanded; they are unearned.

WHAT SURVIVES, AND IT IS NOT NOTHING. "A machine for making somebody walk back in" and "the only merch item that physically drags a customer back through the door" are the two unapologised sentences in the piece, and they are honestly ugly — retention design admitted as coercion. Keep them verbatim. And the object itself, a $14 hand-painted bottle of free salsa, carries the class argument whether the prose owns it or not. But as specified it does not land: the 6 pt credit line makes the buyer COMFORTABLE. The bottle as written reads as ethical sourcing, which is the exact opposite of carrying the danger.

ONE SMALL COWARDICE, NAMED. "The VW roundel is composed out of a 90 mm elevation because at that size it cannot be judged." That is using illegibility to slip a mark past an owner who has rejected it nine times. Either it is good enough to be looked at or it is not on the object.
* WOULD FIX IT: SIX CONCRETE PUT-BACKS. Every one is a change to the OBJECT, not to the prose.

1. PRINT THE RATIO ON THE BOTTLE. If the rotulista's name goes on the back, the fee goes beside it, and the retail price goes beside that. One line of 6 pt: PAINTED BY [NAME], COMMISSIONED [DATE], FEE $150 · THIS BOTTLE $14 · REFILL $6. A named fee against a named price is the whole argument, made by the object, with no editorial. If the fee is too embarrassing to print, raise it until it is not — that is the test, and it is cheap. This single line converts the concept from exculpated to honest.

2. DELETE THE ALŸS SENTENCE. Do not cite a defence. Either pay the painter a per-unit royalty — a real printed number — or drop the claim that commissioning resolves anything. A credit is not a settlement.

3. THE COOK'S NAME IS A CONSENT AND SAFETY DECISION, NOT A TYPOGRAPHIC ONE. Written consent, in the person's own language, naming the run size and the permanence, with a right to withdraw and the run dated so the name is bounded. If they decline, PRINT THE ABSENCE: THE COOK ASKED NOT TO BE NAMED. That is a harder, better object than a name, and it makes the concept about who can afford to be visible — which is the danger it currently walks past. Add this to STATED RISK as item one, above the health code, because it is the only risk with a person on the end of it.

4. PUT THE COKE IN THE OBJECT OR TAKE THE CLAIM OUT. If the returnable economy is the argument, the vessel must be the Mexican returnable form — squat, ribbed, deposit-marked, ENVASE RETORNABLE / NO SE VENDE embossed in the glass, not a generic flint squat with a nice print on it. And the crate must be a used one, scuffed, with somebody else's brand still on it, not a new merchandising fixture. Otherwise the reference is a costume. Separately: before the crate claim is repeated anywhere, open IMG_2073, paint the window, look at it, and state that it is the GREEN vehicle.

5. MAKE THE DEPOSIT REAL. A $6 refill at ~82 % margin is a loyalty programme wearing a poverty technology's clothes. Charge an actual refundable deposit on the glass, refund it in cash at the counter, and accept that people who never return have bought the bottle. That costs money, it is the mechanism the concept claims to be honouring, and it is the difference between citing the envase retornable and running one.

6. REPLACE THE PITCH LINE WITH THE SENTENCE THE CONCEPT IS AVOIDING. Cut "This is the item in the portfolio that actually pays" and write instead: this is a fourteen-dollar bottle of the thing that is free on the table, and the refill exists to make you come back. Say that to the owner in the ask. If it cannot survive being said plainly, it should not be made — and if it can, the concept has stakes again and I would score it three times higher.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

### DIEZ Y MEDIA — audit mean 1.75/4

**FEASIBILITY — 2/4 — Makeable object class, unmakeable spec. Nothing here is blocked on the machine — the asset runs in 2.6 s and needs no line pass, no export, no camera fix — but the central image is not in the source, the hand-embroidery cost is off by 3–6× in the direction that re-creates the harm the concept says it priced, the machine version's cost is off by roughly 10×, and the 300-unit hand MOQ is ~6,000 hours of skilled labour. The week-one test is real and cheap; the product behind it has to be redefined before a taller is contacted.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE RING DOES NOT EXIST — this is the hardest hit and it is measured, not argued. I read `lid_gen.py` live. `N_LOBE = 8 # outer scalloped ring` is the 8-lobe scallop INSIDE one head. The ten head centres, `HEADS_UV`, are a scattered field across the board interior: put in a metric frame at BOARD_ASPECT 1.654, their radii about their own centroid run 0.044 to 0.859 (a 19.5× spread, radial CV 0.46). That is a serpentine scatter, not a ring. The half head sits at (1.0050, 0.8460), i.e. at ONE specific place in that scatter, cut by the right strip. So "the roof lid's own flower ring at its measured count... the half head cut by the hem exactly as it is cut by the strip" conflates two different rings and dresses a studio composition as a transfer. What actually transfers is the COUNT (10 + 1), the petal number (8), and DOT_SZ 0.118. The arrangement — the thing the object IS — is invented. This project has a rule for exactly that (rule 8: do not publish a number from a window you have not painted; rule 10: a claim in prose is not a measurement), and the concept's own boast, "not a version of it, THE COUNT OF IT", is the sentence that breaks.

2. THE HAND COST IS WRONG BY 3–6×, AND THE ERROR IS THE ETHICS RISK. Eleven heads, each three nested 8-lobe satin scallops (RED/ORANGE/GOLD) plus 8 pale dots plus disc and centre mark, at ~70–90 mm across on a 450 mm cloth, plus a hand hem: 15–25 hours of skilled work per piece is a conservative estimate. At $16–26 landed that is roughly $1/hour of embroidery. The concept's own risk (2) says commissioning a real taller "triples the unit cost"; $7–10 machine to $16–26 hand is 2.4×, and it arrives there by paying below any defensible rate. Honest arithmetic from a day rate: 15–25 h skilled hand work lands at $50–150 labour alone, before manta, floss, taller overhead, packing, freight, HTS 6302 duty and the taller's margin. Landed $60–110, retail $120–200. Comparable dense polychrome Oaxacan/Chiapas 45 cm servilletas sit in that band. The stated $58–68 retail is under the honest wholesale. A concept whose stated risk is exploitation and whose stated price only closes by exploiting is not a costed concept.

3. 300 UNITS OF HAND EMBROIDERY IS 4,500–7,500 HOURS. At 30 productive hours a week that is a ten-person taller working on nothing else for 15–25 weeks, not "eight to fourteen". A first hand order is 25–50 pieces at 10–14 weeks, and the cash-cycle risk (3) is therefore worse than stated in one direction (you tie up the taller) and better in another (you can't take that much stock anyway).

4. THE MACHINE VERSION IS COSTED LIKE A LEFT-CHEST LOGO AND IT IS A 450 mm ALL-OVER. Estimated stitch count for 11 heads at three scallop rings + 8 dots + disc each: 90k–165k stitches, six colour changes. Commercial run pricing is per-1000-stitches, so that is $60–140 of machine time per piece, not $7–10 — an order of magnitude. Worse, 450 mm exceeds ordinary commercial hoop fields; an all-over at that size needs a jumbo single-head or multiple hoopings with registration error at every join. And 100k+ stitches on lightweight manta puckers without heavy stabiliser, at which point the object is a stiff panel, not the cloth hot tortillas get folded into. The "$32 nice napkin" line does not exist at that spec.

5. THE SIX MEASURED COLOURS ARE ABOUT FOUR IN CLOTH, AND I MEASURED IT. CIE ΔE76 on the stated palette: RED (159,45,22) vs ORANGE (163,66,21) = 10.0; GROUND (55,26,19) vs GROUND_HI (74,38,26) = 8.0; PALE (184,150,76) vs the measured PEACE (174,133,73) = 9.0. Three pairs under ΔE 10, which floss dye lots and 1–2 mm stitch width on textured cotton will swallow. Separately: two of the six are the GROUND — that palette was measured on a dark brown board with a DARKLINE (72,26,16) keyline under every form. On natural cream manta you either stitch a solid dark ground behind eleven heads (multiplying stitch count several-fold) or you drop it, and dropping it drops the very thing that separates RED from ORANGE in the source. And PEACE (198,172,122) is flagged CONSTRUCTED in the file itself. "Six colours drawn from the measured mural palette" is not executable as written.

6. THE ASPECT HONESTY CANNOT TRAVEL TO A SQUARE CLOTH. "Chart in board-normalised (u,v) with separate u and v radii and the ring survives whichever aspect turns out to be right" is not a deliverable. A taller receives a drawing in millimetres. A ±0.09 aspect bar has to be collapsed to one number before the first stitch, and doing so silently is the defect; doing so loudly is fine.

7. CAPABILITIES NOBODY ON THIS TEAM HAS, all outsourceable, none free, and none of it on the machine: embroidery digitizing ($75–250 and a week of back-and-forth), sourcing and contracting a named taller in Spanish with a credit/royalty agreement, textile import (HTS 6302, duty, declarations), and inventory/fulfilment for a physical SKU with per-piece variation. That is several weeks of the founder-of-one's time in a programme whose owner's complaint is "why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship". This ships nothing to the repo at all, which the concept states honestly — but the honesty does not buy back the calendar.

8. TWO RISKS MISSING FROM A LIST THAT CLAIMS TO KEEP ALL OF THEM: colourfastness and laundering (a food-contact cloth washed hot, repeatedly, with saturated reds and oranges on undyed cotton — cheap floss bleeds and the object is ruined on wash one), and drape (the whole cultural argument is that this cloth wraps tortillas and is inherited; a dense embroidered panel does neither).

9. THE FIRST STEP DOES NOT DO WHAT IT SAYS, though it is close. "Print the ring from `lid_gen.py`" — `lid_gen.mural()` draws the WHOLE board (ground mottle, 520 scrolls, stems, leaves, three menu strips) at 4096 × 2476. There is no ring and no isolated-heads mode. You must write it, ~80 lines reusing the file's own head primitive. Cheap — I ran `mural()` end to end in 2.6 s on this box, so raster generation is free — but say what it is. And the test is compromised: a PRINT of a painted mural under a napkin is not embroidery. He will judge scrollwork, keylines and continuous tone, all three of which stitch quantisation removes. A yes on that photograph is not a yes on this object.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. FIX THE CENTRAL CLAIM BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE, THIS WEEK, FREE. Write `lid_ring.py` (~80 lines, reuses lid_gen's head primitive; the full board renders in 2.6 s so this is seconds) and print TWO 450 mm sheets: (a) the ten measured head centres at their actual `HEADS_UV` positions on the board rectangle, with the eleventh cut by the right edge — the honest "count of it", and it is a scatter, which is stranger and better than a ring; (b) a ring composition, correctly labelled an ARRANGEMENT. Those two sheets are the owner's multiple choice. Do not put the word "ring" next to the word "measured" again.

2. RESTATE WHAT TRANSFERS, IN ONE LINE ON THE SWING TAG: ten full heads and one half, eight petals each, one dot per petal, dot radius 0.118 of the head. Count, petal number, dot ratio. Everything else — layout, ground, hem crop — is the studio's, and saying so is cheaper than being caught.

3. RE-SPEC THE PALETTE TO FOUR COLOURS WITH A ΔE FLOOR. Require ΔE76 ≥ 18 between any two floss shades on the actual ground; the measured pairs RED/ORANGE (10.0), GROUND/GROUND_HI (8.0), PALE/PEACE (9.0) all fail and must be merged. Keep the DARKLINE (72,26,16) as one of the four — it is what makes the two reds read apart in the source — and drop PEACE, which the file marks CONSTRUCTED.

4. COST FROM A STITCH COUNT, NOT A FEELING. Digitize once ($75–250, outsourced, ~1 week), read the actual stitch count off the DST, and put THAT number in the concept before quoting anyone. If it lands where I estimate (90k–165k), the 450 mm all-over machine version is dead on price and hoop size — redesign the volume piece to ONE head at 8–15k stitches, which is the object that genuinely lands at $7–10.

5. SPLIT THE LINE PROPERLY. Volume piece: SCREEN-PRINTED manta, three colours, 450 mm — roughly $4–7 landed, no hoop limit, no puckering, folds around hot tortillas, launders, and can carry the full scattered ten-and-a-half at any scale. Counter piece: ONE hand-embroidered head, or the ten-and-a-half at small scale in a corner, 25–50 units, one named taller. Delete the machine-embroidered 450 mm all-over entirely.

6. PRICE THE HAND PIECE FROM THE TALLER'S DAY RATE × MEASURED HOURS, NOT FROM A RETAIL TARGET. Get three quotes with an actual chart in hand and the hours stated. If the arithmetic will not close above a defensible skilled rate, the concept has failed its own risk (2) and should be killed rather than discounted — which is a real result and worth publishing.

7. ADD THE TWO MISSING RISKS AND TEST THEM CHEAPLY: buy one commercial hand-embroidered servilleta this week, wash it hot five times, photograph before and after for bleed and pucker. $40 and no lead time, and it also gives you a physical benchmark for what 450 mm of dense stitch actually feels like folded around food.

8. COMMIT THE ASPECT AND CONFESS IT. Ship the chart in millimetres at BOARD_ASPECT 1.654 and print "board aspect uncertain ±0.09 across three methods" on the tag. That is the programme's own UNCERTAINTY PLATE move applied to a physical object, it costs nothing, and it is the one place where the rigour and the beauty are the same gesture.

9. MAKE THE WEEK-ONE PHOTOGRAPH TEST THE RIGHT THING. Print the ring flat AND mock a stitch version — posterize to four colours, kill the scrollwork, thicken every line to a 1.5 mm minimum feature — and show him both under the tortillas. Ask which is beautiful. A yes on the painted print is not evidence about a cloth, and this project has a rule about instruments that cannot see the defect they are meant to judge.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1/4 — ORIGINALITY VERDICT — DIEZ Y MEDIA does not survive as a standalone concept. It is a vernacular object, in an existing market segment, whose one distinguishing gesture is the default condition of the substrate, and it is a near-duplicate of a sibling in its own pool that already carries it as a $6 line item. The count is real and genuinely non-obvious; everything built around it is not. It should be absorbed, not shipped.

WHAT IS ACTUALLY FRESH, STATED FIRST SO THE ATTACK IS FAIR. Two things, and they are real. (1) The count. Ten full heads, one half head, eight petals not twelve. I read it live: `lid_gen.py` HEADS_UV has ten entries, HEADS_PARTIAL_UV has one at (1.0050, 0.8460), N_LOBE = 8, N_DOT = 8, DOT_SZ = 0.118, and the six colours quoted are the six in source. The claim that a designer redrawing by eye draws twelve and nine is credible and the record's own history supports it. (2) The register. The programme genuinely has no textile and genuinely has nothing warm. The concept is not wrong about the hole it fills.

AND THE CHARGE THE BRIEF ASKED ME TO PRESS — "old flat-measured-line under a new label" — DOES NOT LAND, and I will not pretend it does. There is no line pass, no dimension line, no confession printed on the face, no orthographic elevation, no cream-and-black. It is polychrome, hand-made, and the numbers are deliberately invisible. This is the antidote to that aesthetic, not a relabelling of it. Score it on that alone and it is a 3.

THE MOVE, THOUGH, IS THE PROGRAMME'S OLDEST HABIT WEARING COTTON. Extract one integer from the record; make an object whose entire content is that integer; put the integer in the title. NO LEGIBLE, TWELVE SHADOWS, TWENTY-FOUR SECTIONS, NINE TIMES, THIRTY-SEVEN PER CENT, TRES HUECOS, THE FILAMENT's 115 — and now DIEZ Y MEDIA. The substrate changed. The authorial move did not. The title being the number is the tell: it is the same reflex that produced eleven objects nobody would call beautiful, executed in a medium that flatters it.

THE FATAL FINDING IS INTERNAL AND IT IS VERIFIABLE, NOT A MATTER OF TASTE. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md`'s own audit already says it: "the mural on cloth three times (beautiful-1, free-4-3, free-7-3)". This concept is free-7-3. Its sibling [dream:beautiful-1] DIEZ FLORES Y MEDIA is ranked #1 in the entire fifty-concept pool, and its artefact line reads: "plus a bleached-cotton napkin, 420 × 420 mm, from the same file", costed at "Napkins from the same file on a cotton sateen, ~US$6 each at 100." DIEZ Y MEDIA's whole artefact — a ~450 mm square cloth carrying the roof lid's flower ring, commissioned from a taller, sold with an appropriation-remedy tag — is ALREADY A LINE ITEM INSIDE THE CONCEPT THAT BEAT IT. Same asset, same file, same count, same substrate class, same taller commission, same authorship risk, same "no bottom strip" fact. The titles collide on the same number. A third, [dream:free-4-3] at line 1665, is the same mural on hule again. Presented side by side to the owner, a reader cannot tell why these are three concepts and not one concept with three tiers — and the honest answer is that they are not three concepts.

WORLD PRIOR ART — THE OBJECT. The embroidered servilleta with floral motifs is not a design idea, it is a living vernacular category sold in every mercado in Mexico: Otomí tenango (Tenango de Doria, Hidalgo), San Antonino Castillo Velasco (Oaxaca), Zinacantán and Aguacatenango (Chiapas), the deshilado and pepenado traditions of Puebla and Tlaxcala. The concept's own framing — "the most-kept textile object in the food culture we are working in" — is the argument FOR the object and simultaneously the argument that it is not new. Correct, and correctly not novel.

WORLD PRIOR ART — THE BUSINESS MODEL, AND THIS IS THE CLOSEST HIT. Contemporary designer draws a motif, a named taller in Puebla or Oaxaca stitches it, it ships with a swing tag crediting the maker and stating the departure, retail $50–70, with a machine-embroidered volume tier beneath. That is, line for line, Onora Casa (Mexico City). It is also Someone Somewhere, Caralarga, Taller Maya, and it is the FONART labelling convention. The "swing tag that states the departure" is not a differentiator; naming and crediting the taller is the CATEGORY NORM in that segment, not an ethical innovation. The concept presents its remedy as costly and honest. It is costly. It is not distinguishing.

WORLD PRIOR ART — THE RESTAURANT PRECEDENT. Mexican restaurant groups selling embroidered textiles at the counter is thoroughly worn: Contramar and Cala (Gabriela Cámara) have run artisan textile retail for years, and it is a standard line in every upscale taquería merch programme on both sides of the border. A taquería selling an embroidered floral servilleta is the single most predictable object this brief could produce.

WORLD PRIOR ART — "THE COUNT IS THE CONTENT". Data-into-stitch is a saturated genre: the Tempestry Project, Sam Meech's Punchcard Economy, the whole knitted/embroidered-dataset field, and behind them the post-Albers reading of textile as notation. And in Mexico specifically, motif-count-as-authenticity is already the discourse of Otomí cooperatives defending their repertoire. "We got the count right" is a claim that market already makes.

THE ONE GESTURE THE CONCEPT CALLS "THE WHOLE OBJECT" IS THE DEFAULT CONDITION OF CLOTH. "Every instinct in a studio says centre the ring and complete the flower. We don't." No. Every repeat is cut at the selvedge; every embroidered servilleta crops its border at the hem; the sibling oilcloth concept puts this same artwork into a half-drop repeat where cropping is arithmetic, not authorship. A motif running off a hem reads as normal to every viewer alive. The gesture is invisible AS a gesture, which means the concept's stated centre of gravity carries no perceptual weight at all. Compare the genuinely strange fact sitting unused two lines above it in the same source: THREE strips, left, right and top, and NO BOTTOM STRIP, measured absent twice. Cut on three sides and open on the fourth is odd, is this board's own, and would be legible as a decision. The concept spends its crop on the ordinary asymmetry and leaves the strange one on the floor.

STRIP THE TAG AND IT IS NOT YOURS. The bench's own survivor test, at `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md` line 131: "if the caption is removed, is this still worth owning?" — it lists beautiful-1, free-7-2, beautiful-2, free-5-1 as passing. It does not list free-7-3. Cover the swing tag and you hold a good embroidered floral napkin from a Puebla taller: the taller's object, not the programme's. Nothing on the cloth is the combi. No aperture, no festoon, no red, no cream, no vehicle. The mural is the ONE asset on this bus that is LEAST specific to it — folk flowers are the most generic thing in the reference set, which is exactly why they clear the VW trade-dress problem, and exactly why they carry no identity. The bench's "art school" verdict — "the idea lives in a label you can cut off" — was aimed elsewhere. It lands here.

AND THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES NOTHING, WHICH THE CONCEPT SAYS PLAINLY AND TO ITS CREDIT. But for an originality verdict that honesty is the finding: the one thing this programme uniquely holds — geometry, true orthographic projection, surface-following line, occlusion — is absent. What remains is a rectified photograph of a painted board, which is upstream, one-time, and shared with both siblings. Anyone with that photograph makes this object in an afternoon. That is the operational definition of unoriginal in a programme whose whole premise is that the model gives it something nobody else has.

ONE PROVENANCE HIT ON THE DIFFERENTIATOR ITSELF. The count is the sole originality asset, so its sourcing matters. "k = 8 with power 42.6 against k = 12 at 6.3" does not appear anywhere I can find: repo-wide, every `42.6` is the rake drop (17.75 mm/m × 2.400 m), and `probe_rev35_harmonic.py` yields no match. What exists is `N_LOBE = 8  # outer scalloped ring: k = 8, measured` — a source COMMENT, which CLAUDE.md rule 10 says is not a measurement. Ten heads and one partial ARE structural in `HEADS_UV`/`HEADS_PARTIAL_UV` and are solid. The petal count, the harmonic powers and the "6 of 7 heads individually" are, on what I can reach, unsourced. If the swing tag prints 42.6 and nobody can produce the probe, the one original thing on the object is the one thing that cannot be defended.

CEILING ON THIS AUDIT. I attacked originality only; I did not price it, and the commercial self-critique in the concept is honest and probably right. My prior-art claims about Onora, Contramar, the tenango traditions and the data-embroidery genre are from knowledge, not from a search run in this session — treat them as named leads to verify, not as measurements. What I did verify against the machine: the ten heads, the one partial, N_LOBE/N_DOT = 8, DOT_SZ 0.118, the six colours, the PEACE warning, BOARD_ASPECT 1.654 CI [1.591, 1.703], the absent bottom strip, and the three-way duplication in the bench.**

* ATTACKS: 1. NEAR-DUPLICATE OF ITS OWN POOL'S #1 CONCEPT — decisive, and internal. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md`:239 already flags "the mural on cloth three times (beautiful-1, free-4-3, free-7-3)". This is free-7-3. [dream:beautiful-1] DIEZ FLORES Y MEDIA, ranked 1st of ~50, ships "a bleached-cotton napkin, 420 × 420 mm, from the same file" at "~US$6 each at 100". DIEZ Y MEDIA is that napkin, upgraded. Same artwork, same count, same file, same taller commission, same appropriation remedy. The titles collide on the same number.

2. THE OBJECT IS A VERNACULAR CATEGORY, NOT AN IDEA. The embroidered servilleta is sold in every mercado in Mexico — Otomí tenango (Hidalgo), San Antonino Castillo Velasco (Oaxaca), Zinacantán and Aguacatenango (Chiapas), Puebla/Tlaxcala deshilado and pepenado. The concept's own best argument ("the most-kept textile object in the food culture") is simultaneously the proof it is not new.

3. THE BUSINESS MODEL IS AN OCCUPIED SEGMENT — closest hit. Contemporary designer + named Puebla/Oaxaca taller + swing tag crediting the maker + $50–70 retail + machine tier beneath = Onora Casa, Mexico City, line for line. Also Someone Somewhere, Caralarga, Taller Maya, and the FONART labelling convention. Crediting the taller is the CATEGORY NORM, not an ethical differentiator.

4. RESTAURANT PRECEDENT IS WORN THROUGH. Contramar / Cala (Gabriela Cámara) and effectively every upscale taquería merch programme have sold artisan textiles for years. An embroidered floral servilleta is the single most predictable object this brief could yield.

5. THE ONE GESTURE IT CALLS "THE WHOLE OBJECT" IS THE DEFAULT CONDITION OF CLOTH. Every repeat is cut at the selvedge; every servilleta crops at the hem; the sibling oilcloth puts this same ring into a half-drop where cropping is arithmetic. "Every instinct in a studio says centre the ring" is false. A motif running off a hem reads as normal to everyone, so the concept's stated centre carries zero perceptual weight.

6. IT LEFT THE ACTUALLY STRANGE FACT ON THE FLOOR. Two lines above the crop in the same source: THREE menu strips — left, right, top — and NO BOTTOM STRIP, measured absent twice. Cut on three sides, open on the fourth, is this board's own oddity and would be legible as a decision. The concept spends its crop on the ordinary asymmetry instead.

7. "THE COUNT IS THE CONTENT" IS A SATURATED GENRE. Tempestry Project, Sam Meech's Punchcard Economy, the whole data-into-stitch field, post-Albers textile-as-notation — and in Mexico, motif-count-as-authenticity is already the Otomí cooperatives' own discourse.

8. STRIP THE TAG AND IT IS THE TALLER'S OBJECT, NOT YOURS. The bench's own survivor test (line 131) names beautiful-1, free-7-2, beautiful-2, free-5-1 as passing "remove the caption". It does not name free-7-3. Nothing on the cloth is the combi — no aperture, no festoon, no red, no cream, no vehicle. The mural is the LEAST vehicle-specific asset in the whole reference set; that is why it clears VW trade dress and why it carries no identity.

9. THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES NOTHING, AND HERE THAT IS THE FINDING. The rectification is upstream, one-time, and shared with both siblings. Anyone holding a photograph of the roof board makes this in an afternoon. In a programme whose premise is that the 3D asset gives it what nobody else has, that is the operational definition of unoriginal.

10. THE MOVE IS THE PROGRAMME'S OLDEST HABIT IN COTTON. Extract one integer, build an object whose only content is that integer, put the integer in the title: NO LEGIBLE, TWELVE SHADOWS, TWENTY-FOUR SECTIONS, NINE TIMES, THIRTY-SEVEN PER CENT, TRES HUECOS, THE FILAMENT's 115 — and DIEZ Y MEDIA. Register is fresh; authorship is not.

11. THE DIFFERENTIATOR'S OWN PROVENANCE IS THIN. "k = 8 at power 42.6 against k = 12 at 6.3" is not findable: every `42.6` in the repo is the rake drop (0.017750 × 2.400), and `probe_rev35_harmonic.py` gives no match. What exists is a source COMMENT, `N_LOBE = 8  # k = 8, measured` — rule 10 says a comment is not a measurement. The ten heads and one partial ARE structural and solid; the petal count is not sourced where the concept says it is.

12. NOT GUILTY on the brief's other charge, stated plainly: this is NOT the flat-measured-line aesthetic relabelled. No line pass, no dimension lines, no elevation, no cream-and-black, numbers deliberately invisible. On that count alone it would score 3. The problem is not the skin; it is that the object underneath already exists, three times over, twice inside this repository.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. MERGE IT. Do not run it as a concept. Fold it into [dream:beautiful-1] DIEZ FLORES Y MEDIA as that concept's top tier, and ship ONE thing with three tiers off one file, one taller commission, one authorship remedy, one tag: hule metre-goods at ~$4/m for the tables, machine-embroidered servilleta at $32, hand-embroidered servilleta at $58–68. That is a stronger commercial object than either concept alone, it kills a duplicate the bench has already flagged, and it costs nothing to do — the sibling's own artefact line already contains the napkin.

2. IF IT MUST STAND ALONE, CHANGE THE SUBJECT — FLOWERS ARE NOT THIS VEHICLE. Embroider what only this bus has and what only cloth can do: a servilleta is FOLDED, and the tortillas go inside. Put the roof mural on the outside faces, and the INSIDE of the bus — the galley, the counter, the three 515.5 × 403.0 apertures at r 55 — on the inner faces, so the cloth closed is flowers and the cloth opened is the inside of the combi with the food sitting in it. That is QUE HAY ADENTRO? made physical, it is the one gesture in the whole programme that requires a textile and cannot be a poster, and it puts the vehicle back on an object that currently has no vehicle on it.

3. MAKE THE CROP LEGIBLE, OR DROP IT AS THE HEADLINE. Cutting at a hem is invisible. Two options that are not: (a) hem the cloth on THREE sides in the strip colours and leave the FOURTH raw — the board has left, right and top strips and no bottom strip, measured absent twice, so the cloth's own border becomes the board's border and the asymmetry is a decision anyone can see without a tag; or (b) place the half head ON THE FOLD, so it completes only when the cloth is opened and the tortillas are taken out. Either way the crop stops being the default condition of textiles and becomes authorship.

4. SOURCE THE COUNT BEFORE IT GOES ON A TAG. Write and run the probe that produces the angular harmonic — stacked V profile at 0.95 R over the seven heads, print k=8 and k=12 power, print the per-head count for all seven, and paint the annulus you sampled and look at it (rule 8). If the numbers reproduce, the tag can carry them. If they do not, the eight-petal claim is a source comment and rule 10 says it is not a measurement — and it is the ONLY original thing on the object, so it cannot be left unsourced.

5. RUN THE CAPTION TEST AND SHOW HIM THE RESULT. Photograph the prototype twice, tag on and tag off, and put both in his hand. The bench's own survivor question is "if the caption is removed, is this still worth owning?" If the tag-off photograph is a nice mercado napkin, the concept has failed its own programme's test and should be merged per (1) rather than fixed.

6. CLEAR THE PRIOR ART WITH ONE HOUR AND A NAME, BEFORE A PESO. Look at Onora Casa's current servilleta line, Someone Somewhere, Caralarga and Taller Maya, and put a screenshot of the nearest competitor beside the prototype photograph in the owner's ask. If it is indistinguishable, he should know that before he commissions. Separately and non-negotiably: check that no motif being commissioned belongs to a specific community's protected repertoire — Otomí tenango and Santa María Tlahuitoltepec have both been at the centre of real appropriation disputes, and the defence is not a tag, it is commissioning the tradition's own practitioners to draw it.

7. GIVE THE MODEL A REAL JOB, OR KEEP SAYING NOTHING. The one thing the 3D asset could contribute to a textile and does not: the FOLD. Simulate the cloth folded around a stack of tortillas, place the ring so the half head lands where it will actually be seen, and size the ring to the folded footprint rather than the flat square. That is cheap, it is a genuine dependency, and it converts the concept's honest "NOTHING" into a measured claim. Do not fake it otherwise — the honesty is the concept's best trait and it should survive any fix.

8. KEEP EXACTLY WHAT IS GOOD AND DO NOT SAND IT: the four risks as written, the PEACE warning, the board-normalised (u,v) charting with separate u and v radii against the ±0.09 aspect bar, and the first step — 1:1 print under a plain napkin, hot tortillas, one photograph, the test word BEAUTIFUL. That first step is correct whichever of the above you take, and it costs a sheet of paper.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**OWNER-AND-BRAND — 2.5/4 — SURVIVES, BUT IT IS CURRENTLY POSITIONED TO BE REJECTED FOR THE WRONG REASONS. On pure owner-and-brand fit this is one of the two or three safest objects in the programme: no VW mark and no bus silhouette (so none of the trade-dress exposure that kills GUIRNALDA and half the sticker pool), no children's-safety surface, no fabricated record, and culturally the correct register for a Mexican-hospitality founder — a hand-embroidered servilleta from a named Puebla taller, wrapping his own tortillas, is exactly the object that founder hands a journalist. It passes the rev-77 adversary's own survival test (remove the caption, is it still worth owning), which that adversary says almost nothing else in the pool does. AND ITS CENTRAL FIGURE IS THE STRONGEST OWNER-FIT FACT ANYWHERE IN THIS PROJECT, which I verified rather than took on trust: SPEC §10.97.11 records that at rev 8 he counted NINE heads off the photograph, lid_gen built ten, and at rev 39, shown the board flat, HE HIMSELF ANSWERED TEN and superseded his own count. This is the only artefact in the programme built on a number he personally supplied and personally corrected. He would be proud to hand someone this cloth. BUT AS WRITTEN HE IS HANDED A REASON TO SAY NO ON THE FIRST PAGE, AND IT IS SELF-INFLICTED.**

* ATTACKS: 1.
