IT IS A LINE ITEM OF A BETTER CONCEPT, SPUN OUT UNDER A COLLIDING TITLE. `dream:beautiful-1` DIEZ FLORES Y MEDIA — the hule tablecloth, screen 8.20, which the rev-77 adversary twice named THE SINGLE BEST IDEA IN THE POOL by name — already contains "plus a bleached-cotton napkin, 420 x 420 mm, from the same file." This is that napkin at 450 mm, priced at $58, with an 8–14 week lead. The titles are DIEZ FLORES Y MEDIA and DIEZ Y MEDIA. That is literally the rev-77 [TOP] finding: "THE TITLES COLLIDE... A client presented with this cannot tell which one to buy, and neither can you." A restaurateur shown both in one deck does not see two products, he sees a team that has lost track of its own catalogue, and the cheap answer he gives is "do the tablecloth" — after which the napkin falls out of that file for free and this concept has no independent existence.

2. "WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES — NOTHING" IS THE WORST POSSIBLE SENTENCE FOR THIS OWNER, AND IT IS ALSO NOT TRUE. His live complaint, quoted at the top of the brief, is "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry." Volunteering NOTHING to a man in that mood is not honesty, it is a rhetorical flourish paid for with his goodwill. And it understates the model in the pessimistic direction: the mural survives in ONE photograph at LID_OPEN_DEG 76 deg, foreshortened. The sibling argues it properly — "the model is the only thing that un-tilts it... the board's width is typed nowhere, it is derived from the opening angle." Without that rectification there are no head centroids, no half head at u=1.0050, no in-situ palette over 2,569,748 px, and no ten. The rectification IS the object. Giving it away costs the concept its only answer to "why did I pay for 76 revisions of a 3D model to get a napkin."

3. IT CONCEDES SPEC §10.10 WITHOUT MAKING THE DEFENCE THAT WOULD LET HIM SAY YES. Risk 1 hands him "a real departure from SPEC §10.10's hard bar" and puts it on a swing tag. §10.10 is his own standing requirement, recorded at his explicit request, and its stated test is that HE RECOGNISES HIS OWN VEHICLE. The sibling makes the rule-34 defence out loud — the bar's object is THE MODEL, a printed cloth is a different object — and says the defence must be made to him rather than assumed. This concept skips it and pleads guilty on the product's own tag. Worse, WHAT IT DROPS IS STRUCTURALLY REV 8's REJECTED FAILURE MODE: `lid_gen.py`'s own docstring calls the large calligraphic C-scrolls and paisley hooks the GROUND FILL of the board, and the risk field says "the scrollwork goes and the rosettes stay." §10.10 exists precisely because rev 8 shipped the dominant motif abstracted from the board and he rejected it — "a statistically correct flower pattern does not do that; the actual board does." Keeping the rosettes and dropping the ground fill is that move again, in floss.

4. IT THROWS AWAY THE FOOD, AT A FOOD BRAND. `lid_gen.py` carries five measured vignettes off the strips — two bolillos with green sprigs, a torta with pink meat and garnish, a tall pale glass and an orange, a tostada, a single pink prawn — with PINK/GREEN/CREAMV measured. That is the half of his roof board that is about what he sells. This concept demotes it to "a second colourway costs a sample." A cloth that wraps tortillas and carries no food on it, when the source object carries five, is a decision he will question in one sentence.

5. THE APPROPRIATION RISK IT STATES IS THE SECOND-WORST ONE. Risk 2 covers offshore stitching of a folk idiom, which is real. It does not mention that THE MURAL WAS PAINTED BY SOMEONE IN PLAYA DEL CARMEN WHOSE NAME IS NOWHERE IN THIS REPOSITORY — the sibling states exactly that, and this one drops it. The brand-damage shape is specific and it is the one a journalist writes: a New York taqueria sells a $58 cloth stitched by a credited Oaxacan taller, reproducing an uncredited Mexican painter's work. Crediting one maker and erasing the other is worse optics than crediting neither, and for a brand whose entire equity is Mexican provenance that is a live hazard, not a footnote.

6. THE HALF HEAD IS THE ONE THING THE PRODUCTION CHAIN WILL SILENTLY FIX. The whole object rests on a flower cut by the hem. Hand embroiderers complete motifs — that is the craft. You are commissioning a named taller, 8–14 weeks, every piece different, and the single feature the concept cannot lose is the one a skilled maker will read as your drafting error and correct as a courtesy. Nothing in the concept protects it. And at the table the uninitiated reading is not "measured," it is "misprinted" — a defect restaurateurs are trained by twenty years of suppliers to reject on sight.

7. IT PUBLISHES HEAD 10 WITHOUT ITS OWN STATED CEILING. The concept carries the board-aspect bar of ±0.09 scrupulously, then quotes ten heads flat. SPEC §10.97.11's detector reads C1 positive 10/10 and states its ceiling in the same breath: "the separation is +0.42 and thin, DRIVEN ENTIRELY BY HEAD 10," worst 0.234 R at "the head behind the palm fronds." The tenth head — the one the title is about — is the weakest detection in the set. He answered TEN at rev 39 so the count is his and it stands, but a concept whose whole claim is THE COUNT OF IT must carry that ceiling onto the tag, not only the aspect bar.

8. IT DOES NOT SERVE HIS ONE NAMED AUDIENCE, AND IT KNOWS. His only stated audience brief is children at the restaurant, "spark joy... for children," attached to F18, the register's oldest live row, open since rev 44. A $58 hand-embroidered napkin is the furthest point in the entire pool from that brief. Risk 4 admits it sells to well under one per cent of tickets. Not disqualifying — a counter needs an aspirational piece — but it means this cannot be the thing he is shown FIRST, and its own first-step section proposes exactly that.
* WOULD FIX IT: Six concrete changes, all inside a week, none of which touch the object itself.

1. MERGE IT INTO `dream:beautiful-1` AND RENAME IT. Present ONE textile programme off ONE `lid_gen.py` file — hule tablecloth as the room, servilleta as the retail piece — with a single first step (one 600 mm printed swatch and one napkin mock, same shoot, same afternoon). Kill the near-identical title: LA SERVILLETA DE DIEZ Y MEDIA, or drop the numeral and call it MANTA DE DIEZ. He must be able to tell the two apart across a table.

2. REWRITE THE MODEL PARAGRAPH TO WHAT IS ACTUALLY TRUE — not NOTHING. "The mural survives in one photograph, seen at 76 degrees and foreshortened. Copy it by eye and you copy a trapezoid. The model un-tilts it: that is where the ten heads, the half head at u = 1.0050, the separate u and v radii and the 2,569,748-pixel palette come from. Everything on this cloth is downstream of the mesh; none of it shows." Checkable, honest, and it is the sentence that answers "why did I pay for the model."

3. MAKE THE RULE-34 DEFENCE EXPLICITLY, THEN LET HIM RULE. One line: §10.10's object is THE MODEL; a stitched cloth is a derivative object that OMITS rather than redraws. Then put the omission to him as multiple choice with a crop, per his own asking protocol — (a) rosettes only, ground plain; (b) rosettes plus the C-scrolls and paisley hooks simplified to two floss weights; (c) do not stitch it, print it. Do not decide his §10.10 departure for him and then confess it on a tag.

4. PUT THE FOOD IN THE FIRST SAMPLE, NOT IN A FUTURE COLOURWAY. One vignette — the prawn or the torta — stitched into a corner in PINK (206,118,124) / GREEN (96,128,54) / CREAMV (232,224,198). One extra colour block on one sample, and it turns a floral napkin into HIS napkin, at a restaurant.

5. FIX THE CREDIT LINE BEFORE ANY QUOTE GOES OUT. Two names on the tag or none: the taller, and the mural's painter. Budget the search for the painter as a real line item — Playa del Carmen, the 2006 conversion, ask the founder, he was there. If the name cannot be recovered, print that instead: THE PAINTER OF THIS BOARD IS NOT RECORDED. WE ARE LOOKING FOR THEM. That converts the sharpest brand hazard into the brand's best paragraph, and it is the house move already — "it cannot be recovered from what we hold" is a real result.

6. PROTECT THE HALF HEAD IN THE CONTRACT, AND TURN THE TAG AROUND. Write DO NOT COMPLETE THE ELEVENTH FLOWER into the stitch chart and the purchase order in Spanish, brief the taller in person on why, and put the reason on the tag warmly — "on the roof of the bus a menu strip cuts one flower in half; we did not straighten it" — with head 10's stated ceiling carried beside the count. Re-cut the tag from a confession into a provenance card: the count he gave, the taller's name, the painter's name. Nothing on it apologises. And do not lead the deck with this — lead with the object aimed at his named audience and let this sit second, as the counter piece, which is what it is and what it is good at.

**DANGER — 1.5/4 — COMPETENT, GROUNDED, AND ALMOST ENTIRELY SAFE. The figures check out live — `lid_gen.py` carries HEADS_UV with ten entries and `N_FLOWERS = len(HEADS_UV)  # 10 full heads`, HEADS_PARTIAL_UV with exactly one `(1.0050, 0.8460)` half head cut by the right strip, `N_LOBE = 8` and `N_DOT = 8` both commented "measured", `DOT_SZ = 0.118`, `# NO bottom strip -- measured absent, twice`, and PEACE flagged as the disc-plus-mark mean rather than the mark. So the rigour is real and my low score is NOT a grounding objection. It is a stakes objection. The artefact is a pretty flowered cotton napkin. There is no register in which that object can offend, indict, embarrass, mourn or cost anybody anything. The concept knows this and pre-empts it — "this is beautiful first" — but pre-empting a charge is not the same as having content. The one thing genuinely at stake, the mutilated flower running off the hem, survives on the page and is worth something. Everything else that could have been dangerous has been converted into a well-run risk register: four risks, each named in capitals, each already solved in the sentence that names it. That is the failure mode this lens exists to catch. A risk you have already mitigated in the same paragraph is a credential, not a risk.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE PAINTER IS MISSING, AND THE PROGRAMME'S OWN RECORD PROVES IT WAS DROPPED ON PURPOSE. This is the sharpest thing in the audit. The mural is not a "cultural motif" and it is not the vehicle's geometry — it is somebody's hand-painting on somebody's roof lid, made by a living folk painter or rotulista whose name Tacombi almost certainly knows. `DESIGN_PROGRAM_rev77.md:107`, on this concept's own ancestor, reads: "Pay the painter or commission a taller — that cost is not negotiable and it is small." The concept as written took the second branch and silently deleted the first. It credits the STITCHER by name and never once mentions the PERSON WHOSE FLOWER IT IS. That is not an oversight, it is the whole discomfort of the object, and it has been sanded to a gleam. And it is worse than generic appropriation: the concept's method is to REPLACE the painter with a measurement. "A designer redrawing this by eye draws twelve petals and nine heads — we counted eight and ten." The boast is that the machine out-observed the human. What it actually did was take an uncredited artist's composition, count it, and sell the count. The programme has a whole sibling concept — ROTULISTA — about the reverence owed to the signwriter's hand. Here the hand is erased and the erasure is presented as accuracy.

2. RISK (2) IS NAMED IN CAPITALS AND THEN SHIPPED ANYWAY, ONE PARAGRAPH LATER, WITHOUT THE OBJECTION BEING RE-APPLIED. The concept states: "IF WE DRAW FOLK EMBROIDERY OURSELVES AND HAVE IT STITCHED CHEAPLY OFFSHORE, THIS IS EXACTLY THE APPROPRIATION THE FIELD CALLS OUT." Then it proposes a machine-embroidered volume SKU at $32 — drawn by us, stitched cheaply, no taller, no name — and says "Run BOTH", and calls the objection nothing worse than "a considerably worse object." The $32 piece IS the case the concept forbade. It is also the SKU that actually turns; the concept says so itself. So the danger has not been retired, it has been moved onto the line that ships and left un-argued. The hand version discharges the ethics; the machine version collects the revenue. That structure has a name and the concept is one sentence away from having to write it down.

3. THE TALLER'S CREDIT IS DOING COMMERCIAL WORK AND THE CONCEPT PRESENTS IT AS PURE HONESTY. "Commissioning a real taller and crediting them by name is the only honest version" — true, and also the exact mechanism that makes a $58 napkin saleable as authentic to a Manhattan customer. The name on the swing tag is the authentication. The concept never admits that the credit is simultaneously the marketing, which is the uncomfortable double function at the centre of every ethically-sourced craft product on earth. Unacknowledged, it reads as virtue. Acknowledged, on the tag, it would be one of the few genuinely brave objects in the bench.

4. THE HALF HEAD IS THE ONE LIVE SPLINTER AND THE CONCEPT UNDER-USES IT. A napkin whose flower is chopped by the hem will be read as a mis-registration by the buyer, by the buyer's mother, and — this is the part the concept does not see — BY THE TALLER, whose craft judgment will say complete the ring and who will be asked to execute what their own eye calls an error, so that a swing tag can explain to a customer why it is not one. That is a real, in-production, person-to-person conflict with an actual power gradient in it: the foreign commissioner overriding the maker's judgment about the maker's own tradition, in the name of fidelity to a photograph. The concept frames it instead as studio courage — "Every instinct in a studio says centre the ring. We don't." Every instinct in the TALLER says it too, and they are the ones who have to stitch it.

5. THE FOOD REGISTER IS CLAIMED AND NOT SPENT. The register line says "food-and-hand-off". The object's actual life is grease, red salsa, a floss bleed at the first hot wash, and a stain that will not come out of natural manta. The concept describes a textile that is "washed, folded, kept, inherited" and never once looks at what it looks like after that. PAPEL DE GRASA — in the same shortlist — is built entirely on grease changing the material. This concept has the identical material fact available to it and declines to touch it, which leaves the artefact in its pristine catalogue state forever. A napkin that cannot be ruined on the page is a picture of a napkin.

6. THE STRONGEST SENTENCE IN THE CONCEPT IS BURIED IN THE RISK SECTION. "It sits on the counter to make the $14 bottle look like nothing and to be photographed." That is honest, unflattering, and true, and it is the only line here with any edge at all. It is filed under commercial risk, where the owner will read it as diligence. On the swing tag it would be an object. In the risk register it is a memo.

7. STAKES TEST, PLAINLY. Ask what this can cost anyone. Not money — the cash-cycle and sell-out risks are ordinary retail. Ask: what can it cost in reputation, in comfort, in truth. The answer as written is nothing, because the appropriation charge is answered before it lands and the authorship question is not asked. Against NO LEGIBLE (a poster indicting the project's own failures to read), THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE (every dimension confessing its provenance) or PAPEL DE GRASA (the object destroyed by being used), this is the only shortlist-grade proposal where nothing is risked by making it. It is well made. It is not dangerous. A programme buying this is buying its own comfort back.
* WOULD FIX IT: Six concrete restorations, in order of how much danger each puts back.

1. PUT THE PAINTER ON THE TAG, FIRST LINE, ABOVE THE TALLER AND ABOVE THE MEASUREMENT. Ask Tacombi who painted the roof lid. If there is a name, it goes first and they are paid — the programme's own record already ruled that cost "not negotiable and it is small". If the name cannot be recovered, the tag must say so in those words: "We do not know who painted this flower. We counted it: ten heads and one half, eight petals, eight dots." That single admission converts the object from a souvenir of the project's competence into a confession of an erasure, and it is the only version of this napkin with anything at stake. It also stops being pretty in the safe way and starts being sad in the useful way.

2. RESOLVE THE $32 SKU OR PRINT THE CONTRADICTION. Three permissible endings and no fourth: (a) kill the machine version; (b) the machine version carries the same tag, the same half head, and the same painter's name, and a share goes to the same people; or (c) ship it and print on it, verbatim, the sentence the concept already wrote — "this one was drawn by us and stitched cheaply offshore, which is the thing we said we would not do." Any of the three is braver than "run BOTH".

3. LET THE TALLER COUNT. Commission two: the measured ring at ten-and-a-half, and the same taller's ring stitched at THEIR count, whatever it comes out — nine, eleven, twelve petals. Sell them side by side, both counts on the tag, no hierarchy stated. Then the object is about who is entitled to count somebody's flowers, which is the political content this concept keeps walking past. It costs one extra sample.

4. MOVE THE CUT TO THE EATING EDGE. Right now the half head is cut by "the hem". Put it where the hand grips the cloth to fold the tortillas, so the amputated flower is the part that gets held, greased and stained first. That is the difference between a design decision and a wound.

5. SPEND THE FOOD. Photograph and sell the object in its used state — one stained piece on the counter under glass, or a second-season line of the returned ones, or simply a tag line that says the red mark is the finish. State what happens to floss dye at 60 °C and put it on the tag rather than in a footnote. A textile object whose ruin is not designed is not a food object; it is a print.

6. MOVE THE PRICE SENTENCE FROM THE RISK REGISTER TO THE PRODUCT. "This napkin costs more than four of the tacos it holds" belongs on the swing tag, at the counter, where the person deciding can read it. It is the concept's best sentence and it is currently addressed to the owner instead of to the customer.

Do 1 and 2 and this goes from 1.5 to a genuine 3+. Do 1, 2 and 3 and it becomes the only concept in the bench that risks something on behalf of a person who is not us. Leave it as written and it is an extremely well-measured tea towel.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

### 515.5 × 403 — audit mean 1.75/4

**FEASIBILITY — 3/4 — FEASIBLE TO BUILD, BUT ITS DIFFERENTIATOR IS NOT.

I verified every load-bearing figure against source before attacking. All of them hold: BAY_W 0.5155 and BAY_R 0.0550 and Z_HEAD 1.7750 − Z_SILL 1.3720 = 403.0 mm (t1_shell.py); CNT_ZT/CNT_ZB 1.2540/1.1470 = 107 mm and CNT_Y_IN/CNT_Y_OUT 0.8450/1.1660 = 321 mm and CNT_NOSE_F 0.1860 (t1_detail.py, with its "113 columns" provenance and the "gives 0.191" second-frame corroboration both present in the source comments); CNT_X1 = X_TAIL −1.873 − CNT_OVERHANG 0.3150 = −2.188, so the run is 3.106 m; RAKE_DZDX 0.017750 with RAKE_Z0 0.047925. I recomputed the rake independently: counter top 1189.8 mm at the front, 1244.9 mm at the tail, rise 55.1 mm, tilt over a 900 mm prop 16.0 mm. It reproduces to 0.1 mm. The cream is CREAM = sRGB(206,208,200) = #CED0C8 in t1_mats.py. This concept did not invent a number, which on this bench is worth saying.

WHAT IS GENUINELY STRONG, ON MY LENS ONLY. This is the only concept on the bench with ZERO machine dependency, and that is not a small thing on a 4-core box at 5 min a frame. It needs no render, no line pass, no glTF/OBJ export, no fix to the clipped front camera, no render queue, no large-format chain, and it does not touch what F191 and F193 gate. It needs no photographer flown in, no bus you cannot reach, no factory minimum — cards are short-run digital, materials are one sheet. The FIRST STEP is the best kill-gate I have seen here: cardboard, a phone, twelve frames, seven days, effectively $0. Under $400 in materials is roughly right (one 12 mm birch sheet $60–110, brass strip $20–40, matched litre $30–60), though "half a day on a router table" assumes you own or can cheaply book one — a one-off CNC profile cut usually meets a shop minimum of $80–250, so call it $500–650 all-in before the shoot, not $400.

WHERE IT BREAKS, AND IT BREAKS AT THE CROWN JEWEL.

1. THE 16.0 mm TILT HAS NO DATUM IN USE, AND IT IS INSIDE THE ARTEFACT'S OWN TOLERANCE. The rake is a tilt RELATIVE TO GROUND — it exists because the bus sits nose-down on its suspension, and step 8b shears it in per-vertex. A 900 × 700 board held up in a doorway has no established relationship to the ground. For 1.017° of lip tilt to be readable, the board itself must be levelled to considerably better than 1° on every frame — which requires a spirit level and a rigid stand, and contradicts "hold it up in the doorway." Worse: the signal and the most likely error are THE SAME QUANTITY. A viewer cannot distinguish "lip tilted 1.02° on a level board" from "board tilted 1.02°." That is a rule-6 tautology in physical form. And 12 mm birch ply, 900 mm span, painted one face and raw the other, living in a humid kitchen, will cup by several mm and can exceed 16 mm over a season. The detail the concept says "separates this from a nice window" is below the noise floor of its own material and has no reference surface. This is my central finding.

2. THE LIP IS UNSPECIFIED AS HOLLOW, AND SOLID IT IS UNLIFTABLE. 900 × 321 × 107 mm of solid birch is 21.0 kg; the board is another 5.1 kg. A fabricator reading the spec as written builds a ~26 kg object that one person cannot hold up in a doorway during service. The concept's own shooting method assumes a prop that can be handled.

3. THE SPEC SAYS ONE CREAM AND THE MODEL HAS TWO. The flank is CREAM sRGB(206,208,200) = #CED0C8; the counter carries COUNTERCREAM (0.7350, 0.7150, 0.6600) ≈ sRGB(223,220,212) ≈ #DFDCD4 — visibly lighter and warmer. The prop has both surfaces on it and the spec names one colour. A fabricator cannot resolve that.

4. THE FIRST STEP TESTS THE REGISTER, NOT THE THESIS. It deliberately drops the lip, the brass, the rake and the paint — everything the model contributes. If the twelve frames come back better, that proves a hole is a good frame, which no measurement gates and which the model did not supply. The kill-gate cannot confirm the concept's distinguishing claim; it can only confirm that shooting through an aperture is a good idea.

5. THE CRITICAL PATH RUNS THROUGH SOMEONE ELSE'S DINNER SERVICE. First step is 7 days, but the finished artefact needs fabrication (1–2 wks incl. CNC queue), venue permission and scheduling (unbounded, third party), a service shoot, then card print (1–2 wks): realistically 6–10 weeks, and the long pole is not under the team's control. Against an owner who says "we need to finally present something" and "why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry," this is the one concept here that CANNOT be compressed by working harder. Compare the lotería deck, which is entirely in-house.

6. RISK 1'S RULE COLLIDES WITH THE KITCHEN. "Shot during service, not after" is the concept's only defence against generic food photography — and it is also the thing a venue's insurer and many health codes will refuse: an unsealed porous 26 kg board held over a live griddle line. The realistic compromise is shooting at the service window or after hours, which is exactly what Risk 1 says would mean the concept has failed. That conflict is unresolved.

7. MINOR BUT REAL: "cut from the mesh's own outline" and "a true 55 mm corner radius" are two different files. T.rrect builds the bay corner at seg=8, an 8-chord polygon 0.265 mm off a true arc. Say which the DXF is.

8. THE MODEL-DEPENDENCE CLAIM IS OVERSTATED. "The model is the cutting file, and without it this is decoration" — the aperture is four numbers (515.5, 403.0, r55, ×3) and the lip is four more. That is a napkin, not a cutting file. Of the eight figures, seven are obtainable with a tape measure on a bus the brand owns. Only the rake is uniquely model-derived — and the rake is the item I just argued is unobservable in the prop. Under attack, the model's exclusive contribution collapses to a number the artefact cannot carry.**

* ATTACKS: (1) The 16.0 mm rake tilt — the one thing claimed to separate this from a nice window — has no datum once the board leaves the vehicle, and at 1.017° it sits inside the cupping and levelling error of a 900 mm one-face-painted birch panel in a kitchen. Signal and dominant error are the same quantity. (2) The 321 × 107 mm counter lip is not specified hollow: solid birch it is 21.0 kg, plus 5.1 kg of board, giving a ~26 kg prop that cannot be held up in a doorway — which is the concept's own shooting method. (3) The spec names one cream; the model carries two, CREAM #CED0C8 for the flank and COUNTERCREAM ≈ #DFDCD4 for the counter, both on this prop. Unresolvable by a fabricator. (4) The seven-day kill-gate strips the lip, brass, rake and paint, so it tests whether a hole is a good frame — a question the model does not answer — and cannot confirm the thesis it is offered as a test of. (5) The critical path is a third party's live service; 6–10 weeks realistic, uncompressible, against an explicitly impatient owner, where the in-house print concepts are not. (6) Risk 1's "during service, not after" is in direct conflict with putting an unsealed 26 kg wooden board on a live griddle line — venue and health-code reality forces the compromise the concept says means failure. (7) "Cut from the mesh's own outline" vs "a true 55 mm radius": T.rrect uses seg=8, an 8-chord polygon 0.265 mm off arc — two different DXFs. (8) "The model is the cutting file, and without it this is decoration" is inflated: eight numbers, seven of them tape-measurable on a bus the brand owns; the only exclusively-model figure is the rake, i.e. the one the artefact cannot hold. (9) Cost is light by one line — a one-off CNC profile typically meets an $80–250 shop minimum, and a 3.57:1 custom-trim 4/1 card on 350 gsm is nearer $120–200 per 100 than $80; call it $500–650 before the shoot. (10) "Never photograph this brand through anything else" is a multi-year governance commitment across staff the program cannot enforce and the owner has not ruled on; the "compounding return" is claimed on exactly that unfunded basis.
* WOULD FIX IT: 1. GIVE THE TILT A DATUM OR DELETE IT. Either build the instrument floor-standing — a machined flat base edge that sits on the floor, so the board's relation to ground is fixed by construction, with "LEVEL TO GROUND · LIP RISES 17.75 mm/m" stencilled beside the reveal-depth stencil — or cut the tilt from the spec and stop claiming it is what separates this from a nice window. If you keep it, measure the as-cut tolerance on the actual part and stencil that beside it; do not stencil a tolerance you have not watched print.

2. SPECIFY THE LIP AS A HOLLOW TORSION BOX. 12 mm skins over a 6 mm rib core, target ≤6 kg all-up, with a 3/8" threaded insert on the back for a C-stand spigot. Price the stand into the build. This also fixes the levelling problem in fix 1 for free.

3. PUT BOTH CREAMS ON THE SPEC SHEET WITH THEIR SOURCE LINES — flank CREAM (0.6172, 0.6308, 0.5776) = #CED0C8; counter COUNTERCREAM (0.7350, 0.7150, 0.6600) ≈ #DFDCD4, both t1_mats.py — or state in writing that the prop uses one deliberately and which.

4. MAKE THE FIRST STEP TEST THE THESIS. Cut TWO cardboard apertures: the measured 515.5 × 403.0 r55, and a same-area control at roughly 470 × 440 r20. Twelve frames through each, same service, same camera, unlabelled. If the owner cannot pick the measured one, the measurement is doing no work and this is a nice window — kill it there, for the price of a second sheet of cardboard, instead of after fabrication.

5. SEAL BOTH FACES before it enters a kitchen, specify a wipeable finish, and get the venue's written sign-off on a prop over the line BEFORE cutting ply. Resolve the during-service/after-hours conflict in writing at that same moment, because it decides whether the concept can exist.

6. DECOUPLE THE SCHEDULE SO THE OWNER SEES SOMETHING IN WEEK TWO. Ship the instrument itself as the first deliverable — the object photographed alone with its stencilled spec, one afternoon, fully in-house and under your control — and date the service shoot separately. That converts an uncompressible third-party dependency into a second milestone instead of the only one.

7. DE-SCOPE THE ABSOLUTISM. "The frame for the dish series" or "for this campaign" is enforceable and ownable; "never photograph this brand through anything else" is neither, and the owner has not ruled on it. Drop the compounding-return claim or fund it with a stated commitment he has actually given.

8. SAY WHICH OUTLINE THE DXF IS — a true 55 mm arc, or the mesh's seg=8 polyline (0.265 mm off). One line on the spec sheet.

9. RESTATE THE MODEL'S CONTRIBUTION HONESTLY: eight dimensions and one colour, of which the rake is the only one no photograph and no tape measure would have given. That is still a real contribution and it is worth stating at its true strength rather than as "the model is the cutting file."
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1.5/4 — DERIVATIVE IN ITS CORE MOVE, HONEST IN ITS NUMBERS. The figures are real and correctly transcribed — I checked all of them. But "build one physical aperture and never photograph the brand through anything else" is Irving Penn's corner with a taco in it, in a food category where shooting through the service hatch became the single most-replicated configuration of the decade in 2020-21. The claimed differentiator — the 17.75 mm/m rake — resolves to 1.017° across the prop, which is invisible in any single frame and reads as a build error to anyone who notices it. Strip the invisible detail and the caption, and the visible artefact is a cream rounded rectangle with a brass line: the most generic object in contemporary food photography. It is also the Uncertainty Plate's epistemic-confession gesture (Mel Bochner, 1969) transposed onto plywood.**

* ATTACKS: ATTACK 1 — THE CONSTANT-INSTRUMENT CONCEIT IS ONE OF THE MOST CANONICAL IDEAS IN PHOTOGRAPHY, AND THE CONCEPT DOES NOT KNOW IT.
"Build one physical constraint, photograph everything through it, the instrument is the constant and the subject is the variable" is Irving Penn's corner (1948) — two studio flats at an acute angle, every sitter jammed into the same wedge — and Penn's portable daylight tent in Worlds in a Small Room (1974), where the constant is the tent and the variable is the world. It is Avedon's white seamless in In the American West. It is, most exactly, the Bechers' typology protocol: one lens, one elevation, one light, and the invariance IS the work. Ruscha's Twentysix Gasoline Stations is the same instrument argument. The concept's own bench already contains THE SAME CAMERA, TWENTY YEARS LATER, which is Nicholas Nixon's Brown Sisters, i.e. the same idea in the time axis. This is not adjacency the author declared — they declared adjacency to THE PASS, LA VENTANA, APERTURA, BODEGÓN, COUNTER HEIGHT and THE BRASS LINE, all in-house. They did not declare the prior art outside the building, which is far heavier.

ATTACK 2 — "SHOT THROUGH THE SERVICE HATCH" IS THE MOST PHOTOGRAPHED FOOD-RETAIL CONFIGURATION OF THE LAST DECADE, AND IT HAS A DATE.
In 2020-21 essentially every restaurant on earth cut a takeaway window, and the hatch-framed hand-off became the default image of the period — a global, involuntary, unpaid campaign. Before that it was already the house device of the Miami ventanita (La Carreta, Versailles: the window IS the brand), of the whole hole-in-the-wall genre, and of Mexican food media specifically — Las Crónicas del Taco leans on the pass/window frame constantly, as does Chef's Table and the entire "the pass" documentary vocabulary. A plate coming through a rounded-corner opening, a forearm, a fixed camera: I can find you two hundred accounts running exactly this frame this week. The concept proposes as a brand-defining constant the single most common frame in its own category.

ATTACK 3 — THE CLIENT ALREADY OWNS THE OBJECT. THIS IS A REPLICA OF THE THING IT IS BRANDING.
Tacombi's brand is a combi with three holes in its flank. The concept's proposal is to build a plywood copy of one of those holes and shoot through the copy. The stated justification is that the hole becomes the constant — but the real hole is already the constant, is already permanent, is already 1:1, and has the entire vehicle attached to it. "Photograph the brand through its own serving hatch" is not a concept; it is what the business does every day by existing. The prop's only advantage over the real aperture is portability into a studio, which is precisely the move that produces the generic food shoot the risk section fears. And 1:1 partial-VW-bus fixtures are themselves a global commodity cliché — the T1 nose mounted as a bar front, the split-screen half-bus reception desk, sold off the shelf by the container-load. This concept steps sideways from the most cloned VW prop into an adjacent one.

ATTACK 4 — THE DIFFERENTIATOR IS INVISIBLE, AND WHERE IT IS VISIBLE IT READS AS INCOMPETENCE.
The author names one thing as separating this from a nice window: the rake. Measured out: 900 × 0.01775 = 15.97 mm, which is atan(0.01775) = 1.017°. One degree. In a photograph shot through a 515.5 × 403 aperture with a hand and a plate in it, one degree of cant on a lip is (a) below the threshold at which any viewer perceives intent, and (b) indistinguishable from a bench that was not squared. The sentence "no fabricator would guess and no photograph would ever have told them" is true and irrelevant: nothing the audience cannot perceive can differentiate an artefact for the audience. This is the project's own rule 8 inverted — you must not publish a number from a window you have not painted and looked at; here you must not claim a differentiator you have not rendered and looked at. Worse: RAKE_DZDX with RIDE_DROP is the vehicle's STANCE — suspension state, load, tyre pressure — not a property of the counter. Freezing 16 mm of a pose into permanent plywood is rule 7 exactly: a guard written against a pose encodes that pose. Load the real bus differently and the prop is wrong forever.

ATTACK 5 — THE VISIBLE ARTEFACT IS A ROUNDED RECTANGLE. THE ORIGINALITY LIVES ENTIRELY IN THE CAPTION.
515.5 × 403.0 is aspect 1.279 with r 55 — a slightly-wide rounded rectangle in warm off-white with a thin brass line. Nothing about that shape says VW T1, says combi, says 1963, says Mexico, or says this vehicle rather than any vehicle. Delete the wall text and no viewer on earth recovers a bus. Compare the bench's own TRES HUECOS, where THREE equal holes in a row at that spacing is at least a rhythm somebody might learn to recognise. This concept is TRES HUECOS minus two holes plus a router table, and the author half-concedes it — "this is the hole, built" — as though fabrication were the differentiating idea. Fabrication is production. The graphic idea is TRES HUECOS's.

ATTACK 6 — THE OLD AESTHETIC UNDER A NEW LABEL: YES, ON THE EPISTEMIC GESTURE, WHICH IS THE BENCH'S REAL TIC.
The register genuinely shifts (photographic, people, food) and I will credit that below. But the *conceptual* move is unchanged. "The reveal depth is stencilled on the back edge: 70 mm, UNMEASURED, A CHOICE" is THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE — measured figures solid, unmeasured confessed — transposed from paper to plywood. The card reverse carrying "the dish, the date, the dimension, and one line of what was not measured" is a lotería card back at 94.8 × 338.3. So four of the five artefacts in play all terminate in the same gesture: print the provenance of the number beside the thing. That gesture also has enormous prior art outside the project — Mel Bochner's Measurement series (1969) stencilled dimensions directly onto gallery walls in Letraset and black tape, which is literally "the measurement written on the object"; Hanne Darboven, On Kawara and Douglas Huebler ran the same confessional-documentation apparatus; Forensic Architecture made provenance-of-evidence a visual language. In commercial design the spec-sheet-as-identity is now the default for craft food, coffee and skincare. The project has convinced itself that stating a measurement's ceiling is a distinctive voice. It is a fifty-seven-year-old conceptual-art convention and a current design cliché, and using it four times in one program is a house style, not four ideas.

ATTACK 7 — IT DOES NOT ENGAGE THE MEXICAN TRADITION AT ALL, AND ITS MATERIAL SPEC IS THE GENERIC IT FEARS.
12 mm birch ply, matched cream, a brass strip, 350 gsm uncoated, one ink on the reverse. That is Brooklyn/Scandinavian restaurant-minimal, 2014-2024, and it is the exact palette of every competent food brand the risk section warns about. The concept locates its generic-ness risk in STYLING (tweezers, spritz bottle) and misses that it is already three-quarters realised in the FABRICATION SPEC. Meanwhile: rotulista enamel signwriting, papel picado, ex-voto, lotería, Puebla tin-glaze, the fonda ventana, Nacho López's and Graciela Iturbide's street vocabulary — the whole tradition the program says it is drawing on — is untouched. The vehicle's own measured colours (menu-strip yellow 172,144,17; dark red 72,46,6) are quoted in the project brief and not used. A concept about a Mexican taqueria that arrives in birch and brass has chosen the international vernacular by default.

ATTACK 8 — "NEVER PHOTOGRAPH THIS BRAND THROUGH ANYTHING ELSE" IS AN OPS POLICY, NOT AN IDEA, AND LARGE FOOD BRANDS ALREADY RUN IT.
Fixed product-photography jigs — a rig, a constant frame, a locked camera, a shot bible — are standard practice at McDonald's, Pret, Shake Shack, Sweetgreen and every QSR with a brand book. The novelty being claimed is that the jig is derived from the product's own geometry. That is a nice provenance and a weak differentiator, because a jig is invisible in its output by construction. You are proposing to make the invisible part of the pipeline the concept.

ATTACK 9 — THE CARD'S DERIVATION IS UNSEEABLE.
94.8 × 338.3 mm is 1:3.57 — a tall narrow menu strip, which is what half the taquerías on earth already print. That its dimensions descend from CARD_W/CARD_H at t1_detail.py:3622, and that three hang on the real bus, is a lovely fact that no holder of the card can ever recover. Provenance you cannot perceive is not originality; it is comfort for the designer.

WHAT IS ACTUALLY FRESH, STATED FAIRLY, BECAUSE IT IS NOT NOTHING:
(a) The register shift is real. Of the 78 bench titles, almost all are print, flat or animation; this is the only one that puts food, hands, a live service and other people's labour in front of the camera, and the program's owner said "we need to finally present something."
(b) The anti-styling constitution — shot during service not after, the hand in frame is the hand that made it, nothing placed, nothing wiped, and if the first set comes back styled the concept is KILLED rather than fixed — is a written, enforceable, falsifiable editorial rule, and rules of that kind are rare in food branding even though the unstyled-food movement (Bourdain, Ugly Delicious, the 2010s backlash against the retouched hero plate) has been running for a decade.
(c) The seven-day, one-sheet-of-cardboard falsification test is the best first step in the shortlist and does not depend on the model at all.
None of those three is the concept's stated core. The stated core — the hole as constant, the rake as differentiator — is the derivative part.

ON DANGER: the risk section is entirely operational (styling, kitchen cooperation, a dimension the photos cannot check). There is no CONCEPTUAL risk anywhere in it, because the configuration is known-good — hatch, hand, plate, fixed camera. A fresh idea carries the risk of being WRONG. This one can only be executed badly; it cannot be wrong. That is the tell.
* WOULD FIX IT: Six concrete changes. The first three are the ones that decide whether it survives.

1. BUILD THE SIDE NO PHOTOGRAPH HAS EVER SEEN. SPEC.md grades the OFF-SIDE elevation "E (never photographed)" — an entire flank of this vehicle that exists only in the mesh. Cut the aperture from THAT side. Then the object is not a replica of something the client already owns and already photographs; it is the only physical instance of a surface that has never had a physical instance. Every prior-art hit in attack 2 and 3 dies instantly, because nobody has ever shot through this hole, including the bus. The stencil on the back edge stops being an epistemic tic and becomes the whole point: THIS SIDE HAS NEVER BEEN PHOTOGRAPHED. That is a sentence no other food brand on earth can print, and it is TRUE and MEASURED.

2. MAKE THE RAKE VISIBLE OR CUT IT. 1.017° is invisible in one frame. Two options, pick one: (a) put the tilt in the APERTURE, not the lip — hang the whole jig off the vehicle's stance so every photograph the brand ever makes is 1.017° off level, in the same direction, forever, and NEVER corrected in post. Invisible in one image; unmistakable and slightly unsettling across a grid of a hundred; and no brand has ever deliberately canted its entire photographic output by a measured degree taken off a real vehicle's suspension. That converts an invisible detail into an accumulating signature. Or (b) drop the rake claim from the pitch entirely and stop calling it the differentiator, because as stated it is a differentiator that requires a caption to exist.

3. PUBLISH IT AS A TYPOLOGY, NOT AS PHOTOGRAPHS. One image through a window is prior art. Three hundred, gridded, one hole, three years, every plate that went through it, no crops, no edits, dated — that is a Becher and it is defensible, and the invariance carries the idea without a word of type. Commit up front to the grid as the artefact and the single frame as a by-product. This also converts risk 1 from fatal to survivable: a styled frame is obvious in a grid of three hundred honest ones.

4. INVERT THE CAMERA FOR AT LEAST HALF THE SET. Every food brand shoots INWARD through the hatch. Shoot OUTWARD — from inside the galley, through the aperture, at the queue, the street, the rain, the kid at counter height. That is the view the vehicle has and nobody publishes it. Combined with fix 1 it is genuinely unoccupied ground.

5. STRIP THE TYPE OFF THE OBJECT AND CHANGE THE MATERIAL LANGUAGE. Delete the stencilled "70 mm, UNMEASURED, A CHOICE" from the plywood — that is the Uncertainty Plate's move and Bochner's, and repeating it a fourth time makes the program legible as one tic. Keep ONE line of it, on the card reverse only. Then re-spec the fabrication out of the birch-and-brass vernacular: the edge lettering hand-painted by a rotulista in signwriter's order (which links it to ROTULISTA, your strongest animation candidate, and makes the two a pair), and the card in the vehicle's own measured menu yellow (172,144,17) and dark red (72,46,6) rather than a neutral uncoated stock. The brass stays — it is measured at 0.186 ± 0.021 and corroborated at 0.191 on a second frame, so it has earned its place.

6. ADD A PRIOR-ART PLATE TO THE SEVEN-DAY TEST. The cardboard test as written asks "are these twelve better than the restaurant's existing twelve?" — the wrong question, because competent is not fresh. Ask instead: shuffle your twelve frames with twelve pulled from Tacombi's own feed and three competitors' hatch shots and hand the stack to a stranger. If they cannot sort yours out of the pile, the concept is dead on originality even if every frame is good. Run that before the router touches ply. It costs nothing and it is the only test that measures the thing I am attacking.

Do 1, 2a and 3 and this goes from 1.5 to a genuine shortlist contender, because "the only physical instance of the side of the vehicle no photograph shows, canted by its own measured stance, accumulating as a typology" has no prior art I can name. Do none of them and it is a nice window.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

**OWNER-AND-BRAND — 1.5/4 — REJECT AS PROPOSED — it is legally the cleanest thing on the bench and operationally the most presumptuous. It asks a working restaurateur to install a plywood prop in his pass, hand over a live service, and accept a permanent rule about how his brand may be photographed — authority he has never granted and that this project has not earned in 76 revisions. Meanwhile the artefact he can actually hand someone is a 95 × 338 mm card printing a confession of what we failed to measure, and the "unique model contribution" that supposedly justifies the whole object (16 mm of tilt) is invisible in every photograph it will ever appear in and is unverified against any frame we hold. Two real virtues keep the score off the floor: it moves the mark off Volkswagen's shape, which is the exact pivot the programme's own IP finding prescribes, and every dimension I could check against live source is honest.**

* ATTACKS: 1. IT ARROGATES BRAND AUTHORITY HE NEVER GAVE, AND THE PROJECT HAS NO STANDING TO TAKE IT. "Never photograph this brand through anything else, so the hole is the constant" is a permanent brand-photography policy for an operating multi-site business (the reference set alone names Nolita and Playa del Carmen). A contractor recreating a vehicle does not get to set that. And it does not survive the second site: one jig either lives in one kitchen — in which case the "constant" is a local prop, not a brand system — or you build several, at which point the constant is a spec sheet and the mystique evaporates. The owner's rulings run the other way on scope every time: "focus on the 3d model", "keep studio — ruling stands" (twice). This is rule 34's shape at the brand level — a requirement inheriting the wrong object.

2. THE DELIVERABILITY CLAIM IS REFUTED BY THIS PROJECT'S OWN RECORD. Its FIRST STEP needs a live service, a phone on a tripod at the pass, staff cooperation, and an enforced no-styling rule. `PHOTOS_WANTED_rev52.md` §7 — "ONE HUBCAP, SQUARE ON AND CLOSE ... the only new ask" — was raised at rev 52 and is still standing now, twenty-four revisions later, for ONE phone photograph of a wheel. `STATE.md` still reads "hubcap badge is SELF-CONSISTENCY ONLY -- CAP_EMBLEM_WFRAC has never been compared to a frame; see PHOTOS_WANTED item 7". A concept that cannot get one hubcap frame in twenty-four revisions is not going to get a scheduled shoot inside a working kitchen in one. The concept names this (risk 4) but prices it as a footnote when it is the binding constraint on the entire artefact.

3. IT SHIPS NOTHING HE CAN SEE, INTO THE REVISION THAT EXISTS BECAUSE OF THAT COMPLAINT. "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry." "we need to finally present something." The COST section answers with "It needs NO RENDER AT ALL: the model's entire contribution is a DXF and six dimensions." What lands on his desk this revision is a cutting file, a shopping list and twelve phone frames somebody else has to take. That is rev 75 again wearing a router bit.

4. THE HANDABLE ARTEFACT IS THE WEAK HALF, AND IT LEAKS THE PROJECT'S INTERNAL EPISTEMICS ONTO A CUSTOMER-FACING OBJECT. The jig stays in the kitchen; the only thing he can hand anyone is a card whose reverse carries "the dish, the date, the dimension, and one line of what was not measured." Reporting a measurement with its ceiling is a method rule for US. On a card in a guest's hand it is a supplier confessing his own uncertainty to the client's customers. On NO LEGIBLE or THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE the confession IS the piece and it earns its place; bolted to the back of a food photograph it is a non-sequitur. Same objection to stencilling "70 mm, UNMEASURED, A CHOICE" on a permanent object that lives in his restaurant.

5. THE FIGURE PRINTED ON THE DELIVERABLE IS DERIVED FROM A RETIRED CONSTANT. "94.8 × 338.3 mm — the vehicle's own measured pillar-card size" traces to `t1_detail.py`: `CARD_W = 20.0/107 * 0.507 = 0.0948`. That 0.507 is bay 1's width from SPEC §1.1's taper, which `STATE.md` retires in terms: "SPEC §1.1's taper (0.507 / 0.516 / 0.526) is RETIRED — it was the 100 mm origin error of rev 13, not a real taper. The bays are EQUAL at 0.5155 m." Re-derived on the live width the card is 96.4 mm, not 94.8. Worse, the same comment's read is "cols 432.0 - 452.0 (+-0.5)" — a ±0.5 px read on a 20 px feature is ±2.5 %, so 94.8 carries ~±2.4 mm before the retired-taper bias. This is the forbidden-figure class exactly: a number leaning on a retired constant, quoted to 0.1 mm, and this time it is cut into a physical product run rather than sitting in a paragraph where it can be retracted.

6. THE TILT — ITS STATED REASON TO EXIST — IS AN ARTEFACT OF THE MODEL'S AUTHORING FRAME, NOT A MEASUREMENT OF THE REAL COUNTER. `t1_detail.py`'s counter block says it plainly: "FRAME: UN-DROPPED -- these build in build.py step 6, before step 8b subtracts the rake shear from every vertex." The counter is authored at constant z and tilts ONLY because the whole body is sheared. Whether the real welded counter was set level to the WORLD (which is what a fabricator does, so plates do not slide) or level to the VEHICLE is established by nothing in the reference set. So "That one detail is what separates this from a nice window" rests on an inference the record cannot check — and if it is wrong, the object is a permanently, confidently wrong artefact standing in his restaurant, which is the precise failure mode he has rejected nine times on the emblem. Separately: 16.0 mm over 900 mm is 1.02°. No photograph will ever show it. The one thing only the model could give is invisible in the output the model exists to serve.

7. IT DELETES THE BUS AND LEAVES AN UNPROTECTABLE RECTANGLE. Tacombi's brand asset IS the combi — it is the company's name. This concept reduces it to a cream rounded rectangle and then declares that rectangle the permanent frame for everything. TRES HUECOS at least keeps THREE holes in a measured rhythm, which is a mark; one hole is a serving hatch, and every ghost kitchen, hawker stall and diner pass on earth has one. You cannot own it, cannot register it, cannot stop a competitor shooting through a window. The concept's own risk 1 fears generic food photography and proposes a shooting rule as the defence — but the genericism is not in the styling, it is in the frame.

8. NOBODY HAS COSTED THE PROP AS A THING IN A COMMERCIAL KITCHEN. Bare 12 mm birch ply and an unlacquered brass strip, held in the pass, over a griddle, with hands and plates crossing it during service. No food-safe sealed finish, no cleanability, no wipe-down, no mention of it being an obstruction in a service line or of a health inspector's view of untreated ply at the pass. A restaurateur rejects this in five seconds and is right to. The bench already learned this lesson one concept over: "Unlacquered brass and oiled ply will pit and delaminate in a commercial dishwasher. Hand-wash-only is a real imposition on a working taqueria at service speed, and he may refuse on those grounds and be right."

9. IT IS THE THIRTEENTH COPY OF ONE RECTANGLE AND ITS NEAREST NEIGHBOURS ARE IN THE SAME POOL. `CONCEPT_BENCH_rev77.md`: "Thirteen concepts are the 515.5 x 403 rounded rectangle in a different material", listing this one first as "photographic-2 (1:1 jig)" alongside "environmental-1 (1:1 hatch)". CINCO SEGUNDOS' own first step already proposes "the 1:1 cut aperture card". The concept declares adjacency to THE PASS, LA VENTANA, APERTURA, BODEGÓN, COUNTER HEIGHT, THE BRASS LINE and TRES HUECOS — seven — and answers the charge by saying "this is the hole, built". That is a materials distinction, not a concept distinction. A client shown this cannot tell it from its neighbours.

10. WHAT IS GENUINELY GOOD, AND I WILL NOT PRETEND OTHERWISE. (a) It is on the right side of the one legal problem in the whole programme. The bench's own top IP finding says "MOVE THE MARK OFF THE VEHICLE ... the three serving apertures are Tacombi's own cut, not Volkswagen's pressing ... they are legally clean." This concept does that natively — no roundel, no T1 silhouette, no trade dress. (b) SPEC 10.10 really is not engaged on the JIG: 10.10 is about reproducing painted elements from photographs, and an unpainted cream panel omits rather than invents. (c) F191/F193 untouched — correct. (d) Every figure I checked reproduces off live source: `BAY_W 0.5155`, `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550`, `CNT_ZT, CNT_ZB = 1.2540, 1.1470`, `CNT_Y_IN, CNT_Y_OUT = 0.8450, 1.1660`, `CNT_NOSE_F = 0.1860`, `CNT_X0 = 0.9180`. (e) It correctly derived height above ground as z − rake_drop and validated against STATE's 71.0 / 28.4 / 47.9, which is the trap the bench critic caught other concepts falling into ("the counter at '1254 mm above ground' is the authored un-dropped constant the source itself warns about"). (f) The sub-$50 cardboard falsification test is the best first step in the pool. None of that rescues the artefact; it does mean the reasoning under it is sound and reusable.
* WOULD FIX IT: FIVE CHANGES, IN ORDER. Do 1 and 2 or kill it.

1. DEMOTE IT FROM BRAND POLICY TO A TOOL, AND SAY SO ON ITS FACE. Strike "never photograph this brand through anything else" entirely. Pitch it as what it is: a camera jig the kitchen can use when it wants, one of several frames, owned by the restaurant not the programme. The instant it stops dictating and starts serving, objections 1 and 7 both collapse and the piece survives contact with a second site. Rename it so the title is not a dimension a customer cannot parse.

2. MOVE THE HANDABLE ARTEFACT OFF THE CARD AND ONTO SOMETHING HE WOULD BE PROUD TO GIVE AWAY, AND STOP CONFESSING AT THE CUSTOMER. The reverse gets the dish and the date. That is all. Move "one line of what was not measured" and the 70 mm reveal stencil to the BACK OF THE JIG, where the only readers are the people who built it — the confession stays intact and load-bearing, it just stops being addressed to a guest. Then reconsider the card format entirely: 95 × 338 mm is a strip nobody has a use for, and it is the one dimension in the concept that is contaminated.

3. FIX OR DROP 94.8. Either re-derive on the live bay width — 20.0/107 × 0.5155 = 96.4 mm — and print it with the read error it actually carries (±0.5 px on a 20 px feature is ±2.4 mm, so "96 mm" not "94.8 mm"), or drop the pillar-card provenance and pick a card size for the card's own reasons. Do not print a figure descended from the retired 0.507 taper onto a physical run. Write the retraction into the concept's FIGURES block in the same edit.

4. DEMOTE THE TILT FROM JUSTIFICATION TO DECLARED CHOICE, AND SAY WHICH WAY YOU BET. Add to the FIGURES block, verbatim: the counter is authored at constant z in the UN-DROPPED frame and is sheared by the global rake at build.py step 8b; whether the real counter was levelled to the world or to the vehicle IS NOT RECOVERABLE FROM WHAT WE HOLD. Then decide and label it — I would build the prop LEVEL and stencil "counter tilt 16.0 mm over 900 mm, MODEL FRAME, NOT VERIFIED, OMITTED" — because a 1° lean nobody can see is not worth a permanent object that might be wrong. If you keep the tilt, it stops being the reason the concept exists and becomes a footnote, and you need a different answer to "why did I pay for a 3D model." The honest one is the 1:1 cut path with a true radius, which no photograph can give.

5. MAKE IT SURVIVE A KITCHEN AND A SHOOT DAY BEFORE IT IS BUILT. Sealed food-safe finish spec (two-pack polyurethane or equivalent), lacquered brass, radiused edges, wipe-down procedure, and a stated footprint so it is not an obstruction in the pass — or state plainly that it is used at a station OUTSIDE the service line. Then get the shoot access agreed IN WRITING before any material is bought, because that is the real long lead item, not the ply. And run the cardboard test at a time the kitchen chooses, not during peak.

WHAT I WOULD ACTUALLY DO WITH THE HOUR: run the concept's own first step exactly as written — cardboard, 515.5 × 403.0, r 55, twelve frames — but bring him the twelve frames beside twelve of his existing photographs and ONE question, multiple choice, with the crops attached: does the hole make the picture better, worse, or no different? That is a cheap, honest ask he can answer from a phone, it costs nobody a service, and it settles the whole concept for the price of a sheet of card. If he says "no different," this dies for £3 and the aperture rectangle stops eating thirteen slots on the bench.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**DANGER — 1/4 — Competent, cheap, buildable — and de-risked to the point of inertness. The genuinely dangerous content of this concept exists in exactly one place: the STATED RISK section, item 3, "scarred hands, stained cloths, a black griddle." Nothing of that survives into the artefact. The artefact's own shot list is "a plate, a forearm, a jug blender mid-pour, the tray crossing the brass" — that is the Kinfolk feed with the tweezers removed. The warning label kept the content the object threw away, which is precisely the failure this round was convened to correct. Worse, the concept is blind to the one genuinely disturbing thing it has actually built, which falls straight out of its own measured figures: at Z_SILL 1372 / Z_HEAD 1775 un-dropped, over a galley floor whose top sits ~540 mm above ground, this aperture frames a band roughly 767–1170 mm above the floor the cook stands on — hip to mid-chest. (Ceiling: van_floor's z is AUTHORED, t1_detail.py says so in the comment above it; the band is an inference, but the conclusion survives any plausible floor height.) The jig cannot photograph a face. So "never photograph this brand through anything else" resolves to: this brand will never again publish the face of a person who works for it. That is the sharp, political, brand-risky idea sitting inside the piece — derived entirely from the model — and the document does not notice it said it. dangerIntact: false.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE DANGER IS QUARANTINED IN THE RISK SECTION. Risk 3 names the real kitchen — scarred hands, stained cloths, a black griddle, "the restaurant may not want that photographed." That content appears nowhere in ARTEFACT, nowhere in the shot list, nowhere on the card. The concept has written its own warning label and then shipped the safe object. This is the exact "tended herb in a chipped tin" failure at one remove: instead of sanding the risky image, it kept the risky image only in the paragraph that says the image is risky.

2. EVERY RULE IS A PROHIBITION. "Shot during service, not after; the hand in frame is the hand that made it; nothing placed for camera; nothing wiped." Three of four are negative. Prohibition buys plainness, not stakes. Removing the spritz bottle does not make a photograph uncomfortable — it makes it unglamorous. There is no rule anywhere requiring that anything difficult be SHOWN. An anti-styling rule with no positive obligation produces competent documentary food photography, which is the outcome the concept itself says it would rather die than become.

3. THE CARD REVERSE PRINTS THE MEASUREMENT AND NOT THE PERSON. "The dish, the date, the dimension, and one line of what was not measured." The corner radius gets 0.1 mm of precision. The cook gets no name, no station, no hours. A merchandise programme that publishes the aperture's brass fraction to three figures and the labour behind it to zero is making a statement — just not the one it intends. The reverse of that card is where the whole concept's politics are decided and it spent all four lines on the object.

4. IT IS BLIND TO ITS OWN STRUCTURAL VIOLENCE. The 403 mm aperture, at that sill height over that floor, decapitates every worker in every frame for the life of the jig. Torsos and hands, forever, by measurement. That is the most uncomfortable, most defensible, most model-derived idea in the submission and it is nowhere in the text. It is not even listed as a limitation. A concept that proposes a permanent frame and never computes what the frame excludes has not looked through its own instrument.

5. "THE HOLE IS THE CONSTANT AND THE FOOD AND THE HANDS ARE THE VARIABLE." This is the best sentence in the document and it is used as a method note. Read literally it is a statement about churn in food service: the engineered aperture outlives everyone who passes through it, and the people are the interchangeable term. That is mournful and true and it is the subject the piece should be about. It is instead a caption about camera discipline.

6. THE COST SECTION IS AN ARGUMENT FOR INERTNESS, AND IT IS PROUD. "No artwork is touched, so SPEC 10.10 is not engaged; no emblem appears, so F191 is not engaged." "Under $400." "It needs NO RENDER AT ALL." "No collision with F191 or F193." "A jig that keeps working for years... compounding return." Every clause reassures. The concept's stated advantage is that it cannot break anything and costs nothing to be wrong about. Nothing is staked; therefore nothing can be lost; therefore nothing is risked.

7. IT WILL NOT ADMIT IT IS BUILDING A COUNTERFEIT. The real aperture exists, on the real bus, metres away. This builds a plywood replica and declares the replica the only legitimate frame — a fake more faithful than any photograph, faithful in a way (the 16 mm tilt) that no photograph could have revealed. That is queasy, funny and genuinely destabilising: the authentic frame for this brand is a fabrication. The concept presents it as a craftsmanship brag. The knife is in its hand and it is using the handle.

8. THE GAZE RUNS ONE WAY AND NOBODY DECIDED IT. Camera outside, looking in at labour. A service hatch is architecturally kin to a teller screen, a visiting-room window, a confessional grille. Nothing ever shoots back out at the customer. That is the political choice in the piece and it was made silently, by default, because it is the easier shot.

9. THE PEOPLE ARE FILED UNDER LOGISTICS. Risk 4: "an operational dependency: it needs a working service and the cooperation of the people in it." Their cooperation is the artefact's material, not a dependency. There is no consent structure, no credit, no share of the prints, no answer to risk 3's own concession that the restaurant may not want this photographed. The concept identifies the ethical live wire and then routes around it.

10. ALL DANGER IS DEFERRED TO AN UNWRITTEN SHOT BRIEF. Credit where due: "if the first set comes back styled... it should be killed rather than fixed" is a real kill condition and the strongest sentence in the risk section. But it concedes the point. The object as specified is a painted board with a hole. Plywood is not dangerous. Every gram of edge this concept has is contingent on a future photographer's discipline that no part of the artefact enforces.
* WOULD FIX IT: Put the risk section's content into the object. Concretely:

A. REWRITE THE SHOT LIST TO WHAT RISK 3 ALREADY NAMES. Delete "a forearm, a jug blender mid-pour." Specify what must be in frame: a burn scar, a taped finger, a cloth the colour it actually is at 23:00, a forearm at the end of a shift rather than the start. Mandate a matched pair on every service day — one frame at 11:00 and one at 23:00 from the identical camera position — and print them adjacent. Same hole, same lens, same brass; twelve hours of a person on it. That is a positive rule, and it is the only kind that produces stakes.

B. STENCIL THE FRAME'S BLINDNESS ON THE FRAME. Alongside the 70 mm reveal confession, stencil the measured band: "THIS APERTURE SHOWS 767–1170 mm ABOVE THE FLOOR. NO FACE PASSES THROUGH IT." Then break the rule exactly once per series: one card, shot from INSIDE looking out, carrying the cook's face and the customer's. A rule is only felt when it is broken once on purpose. This costs nothing and converts the concept's biggest blind spot into its subject.

C. GIVE THE PERSON THE LINE THE MEASUREMENT GETS. On the card reverse, in the same type size as the dimension: name, station, hours worked that day. If the brand refuses to print that beside a brass fraction quoted to 18.6 %, that refusal is the finding, and it is worth more than the shoot. Get written consent, credit them, and give them the prints — the cooperation is the material, not a dependency.

D. CLAIM THE COUNTERFEIT OUT LOUD. Second stencil, back edge: "REPLICA. THE ORIGINAL IS ON THE VEHICLE. THIS ONE IS TRUER." Let the object state that the brand's most honest frame is a fake — a fake whose 16 mm of tilt no photograph could have taught anyone. The rake stops being a craftsmanship brag and becomes the point.

E. MAKE "THE HANDS ARE THE VARIABLE" A DELIVERABLE. Run the jig for twelve months. Twelve cards, same 515.5 × 403 hole, a different pair of hands on each. On the thirteenth, list everyone who passed through it and how long they lasted. That is the compounding return, and it stops being a business case and starts being about something.

F. STRIKE THE INERTNESS BRAGS. "Touches no artwork," "engages no finding," "under $400," "compounding return" — these are reasons it is safe to approve, not reasons it is worth making. Replace them with the one true cost: if this becomes the only frame, the brand gives up faces, gives up new locations that do not fit 515.5 × 403, and gives up the ability to flatter its own food. Say that plainly and let it be chosen.

With A–E the score is a 3. Without them it is a well-machined window, and the previous round's disease with better tolerances.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**

### SILBATO 1:20 — audit mean 1.62/4

**FEASIBILITY — 1/4 — FEASIBILITY: REFUSE AS SPECIFIED. The object is buildable in principle, but four of its central claims are refuted by arithmetic I ran this session, its manufacturing route is not the one named, and its "two days, then it is in his hand" first step is not available to this project at all. The half-day STL export is real and worth doing; everything downstream of it is understated by roughly an order of magnitude in time, money and capability. Score 1.**

* ATTACKS: WHAT I VERIFIED, ON THE MACHINE, THIS SESSION (all watched print):
  T1_body volume 0.063157 m3, area 47.214 m2, non-manifold edges 0, bbox 4.0000 x 1.7497 x 1.6256 m
  229 mesh objects, 223 of them closed-manifold, 6 not
  bpy.ops.wm.stl_export EXISTS in this Blender (4.5.3) -- so does export_mesh.stl and wm.obj_export
  hole 25.775 x 20.150 mm, r 2.750, area 512.9 mm2, three = 1538.6 mm2  <- the concept's arithmetic REPRODUCES
  BAY_CX (0.6720, 0.0470, -0.5980) -> hole pitch 31.25 / 32.25 mm, c1->c3 span 63.50 mm
  45.3 cents for 343.4 -> 352.5 m/s  <- the concept's temperature figure REPRODUCES

1. THE INSTRUMENT HAS NO VOICING, AND THE MESH CANNOT SUPPLY ONE. Nowhere does the concept say where you blow. An ocarina is a Helmholtz cavity PLUS a fipple: a windway of order 0.8-1.0 mm gap, a labium, a stated cut-up. None of that is on this vehicle, none of it can be surveyed off the mesh, and all of it is precision geometry that decides whether the object speaks at all. Worse for the concept's own logic: with all three apertures covered, the ONLY remaining port is the voicing window -- so "cover all three and it sings low" is a note set entirely by an invented, unmeasured feature, not by the vehicle. The purity claim ("straight off the mesh, not off a die-cast maker's eye") breaks at the one feature that makes it an instrument.

2. "ONE-PIECE MOULDED" IS NOT MANUFACTURABLE FOR A CLOSED CAVITY. A hollow sealed body cannot be injection moulded in one piece -- the core has to come out. Real whistles and ocarinas are two shells ultrasonically welded, or blow-moulded. So the process named on the packet is wrong before tooling is quoted, and the correct process (two cavities + a weld fixture + an airtight seam spec) is a bigger tool and an extra operation than the concept costs.

3. THE FINGERING IS REFUTED BY THE VEHICLE'S OWN MEASUREMENTS. Three holes of 25.775 x 20.150 mm at a mean 31.75 mm pitch, 63.50 mm from first centre to third, ALL ON ONE FLANK. (I am not leaning on the 31.25/32.25 difference -- that is the forbidden pillar asymmetry and it sits inside the measurement.) A 26 x 20 mm rectangular opening cannot be sealed by a child's fingertip pad -- order 10-14 mm, an estimate, not a measurement -- so each hole needs a finger laid flat lengthwise or the flat of the hand; and three fingers laid flat at 31.75 mm pitch cannot be lifted one at a time. "Lift a finger at a time and you get three pitches" is the concept's whole conceptual payload and it is the part hand geometry kills, for the stated user, before any acoustics.

4. THE ACOUSTIC MODEL IS PAST ITS VALIDITY LIMIT, NOT MERELY UNTESTED. Transverse cavity cross-section at 1:20 is about 7112 mm2; three ports at 1538.6 mm2 are 21.6 % OF IT. Ocarina fingerholes run 1-8 %. Above roughly 10-15 % the lumped model degrades and the cavity behaves as a leaky duct: Q collapses and the note goes breathy, unstable or absent. Nominal frequencies IF it worked: 430-630 Hz across plausible cavity volumes and 1.5-3.0 mm walls -- musically fine. But the concept prints "whether it speaks is UNMEASURED" as an even bet; the port ratio says the null is the LIKELY outcome, and it is computable today without printing anything. Also worth stating: three open vs one open is 9.5 semitones. That is three widely separated pitches, not a scale.

5. THE WALL THICKNESS CANNOT BE INHERITED FROM THE SURVEY. 0.063157 m3 over 47.214 m2 is a 2.68 mm shell at full scale -- 0.134 mm at 1:20. Unprintable on FDM (0.4 mm nozzle), marginal on resin. So the whistle's wall is a designed 1.5-2.5 mm, 11-19x the measured value, and the "surveyed rather than remembered" claim survives only for the OUTER surface. At 2.0 mm that is 236 cm3 of polypropylene, 214 g. Two hundred grams of PP is a heavy, material-expensive object to hand out free.

6. THE MESH IS A COLANDER, NOT A CAVITY, AND CLOSING IT IS DAYS NOT HOURS. The good news first: non-manifold edges 0, so T1_body IS a closed 2-manifold surface, and 223 of 229 objects are closed-manifold. But STATE's own rows say roof aperture OPEN, shut lines door+1 / door-1 / cargo / englid 100 % OPEN, plus every glazing aperture, both wheel houses and the windscreen cut through. There is no enclosed volume anywhere in this model. To get one you must plate over every opening except the three apertures and the windway, then union interpenetrating parts across 366,490 faces with 6 non-manifold objects in the mix. Blender booleans at that scale are a known fight. And every plate you add is a DESIGN DECISION absent from the measurement -- the concept's premise erodes once more with each one.

7. THE STL EXPORT IS NOT THE GATE, AND CALLING IT THE GATE HIDES THE REAL COST. Export is genuinely half a day; I confirmed the operator exists. The gate is item 6.

8. THE PROTOTYPE PATH CANNOT ANSWER ITS OWN QUESTION, AND CANNOT BE GIVEN TO CHILDREN. Two halves glued: a leaky seam produces a false negative on the ONE thing the print exists to test. FDM layer ridges in a 0.9 mm windway and a rounded labium produce a second false negative on voicing -- an FDM part is a poor predictor of a moulded one at exactly the feature that decides success. And the "50-piece printed trial in one restaurant" is not available: FDM and resin parts are not food-contact certified, layer lines are porous, uncured resin monomer is a cytotoxicity issue. You cannot put printed parts in fifty children's mouths. So the concept's stated cheap de-risking path does not exist, and the economics collapse to the tooled path immediately -- which is the opposite of what it claims.

9. THIS PROJECT HAS NO FABRICATION CAPABILITY WHATSOEVER. Four cores, a Linux container, no printer, no bureau account, no shipping, no hands, no glue. "TWO DAYS, THEN IT IS IN HIS HAND" is not achievable by anyone in this loop: a resin bureau is 5-10 days plus post, and the AI cannot glue two halves or blow into anything. Every other concept in the program ships as a file. This one is the only one that needs atoms, and the project has never moved an atom in 77 revisions.

10. THE COSTS ARE UNDERSTATED 2-4x. Tooling for a 203 mm two-shell part: order 8-15k USD for an aluminium bridge tool, 15-40k for production steel, plus a weld fixture -- not 4,000-9,000. Unit at 214 g: material ~0.35 + press time on a 150-250 t machine ~1.00-1.30 + weld and assembly ~0.30, so ~2.00-3.50 at 2,000 pieces, not 0.60-1.50. Third-party testing on a MOUTH-ACTUATED children's article (F963 mechanical incl. mouth-actuated toy clauses, small parts, sharp edges at the labium, CPSIA lead and phthalates, CPC): order 1,500-4,000 per SKU, not 500-1,500. These are estimates and I label them so. The honest headline: no child touches this for under ~15,000 USD and several months.

11. VOLKSWAGEN. The concept's entire selling point is that it is the most recognisable-as-a-VW-T1 object ever made, given away by a commercial restaurant. VW licenses the T1 body shape to every die-cast maker on the market. Moulding "EMBLEM OMITTED, NOT LICENSED BY VOLKSWAGEN" into the part is an admission, not a defence, and arguably aggravating. This is the one risk the concept's own risk list does not name, and at tooling scale it is the one that turns a 15k tool into scrap. Contrast the lotería deck, the poster and the deli paper: 2D depictions of one customer's own vehicle, no tooling, no mouth, no minimum order.

12. OPPORTUNITY COST, NAMED. Rev 77 shipped line_pass.py, sheet.py and two finished artefacts, and the owner FIRED F18 -- the die-cut sticker -- at rev 77 (F330). The sticker is live work NOW and is a cutter job the line pass feeds directly. This whistle shares zero tooling with the line program, consumes the mesh in a way nothing else does, and asks an impatient owner who just said "we need to finally present something" to wait months and spend five figures. It is the only concept on the bench that cannot be shipped as a file.

WHAT SURVIVES, AND IT IS NOT NOTHING: the STL export is cheap, real and reusable by any future 3D concept; the body being closed-manifold is a genuine head start; the aperture arithmetic and the 45.3-cent temperature figure both reproduce exactly; and printing "whether it speaks is unmeasured" on the face of the object is precisely this project's method applied to a physical artefact. The idea is good. The build plan is not.
* WOULD FIX IT: CONCRETE, IN ORDER, AND THE FIRST TWO COST NOTHING:

1. RUN THE ACOUSTICS BEFORE THE GEOMETRY -- IT IS FREE AND IT IS TODAY. The port ratio is 21.6 % of the cavity cross-section and that is computable without a printer. Set a design target of <= 8 %, solve for the aperture scale that reaches it, and put THAT number on the underside next to 515.5 x 403.0 / 20 -- the concept already licenses exactly this move ("the apertures get a scale of their own and THAT number goes on the underside"). Expect roughly 1:20 on the body and about 1:34 on the holes. Do this before anyone models anything.

2. TEST THE FINGERING WITH A PIECE OF CARD. Cut three 25.775 x 20.150 mm holes at 31.75 mm pitch in card, hand it to a child, ask them to seal all three and then lift one. Costs minutes. If it fails -- and hole size against a 10-14 mm fingertip says it will -- go to two holes plus a thumb, or shrink the holes per fix 1. Do not model a fingering that hands cannot play.

3. NAME THE VOICING AS AN EXPLICIT NON-VEHICLE ADDITION AND SPEC IT. Windway 0.9 x 6 mm, labium at the tail cap, cut-up stated in millimetres. Then mould a FOURTH line underneath: "VOICING: NOT FROM THE VEHICLE." That converts a hidden break of the concept's own purity rule into a declared one, which is what this project does with every other ceiling. Right now the object would silently lie about its own most important feature.

4. SPLIT THE DELIVERABLE, AND SHIP THE HALF THAT IS ACTUALLY TWO DAYS. Near-term: STL export plus a 1:20 SOLID desk object -- no cavity, no fingering, no mouth, no giveaway. It keeps the entire "first accurately-proportioned VW combi anybody has ever made" claim, which is the concept's strongest and most defensible line, and it drops acoustics, child-safety, tooling and mass-distribution trade dress in one move. ONE object, printed by a bureau, in the owner's hand. That is a real ship. The whistle becomes phase two, gated on fixes 1-3.

5. SAY "TWO SHELLS, ULTRASONICALLY WELDED" WHEREVER THE CONCEPT SAYS "ONE-PIECE MOULDED", AND SPLIT ON THE LONGITUDINAL CENTRE PLANE so the three flank apertures pull straight off one core with no side actions. Re-cost against that: bridge tool 8-15k, steel 15-40k, unit 2.00-3.50 at 2,000, testing 1,500-4,000. If the owner will not commit ~15k, the whistle is a one-off object, not merchandise, and should be pitched as one.

6. KILL THE 50-PIECE PRINTED RESTAURANT TRIAL OUTRIGHT. Replace with: one printed body, labelled ADULT PROTOTYPE -- NOT A TOY, blown by the owner and nobody else. Printed parts do not go in children's mouths, and saying so protects him. Only a lab-tested moulded pilot reaches a child.

7. PROTOTYPE THE SEAL, NOT ONLY THE SHAPE. Print the halves with an interference lip-and-groove joint, or print a single-piece resin body with a sacrificial drain hole you plug afterwards. Otherwise a bad glue line fakes the null result and you will not be able to tell "the design does not speak" from "my seam leaks" -- which is precisely the kind of unfalsifiable experiment this project's rule 3 exists to prevent.

8. GET A LICENSING READ BEFORE ONE DOLLAR OF TOOLING. A VW T1 silhouette given away by a commercial restaurant is the exposure, and the concept's premise -- maximum accuracy, maximum recognisability -- maximises it. Find out first. Discovering it after the tool is cut is the failure mode.

9. BUDGET THE MESH-TO-SOLID WORK HONESTLY IN THE BRIEF: half a day for the STL writer, then 2-4 days closing the roof aperture, all glazing apertures, the wheel houses, the door gaps and the windscreen into a single watertight volume, with each closure recorded as a design decision that is NOT from the survey. Then re-derive the wall as a designed 2.0 mm and state plainly that the measured 2.68 mm scales to 0.134 mm and could never have been used.

DO NOT TRIM THE AMBITION. The riskiest parts -- it may not speak, it is a mouth toy for children, it omits the emblem in moulded type -- are the parts worth keeping. What needs replacing is the build plan, the process claim, the cost sheet and the two-day promise.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1.5/4 — Prior art at maximum density on every axis the concept relies on, and its two headline claims cancel each other. The Mexican silbato/ocarina is the deepest living craft groove available — pre-Columbian through Ocumicho, Metepec, Tlaquepaque, Atzompa, plus the viral "silbato de la muerte" — and figurative ocarinas have used an object's own existing openings as finger holes for a thousand years, so "the three apertures ARE the fingering" is the standard first solution, not a discovery earned by measurement. VW T1 merchandise is the most saturated category in this exercise (die-cast, tin, plush, kettles, cookie jars, ceramic combi and Beetle ocarinas in Mexican and Peruvian markets, combi alcancías); "restaurant hands a child a whistle" is a cliché with its own recall history; "the electric one lost the engine sound" has been worked to death around the ID.Buzz.
