`pass_camera.py` — a new entry in `studio.py`'s camera dict named `pass` (NOT `playa`-anything; that name drags F92 into the room), perspective, eye 1.600 m, standing at arm's reach outboard of the counter's measured outer face `CNT_Y_OUT = 1.1660`, aimed into one bay at a measured centre from `BAY_CX_SPEC`. Render it BLENDER_WORKBENCH wireframe + flat clay at 2400 × 1800 — seconds, not the 5-minute Cycles path, so no tree freeze and no render-queue bookkeeping. Over it, print the measured landmark grid: the 515.5 × 403.0 aperture at r 55 with sill 1.307 / head 1.710 AG, the counter slab 1.2540/1.1470 with `CNT_Y_IN/OUT` depth, the brass strip at `CNT_NOSE_F` × slab = 19.9 mm, and the fringe ball centres struck at `FRINGE_PITCH` 26 mm, `FRINGE_INSET` 13 mm, `FRINGE_R` 9 mm.
PRINT ITS CEILING ON THE SHEET: "trustworthy in the aperture plane only — everything behind it is a construction; Y is chosen everywhere in this model and never measured." Then any illustrator, including a future one, knows which lines to believe. This is reusable across the whole programme and costs half a day.

MAKE THE FIRST STEP CHEAPER THAN A PAINTING. Do not hand him an A4 painting first — that step already assumes the expensive resource. Hand him TWO clay/wireframe crops from the new camera and ask, multiple choice with the reference attached and via the question tool as CLAUDE.md requires: which is the moment — the plate leaving the hatch, or the plate arriving in your hands? Half a day, and it de-risks the only expensive decision in the concept.

SOURCE THE PAINT ROUTE EXPLICITLY AND PRICE BOTH, IN THE CONCEPT, BEFORE ANY WORK:
(a) COMMISSION — US$1,200–4,000, 2–4 weeks calendar, two revision rounds budgeted because the concept itself invites the owner to correct the food.
(b) GENERATIVE + HAND OVERPAINT — ~US$0, 2 days, with INTERACTING HANDS named as the known failure mode and an explicit plan to redraw them over the measured grid.
Pick one and put it in the cost line. "An illustrator" is not a resource this team has.

TAKE THE HANDS-ONLY BRANCH FOR V1. No cook, no face, no consent, no release form, no re-do risk if that employee leaves. The concept says this costs "half the warmth" — pay it for the first object and revisit once he has smiled at something.

CUT THE SECOND SIZE FROM THE FIRST QUOTE ENTIRELY. The charolita is a phase-2 decision with its own plates and its own press tool.

IF AND ONLY IF HE SAYS TIN: get a written quote against an EXISTING STOCK TRAY FOOTPRINT before fixing 380 × 290, and get tooling and MOQ in writing rather than assumed. Report both figures as quoted, with their date — never as a remembered price.

STOP-LOSS, SO THIS CANNOT EAT A REVISION: if the painting route is not chosen and started within one working day of the camera shipping, the concept parks with the `pass_camera` grid banked as reusable infrastructure and NOTHING else spent. The grid is worth having whether or not the tray is ever made — which is the honest test of whether this concept has a cheap core, and it does.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**ORIGINALITY — 1/4 — FAILS THE ORIGINALITY LENS, AND NOT NARROWLY. Every load-bearing layer of PROVECHO is an occupied position that you can buy at retail today: the artefact is a 129-year-old advertising format, the word has its own Etsy market page, the subject is a stock-illustration category, and the composition is already sitting in this project's own reference folder — made by a customer with a phone, no model, no measurement. The one thing that could have made it this project's image is explicitly painted out ("it contributes a wireframe and then disappears"), so the originality claim is invisible in the finished object, which means it is not a claim. It passes the second charge cleanly — this is genuinely NOT the flat-measured-line aesthetic, it is the only real register break on the bench, and I will not pretend otherwise. But breaking out of a house cliché into a world cliché is a lateral move. Score 1: it earns its point for the register break and for being the only concept that puts a human being in a taqueria programme, and loses everything else.**

* ATTACKS: **1. THE ARTEFACT IS THE SINGLE MOST-OCCUPIED OBJECT IN ADVERTISING HISTORY, AND THE CONCEPT NAMES ITS OWN PRIOR ART IN ITS OWN COPY.** "the way a good tin tray from 1965 is over-saturated." You cannot cite the genre as your reference and claim freshness inside it. The formal specification here — painted illustration, rolled rim, brush-script legend, multi-colour offset litho on tinplate — is the Coca-Cola serving tray, issued annually since **1897**, printed "using a special method called lithography… bright colors, sometimes up to 8 or 10" (PROVECHO proposes six), art by Sundblom, Crandell, Hayden and a 1931 Norman Rockwell, most of the red-rim series by American Art Works of Coshocton. The "red rim" series is a named collector category. PROVECHO is a Coke tray with the Gibson Girl swapped for a cook. Below it sits the entire Mexican **charola** tradition — "Vintage Mexican Titan Litho Soda Metal Tray" is a live eBay listing; "Serving Trays / Charolas Para Servir" is a standing retail category at Mexican importers; cerveza charolas (Carta Blanca, Corona, Superior, Victoria, Indio, Bohemia) are a collector market in the thousands of extant designs. This is not adjacent prior art. It is the same object.

**2. THE WORD IS A COMMODITY.** "PROVECHO" is presented as the single considered word on the object. It has its own **Etsy market page** (`etsy.com/market/buen_provecho`), a second one for signs, an Amazon search category, eBay listings, printable-wall-art inventory, and a US food brand literally called **Buen Provecho Foods**. It is on towels, aprons, mugs, wood signs, papel picado and metal art. A one-word Spanish food benediction on a warm object is the default, not a decision. The concept spends its entire typographic budget on the least distinctive string available.

**3. THE COMPOSITION IS THE DEFAULT HERO SHOT OF THE MODERN FOOD ECONOMY — AND, DECISIVELY, IT IS ALREADY IN YOUR REPO.** I opened `ref_nolita_flank.jpg` (450×450, one of the seven distinct target-bus frames). It is a **first-person customer POV with the viewer's own hand entering the frame at the right, holding a bottle of Monopolio, with a plate of food on the table beyond and the bus filling the background.** The exact move PROVECHO claims as its emotional engine — "yours are going up to meet them at the bottom of the frame, close enough that you can see they are yours" — was already made, in this vehicle, at this venue, by a punter with a phone, and it is checksummed into the reference set. Outside the repo it is the composition of a decade of food TikTok, of drive-thru advertising, and of every "the pass" restaurant campaign since ~2015. The concept asserts "the one picture this project has never made." It is the one picture this project already holds.

**4. THE SUBJECT IS STOCK ART.** VW bus + tacos is the most saturated intersection in the souvenir market — the Combi was built in Puebla until 2013 and is a national object. iStock carries a **"taco truck" illustration category with 610+ royalty-free illustrations**; Vecteezy has 263 taco-truck vectors; Etsy runs standing markets for **"taco truck art"** and **"VW bus art"** simultaneously. A warm nostalgic painting of a VW taco bus is a purchasable template. The distinguishing asset — that this is *one specific measured vehicle* — is then deliberately erased.

**5. THE MODEL'S CONTRIBUTION IS INVISIBLE, WHICH MAKES IT NOT A CONTRIBUTION.** "The armature only, and then it is painted over until nothing of it shows." So 76 revisions buy a perspective grid. Ask the falsifying question: hand two illustrators the brief, one with the grid and one with a tape measure and a phone photo taken at the hatch — could the owner tell the trays apart at arm's length? Almost certainly not, and *nobody can check*, because the evidence was painted over. An originality claim you cannot see in the artefact is not an originality claim; it is a production note. This tray could have been commissioned in week one for US$35 and two days of illustration, and that is not a rhetorical flourish — it is the concept's own COST section.

**6. THE PREMISE IS NOT THE CONCEPT'S OWN DISCOVERY. IT IS AN ASSIGNED SLOT, AND THE PROGRAM DOCUMENT ALREADY WROTE IT.** `DESIGN_PROGRAM_rev76.md` finding 4: *"NO FOOD. NO PEOPLE. IN A PROGRAM FOR A TAQUERIA… The brand's actual emotional transaction is being handed hot food through a hole by a person standing there. At minimum one item must contain the hand-off — **the tray crossing the brass line**, the cook's view out five seconds before service. This is an unoccupied market position the program walked past."* And §8.1 lists **"FOOD AND THE HAND-OFF (the tray crossing the counter…)"** as directorial slot #1. PROVECHO's rhetorical centrepiece — "Seventy-six revisions have been spent on a bus with nobody in it" — is a restatement of a finding already carried in the file it is answering, down to the phrase "the tray crossing." Filling a brief competently is worth credit. It is not worth *originality* credit, and the concept presents it as insight.

**7. TWO EVIDENCE DEFECTS, ONE OF THEM LOAD-BEARING.** (a) *"All nine reference photographs"* — a figure nobody watched print. The record names **seven** distinct target-bus frames (`ref_side`, `ref_rear34`, `ref_playa_34`, `ref_nolita_front34`, `ref_nolita_front34b`, `ref_nolita_flank`, `ref_nolita_doorshut`), plus two green-vehicle frames and a school bus, against a floor of 54 reference-class tracked images. Five of those are byte-duplicates under `IMG_*` names — and `HANDOFF_CARRIERS.md` warns explicitly that a duplicate is not corroboration. Rule 5. (b) *"not one of them is taken AT the pass… the model is the only way to stand where the customer stands."* Narrowly survivable — `ref_nolita_flank.jpg` is at the flank, not the hatch — but the sentence as written is refuted by that frame, and it is the sentence the model's entire justification rests on. State it at its true, much smaller strength.

**8. THE ONE CHARGE THAT FAILS, STATED PLAINLY.** This is *not* the old flat-measured-line aesthetic relabelled. Gouache, six colours, over-saturated, no hairlines, no small-caps captions, no confessional tint, no orthographic projection, a human being in it. It fills three of §8.1's named empty slots at once (FOOD AND THE HAND-OFF, SATURATED POLYCHROME, BEAUTIFUL RATHER THAN RIGOROUS) and touches a fourth. Against LOTERÍA / NO LEGIBLE / THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE / AS MEASURED / SECTION AA / CONDITION REPORT, it is the only thing on the bench that is warm. That is real and I am not taking it away. It just is not the same property as being new.

**9. LIKENESS COMPOUNDS THE PROBLEM.** Painting the real cook from `ref_side.jpg` — he is there, white cap, apron, framed in the aperture — moves the piece into "our people" restaurant-campaign territory, the most-produced category in food branding, *and* takes on a consent liability. The concept chooses the option that is both the least original and the most legally exposed.

**10. THE COMMERCIAL CHECK NOBODY RAN.** `shop.tacombi.com` exists and sells merchandise at $12 / $24 / $38. Before a single stroke is painted, someone must establish whether Tacombi already sells serveware. If it does, PROVECHO is not a concept, it is a line extension with a bigger setup bill (US$2,800 on six plates, 500 minimum).

**Sources:** [Coca-Cola red rim serving trays, Journal of Antiques](https://journalofantiques.com/digital-publications/joac-magazine/features/nostalgia-and-beauty-the-red-rim-coca-cola-serving-tray-series/) · [How Coke serving trays were made, 1897–1968](https://www.earlycoke.com/how-coke-serving-trays-were-made) · [Coca-Cola serving trays 1897–1950s](https://www.nostalgia-tymes.com/post/coca-cola-serving-trays-a-look-back-1897-1950s-by-brad-cross) · [Vintage Mexican Titan Litho Soda Metal Tray](https://www.ebay.com/itm/134175496840) · [Charolas Para Servir, TheMexHub](https://ibarraimports.com/collections/serving-trays-charolas-para-servir) · [Etsy "buen provecho" market](https://www.etsy.com/market/buen_provecho) · [Buen Provecho Foods](https://buenprovechofoods.com/) · [iStock taco truck illustrations](https://www.istockphoto.com/illustrations/taco-truck) · [Etsy taco truck art](https://www.etsy.com/market/taco_truck_art) · [Etsy VW bus art](https://www.etsy.com/market/vw_bus_art) · [Tacombi Global E-Store](https://shop.tacombi.com/)
* WOULD FIX IT: **A. KILL THE WORD. It is the cheapest fix and the largest single gain.** "PROVECHO" has a retail category attached to it. Replace it with a string only this counter can produce, and get it from the owner rather than the dictionary — he is a restaurateur who has heard ten thousand of these hand-offs, so ask him, as multiple choice with the crop attached, *what the cook actually says when the plate crosses.* If the answer is "provecho," you have earned the word by measurement instead of defaulting to it, and you can say so. If it is something else, you have a word no competitor can copy. Either outcome beats the current state. Failing that: **no word at all.** A painted tray with nothing written on it is rarer than one with a benediction on the rim.

**B. PUT THE MEASUREMENT ON THE OBJECT — LITHO THE REVERSE.** This is the fix that converts the concept from generic to unrepeatable, and it costs one extra plate pass. Front face: the painting, exactly as described, warm and unashamed. **Back face: the factory backstamp — the perspective construction the painting was built on.** The eye height, the 515.5 × 403.0 aperture with r 55, sill 1307 / head 1710 above ground, counter top 1254, the 107.0 mm slab, the 19.9 mm of brass at 18.6 % of the outer edge measured over 113 columns at 4.52 ± 0.23 px, the fringe at r 9 / 26 mm pitch — and, in the project's own honest register, **the four things that were drawn and never measured: the hands, the plate, the food, and Y-depth everywhere.** Now the tray carries both registers, the program's unique asset is *visible in the artefact* instead of erased behind it, and PROVECHO stops being a Coke tray with a bus on it. Nothing in the charola tradition, the Coca-Cola series or the Etsy inventory does this. It also answers your own stated risk (3) — "nothing on this tray admits anything" — without touching the front face's warmth. The person eating turns the tray over and finds the whole 76 revisions on the back.

**C. BREAK THE FORMAT, NOT JUST THE REGISTER. Die-cut the tray to the vehicle's own side elevation.** Every charola and every Coke tray in the search results is round, oval, rectangular or square. The model can supply the exact silhouette of this specific bus — that is precisely what an orthographic side camera that already *fits* is for, and the side camera is the one that works today. A tray cut to the outline of the vehicle it depicts is a form the prior art does not contain, it is unmistakably this project's, and it needs the model to exist. Combine with B and the object is defensible.

**D. MAKE THE CHAROLITA THE PRIMARY ARTEFACT AND ROUTE IT TO F18.** Right now the child's tray is a size variant in a subordinate clause. §8.1 lists **THE CHILD'S EYE** as an unfilled directorial slot and calls it "his only stated audience"; F18 — the die-cut sticker, *"spark joy… for children"* — is the project's **original deliverable, open since rev 44 and still the oldest live row in the register.** A 240 × 180 tray a child eats off, cut to the bus's outline (C), with the numbers on the back for the parent to turn over, hits the owner's only named audience, the oldest open deliverable, and an empty program slot simultaneously. That is a claim no other concept on the bench can make, and it costs nothing — the plates are already shared.

**E. RETRACT THE TWO FIGURES, IN THE SAME EDIT (rule 13).** Strike "nine reference photographs" — the record names seven distinct target-bus frames, five of the `IMG_*` files are byte-duplicates, and a duplicate is not corroboration. Re-state the model claim at its true strength: *no frame is taken at the serving hatch, so the geometry of looking INTO a measured 515.5 × 403.0 aperture from a standing adult's eye is not in the reference set* — which is true and useful — rather than *"the model is the only way to stand where the customer stands,"* which `ref_nolita_flank.jpg` refutes with a customer's own hand in the frame.

**F. RUN THE PRIOR-ART BENCH BEFORE THE PAINTING, NOT AFTER — this is rule 1 applied to a merchandise category.** Pull twenty cerveza/refresco charolas and twenty Coca-Cola red-rim trays, print the proposed composition at 380 × 290, and lay it in the row. **Look at it.** If a stranger cannot pick yours out of the twenty-one at arm's length, the concept has failed on its own terms and no argument recovers it. The project's whole method is that a green check is not evidence and a crop is; the same test applies to an object whose entire risk is banality. Do this before the two illustrator-days, not after.

**G. RUN THE COMMERCIAL CHECK FIRST, IN ONE HOUR.** Open `shop.tacombi.com/collections/all`. If serveware is already on it, this is a line extension and must be re-argued as one. That check gates everything above.

**H. KEEP THE FIRST STEP EXACTLY AS WRITTEN.** "One A4 painting, handed to him with nothing written on it and no explanation. If he smiles, it is a tray. If he corrects the food, it is a better tray." That is the best sentence in the document and the correct instrument — it is the only proposal in the whole program that puts a picture in front of the owner instead of a measurement, and his correction of the taco is worth more than any of my prior-art objections. Do A, B and E *before* that A4 is painted, though, so what he judges is the fixed concept and not the generic one.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**OWNER-AND-BRAND — 2/4 — OWNER AND BRAND FIT — CONDITIONAL REJECT. He would like the painting and refuse the object as it stands.

WHAT IS GENUINELY STRONG, SAID FIRST SO THE ATTACK IS NOT MISTAKEN FOR A DISMISSAL.
(a) It is the only concept in the round that answers the open critic finding by name. `DESIGN_PROGRAM_rev76.md` §2 finding 4: "NO FOOD. NO PEOPLE. IN A PROGRAM FOR A TAQUERIA … At minimum one item must contain the hand-off — the tray crossing the brass line." F331 records that this finding is "UNTOUCHED by this ruling and stays open." PROVECHO is the tray crossing the brass line. It is on-brief.
(b) It steps around every trademark and emblem landmine in the project. Standing at the pass looking in, there is no VW roundel in the frame. Nine owner rejections of the emblem, F191 and F234 both standing, and this artefact cannot trip any of them. That is not luck, it is the view choice, and it is worth crediting.
(c) SPEC §10.10's hard bar is "Every painted element ON THIS VEHICLE must be REPRODUCED from photographs of the actual combi." §10.10's own in-scope table is livery — mural, paisley, script, Calidad decal, menu strips, menu cards, rear lettering, plate surround. None of that is in this frame. The tray does not breach 10.10 because it does not depict any of the eight elements 10.10 governs. Say this explicitly in the concept; a reader will assume it does breach.
(d) Its figures are real. I checked all of them in source: `emit=(1.000, 0.918, 0.790)` at `t1_detail.py`; `GAL_SEAM_X = -0.6140  # measured back-wall material change`; `FRINGE_R, FRINGE_PITCH, FRINGE_INSET = 0.0090, 0.0260, 0.0130`; `CNT_ZT, CNT_ZB = 1.2540, 1.1470  # 107 mm thick`; the brass `(0.6600, 0.4750, 0.1750)`; and the 18.6 % nosing fraction with its "MEASURED, ref_side.jpg" comment. Nothing is invented and nothing is transcribed wrong. That is rare in this repository.

NOW THE FOUR THINGS THAT WOULD MAKE HIM SAY NO.

1. THE OBJECT DOES NOT CONTAIN HIS BRAND OR HIS BUS. THIS IS THE FATAL ONE.
His single stated conception of merchandise, in his own words, is F155/`RULED-rev62`: "this is just the render to plug into company merch with different backgrounds once i determine the model is done." The one instruction he has ever given about merch is that the merch carries the vehicle. PROVECHO carries neither the vehicle nor the wordmark: the concept states "one word: PROVECHO. That is the only word on the object," and the customer's-eye view at a 515.5 mm hole excludes the flank script (`script_L`/`script_R`), the Calidad decal (`calidad_L`), the mural lid and the roundel — every mark the brand owns is outside the frame by construction. What remains is a hole, a fringe, a brass line, hands, tacos. That is a beautiful generic taqueria tray. The concept's own risk 3 frames this as an aesthetic price ("it can be dismissed as generic taqueria illustration"). It is not aesthetic. It is that the artefact is unattributable, uncopyrightable in any useful way, freely imitable by the taqueria across the street, and it advertises the category rather than the client. A merchandise programme whose warmest object omits the client is dilution by omission.

2. IT IS SERVICE WARE THAT CANNOT BE SERVICE WARE, AND HE IS THE ONE PERSON WHO WILL KNOW IN ONE SECOND.
"A lithographed tin serving tray … he can carry one out to a table the day it arrives." Six-colour offset litho on 0.28 mm tinplate with clear varnish is a decorative and retail object. In a working taqueria it goes through grease, citrus, sanitiser and a dish pit; the varnish chips at the rolled edge, the tinplate rusts at the chip, and it is not a surface any health inspector or any operator will accept in food service. He runs restaurants. Presenting it as a *serving* tray is a domain error in front of the one audience that cannot miss it, and it costs credibility on everything else in the pitch. The proof spec (dye-sub on 1 mm aluminium) is quietly closer to food-safe than the production spec, which means the object he is shown and the object he would buy are different objects — the concept has this backwards.

3. THE CHAROLITA IS OFFERED AS THE CHILDREN'S ITEM AND CONTRADICTS THE ONLY CHILDREN'S ART DIRECTION HE HAS EVER WRITTEN.
F331 (`RULED-rev77`) splits the audience — "Split it deliberately — kids get one line, adults another" — and makes a children's line "of equal standing" owed by every future shortlist. The one written brief for children in 77 revisions is F18's, and it is locked twice over: "SCENE LOCKED — nothing but the bus, die-cut tight, plus the sun and the papel picado," "STYLE LOCKED — cartoon with rendered depth," "I like how the wheels were drawn in the earlier cartoon version." The charolita as specified is a painted gouache image of two adults' hands exchanging a plate, with no bus, no wheels, no sun, no papel picado, in a register that is not the locked one. It is a small adult tray, not a child's object. Offering it as the children's half of a ruled split will read to him as not having read the split.

4. ITS FIRST STEP CANNOT BE EXECUTED BY THIS MACHINE, IN THE REVISION CULTURE THAT ASKS "Why do we keep doing Revs that don't ship geometry."
"The painting itself is 2–3 days of one illustrator." There is no illustrator. Every other shortlist item is producible by the line pass this programme is building; PROVECHO's deliverable is a commission, and the model's role is explicitly "a wireframe and then disappears." Worse, the concept concedes that the part of the view the model uniquely supplies is the part the model cannot vouch for: "the galley's DEPTH — Y is chosen everywhere in this model and never measured, so any view into a bay is a construction." So what 77 revisions actually buy this image is the aperture rectangle, the counter slab and the brass line — the flat frame around the hole — and the illustrator invents everything through it. Handed to him as "here is what the model gave us," that is a thin answer to a man who has paid for weeks of compute.

TWO SMALLER ONES, RECORDED RATHER THAN LEANED ON.
5. LIKENESS. Risk 2 is honestly stated and the fix ("get the consent") is right, but it is understated as an art problem. `ref_side.jpg` shows a named-employer's actual employee — F111 reads "the cook's bare forearm at FULL saturation" in bay 1 and describes the vehicle "in service with a cook working." A recognisable employee on 5,000 trays is an employment and publicity-rights matter that outlives the employee's tenure. It needs a written release before plates are cut, not before the painting.
6. THE FOOD. Risk 1 is correct and its mitigation is correct — but "four tacos and a wedge of lime" on a *plate* is a specific service claim about a specific operator, and the concept has already picked plateware, count and garnish before asking him. Ask before drawing, not after.

ON F92 — the concept's paragraph is right and I checked it. F92's object is the Playa hero as a second DELIVERY FRAME with `playa_env.py`. This is a painted tray at a counter. It touches neither, and it correctly does not act on it.

WOULD HE BE PROUD TO HAND SOMEONE THIS? He would be delighted to hand someone the painting. He would not be proud to hand someone a tin tray that does not say who he is.**

* ATTACKS: A1 (fatal, brand). Carries neither the wordmark nor the vehicle. Contradicts F155, the only merch instruction he has ever given: "this is just the render to plug into company merch." Every brand asset — script_L/script_R, calidad_L, the mural lid, the roundel — is outside a customer's-eye frame at the aperture by construction. Result: a beautiful, unattributable, freely imitable object that advertises the category, not the client.

A2 (severe, domain credibility). Six-colour litho on 0.28 mm varnished tinplate is retail decor, not food service. Grease, citrus, sanitiser and a dish pit chip the varnish and rust the rolled edge. The one man in the room who runs restaurants will spot it instantly. The proof (dye-sub on aluminium) is a different, more serviceable object than the production spec, which is the wrong way round.

A3 (severe, ruling conflict). The charolita is presented as the children's item but breaks F18's SCENE LOCK — "nothing but the bus, die-cut tight, plus the sun and the papel picado" — and its STYLE LOCK ("cartoon with rendered depth"), while F331 now makes a children's line of equal standing owed by every shortlist. It is a small adult tray, not a child's object.

A4 (severe, owner patience). Its first step is an illustrator commission, not a build. The programme has no illustrator, and the model is explicitly reduced to a discarded wireframe. Compounded by the concept's own admission that the galley depth — the part of the view only the model could supply — is unmeasured Y, so the model contributes the flat frame around the hole and nothing through it.

A5 (moderate, legal/ops). A recognisable employee's likeness on a print run that outlives his employment. Needs a signed release before plates are cut. F111 confirms a real cook is in the reference frame.

A6 (moderate, expertise). Plateware, taco count and garnish are chosen before the restaurateur is asked. He will read that as a picture of a taqueria rather than a picture of his taqueria.

A7 (minor, positioning). The customer's hands in frame is fine; the concept's risk 4 overstates it. Not a rejection path.

NOT AN ATTACK, STATED SO IT IS NOT MISREAD: SPEC §10.10 is NOT breached. Its in-scope table is the eight livery elements, none of which appear in this view. And the concept is the round's cleanest on trademark — no VW roundel is visible from the pass, so it cannot trip F191/F234 or a tenth emblem rejection. Its figures also all verify in source.
* WOULD FIX IT: Five concrete changes. The first two are not negotiable for this lens.

1. PUT THE BUS AND THE MARK IN THE FRAME WITHOUT LOSING THE HAND-OFF. Widen the view from "at the pass" to "one pace back and one pace to the right." You keep the hands crossing the counter, the plate, the brass line, the fringe — and you gain the cream flank, the red, the aperture's cream surround, and the hand-painted script running along the flank at the edge of the composition. `script_L`/`script_R` and the flank paisley then reproduce from photographs, so §10.10 is satisfied rather than merely inapplicable, and the object becomes recognisably HIS. Keep PROVECHO on the rim as the only rim word; let the vehicle carry the name, which is what it does in life.

2. CHANGE THE SUBSTRATE, OR CHANGE THE CLAIM. Either (a) build it as the retail object it actually is — powder-coated or dye-sub on 1 mm aluminium with a rolled polymer edge, sold, not served, and call it a tray for keys and limes and loose change, not for food; or (b) keep the litho-tin romance and re-scope it as a WALL PIECE or a lid for a tin, where 0.28 mm varnished tinplate is honest. Do not present tinplate litho to a restaurateur as service ware.

3. MAKE THE IMAGE WITH THE SPINE, NOT AROUND IT. His locked style is "cartoon with rendered depth — vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset." Render the aperture, counter, brass nosing, fringe and galley from the customer's eye through the line pass (F332), flat-fill it, and paint ONLY the hands, plate and food over that. This turns risk 3 from a confessed price into a non-issue, makes the object shippable by this machine instead of by a commission, and lets the tray and the sticker share one pipeline — which is the argument that gets it built.

4. SPLIT THE CHAROLITA OFF AND REDRAW IT TO F18's LOCK. The child's tray is the bus, die-cut-tight framing, the sun, the wheels drawn the way he liked them — the same art as the sticker, on tin. Same plates, same run, different image. That satisfies F331's children's line honestly instead of shrinking an adult image.

5. ASK HIM THE FOOD AND THE HANDS IN ONE QUESTION, BEFORE THE PAINTING, WITH THE REFERENCE ATTACHED. Multiple choice, one crop, the question tool: hand him the `ref_side.jpg` bay-1 crop that F111 reads as "the cook's bare forearm at FULL saturation" beside four options for what crosses the counter — paper-lined basket / plate / paper sheet / hand-to-hand with no vessel — and how many tacos. That crop is also the only hand-scale reference this project holds, against a measured 515.5 mm aperture, so it does double duty as grounding and as the ask. Get the employee's written release in the same conversation, before plates are cut. The concept's instinct to show him a drawing early is right; ask the question one step earlier than it currently does.
* **DANGER INTACT: True**

**DANGER — 1/4 — DANGER LOCATED, THEN SANDED — and the sanding is done in the concept's own voice. The one live wire (two pairs of hands crossing a counter, one of them yours) is a picture of a labour relation, on an object whose function is to be covered by the actual meal and stained by it. PROVECHO as written refuses both: it directs the image as "warm, generous, a little over-saturated," it promises the tray "outlives three refits" (i.e. is never marked), and every single one of its five stated risks resolves to asking permission — get his consent, show him the drawing early, some people find it presumptuous, paid on purpose. Risk 3 is the tell: the concept knows it has no confession in it and writes "that is the price of this territory, paid on purpose." That sentence is the warning label the lens exists to catch. Nothing is at stake in the object; the stakes are all in the paragraph next to it. dangerIntact = false.**

* ATTACKS: 1. THE REAL DANGER IS UNNAMED, AND IT IS NOT AESTHETIC. Two hands come OUT of a hatch and two hands go UP to meet them. That is not "the moment the food crosses" — it is a picture of who cooks and who is served, made for a New York restaurant group selling Mexican street food, in Spanish, in signwriter's brush script, on a 1965 tin-litho tray. The souvenir-tray idiom IS the authenticity-as-merchandise idiom. The concept's stated risk 4 gets within one inch of this and then makes it a question of manners ("some people find that presumptuous"). Presumptuous is the small version. Unexamined danger is worse than sanded danger, because it will surface anyway — in execution, as an accident, with nobody having decided what it means.

2. THE ARTEFACT'S OWN FUNCTION CONTRADICTS THE BRIEF AND THE CONCEPT SIDES WITH THE BRIEF. A tray is the surface you put the plate ON. This tray's picture is a man's hands offering a plate — and the first thing that happens to it, forever, is that a real plate is set down on top of the painted one, and grease and salsa land on his face. That is a genuinely uncomfortable, genuinely beautiful thing the object does BY ITSELF, for free. Instead the concept writes "the kind that outlives three refits" — an explicit promise of permanence — and specifies clear varnish. It has chosen the version where nothing happens to the image. PAPEL DE GRASA on the same shortlist is built entirely on use completing the object; PROVECHO has the same move available in its own material and declines it.

3. EVERY MITIGATION IS PERMISSION-SEEKING. "The mitigation is not measurement, it is showing him the drawing early and letting him correct the food." "I would paint him and get the consent." "If he smiles, it is a tray." Three separate risks, three resolutions that consist of the owner approving in advance. A concept that pre-clears its only sharp edges with the person it might discomfort has already agreed not to have any. And the smile test is worthless as a test: a warm painting of tacos will draw a smile from anyone, which is exactly why it proves nothing about this artefact.

4. THE LIKENESS PROBLEM IS TREATED AS A CHECKBOX AND IT IS THE PIECE'S BEST MATERIAL. A named employee's hands and face on merchandise sold by his employer is not a consent form, it is payment, credit and royalty — and the fact that no other object in this 78-concept programme credits a single human being is the sharpest fact available. "I would paint him and get the consent" converts the one genuinely political element into paperwork.

5. IT RISKS NOTHING ELSEWHERE EITHER. US$35 proof, US$2,800 setup, "no new pipeline, no line pass, no render queue, nothing this project has to build first." Cheapest and safest object on the bench, and the cheapness is presented as a virtue in the COST block. Fine as programme management; it means there is no expense, no exposure and no possibility of public failure to score.

6. THE ONE PROVOCATION IT DOES CARRY IS IMMEDIATELY SOFTENED. "The armature only, and then it is painted over until nothing of it shows" is a real affront to seventy-six revisions and to an owner who just asked why revisions don't ship geometry — the correct use of a 366,490-face measured model turns out to be a hidden underdrawing, and the actual deliverable is a 2–3 day illustration commission by someone else. That is a live, uncomfortable claim about the whole programme. The concept immediately reframes it as service ("the one thing an illustrator cannot guess") and never lets it sting.

7. THE DIRECTION LINE WILL PRODUCE THE GENERIC TRAY IT FEARS. "Warm, generous, a little over-saturated, the way a good tin tray from 1965 is over-saturated" is a mood, not a constraint, and it is exactly the instruction that yields the dismissible object risk 3 predicts. The project holds measured colour — menu yellow (172,144,17), dark red (72,46,6), brass linear (0.6600,0.4750,0.1750), strip-light emission 1.000/0.918/0.790, the seam at x −614 — and the concept lists them under FIGURES then does not bind the palette to them.

8. THE CHAROLITA IS LINE EXTENSION, NOT AN IDEA. "A second size, 240 × 180 mm — the charolita, the child's tray" is the same picture shrunk. A child cannot see over a 1254 mm counter. The child's tray as written contains no child.

9. CREDIBILITY NOTE, OUTSIDE MY LENS BUT IT WEAKENS THE PITCH: the counter figures (top 1254, bottom 1147, 107.0 mm slab) sit beside SPEC's "counter fascia bottom at 1.082" — possibly different objects, possibly not. Someone should watch those print before they go in a FIGURES block, per the project's own rule.
* WOULD FIX IT: Put the danger back into the OBJECT, not the risk paragraph. Six concrete changes, none of which cost more than the version as written:

1. LET THE PICTURE DIE UNDER THE PLATE. Reverse the permanence claim. Specify thin ink and NO varnish across the well — the plate-sized ellipse in the centre — and full varnish only at the rim where PROVECHO is. A tray in daily service then loses the cook's face first, from the middle out, in exactly the shape of the meals served on it. After a year the object is a bright brush-script word around an erased hand-off. That is the vehicle's own subject (a thing that has outlived three refits and shows it) transferred into merchandise, and it is measurable: sell the tray, photograph it at 6 and 12 months, and that record is the piece. Delete "the kind that outlives three refits."

2. PAINT THE LABOUR RELATION INSTEAD OF THE WARMTH. Decide and specify, on the artefact: the cook's hands have plancha burn scars and short nails; the customer's hands hold a phone in one of them. One phone. It costs nothing, it turns a warm picture into a true one, and it makes the tray about 2020s Nolita rather than about 1965 nostalgia the brand did not live through.

3. PAY HIM AND NAME HIM. If the man in ref_side.jpg is painted, his name goes on the reverse in the same brush script, and he is paid a per-unit share. That single line makes this the only object in 78 concepts that credits a person, which is a stake worth having. If he will not be named, do not paint him — hands only, and accept the loss of half the warmth as the price rather than splitting the difference.

4. REPLACE THE MOOD DIRECTION WITH THE MEASURED PALETTE. Six litho colours, named from this project's own measurements: menu yellow (172,144,17), dark red (72,46,6), brass (0.6600,0.4750,0.1750) linear, the strip-light ramp 1.000/0.918/0.790 across the seam at x −614, plus cream and a black keyline. The illustrator works inside those six. The tray is then chromatically THIS vehicle, and "generic taqueria illustration" stops being available as a dismissal.

5. ONE CONFESSION, ON THE UNDERSIDE, WHERE IT COSTS THE IMAGE NOTHING. The concept says "no ceiling, no tolerance, no confession" and calls that the price of the territory. It isn't — the underside is free surface. One line in brush script: the aperture and counter figures as drawn, and the plain sentence that the hands, the plate, the food and the galley's depth are invented. The front stays warm; the object stops being a lie about how it was made; the programme's unique asset is present without contaminating the register.

6. MAKE THE CHAROLITA A CHILD'S EYELINE, NOT A SMALL TRAY. Same counter from ~900 mm off the ground: the underside of the 107 mm slab, the brass nosing seen from below, and two hands appearing over an edge the viewer cannot see past. It is the one view the adult tray cannot have, it is derivable from the model, and it is genuinely about a child at a counter. It also earns F18's "spark joy for children" instead of borrowing it.

Then change the FIRST STEP. Do not hand him a nice painting and watch for a smile. Hand him THAT painting — phone, scars, name on the back, unvarnished centre — and ask one multiple-choice question about the thing that is actually at stake: whose hands are outside the hatch. If he says paint it warmer and take the phone out, you have learned something real, and the concept can lose on its merits rather than pass by being pleasant.
* **DANGER INTACT: False**
